The flagstones kept the place where the stroke stopped as a shallow brightness at the edge of the patch nobody swept; neither man named the other's silence and the loan that can hear was not called; the boy filed it on the flagstone's own place as the fourth of four held changes on four places and would not let them stand up as a rule (T062/T035/T031, T036/T076, T061/T082 advanced, not paid). Three or more men went low into \place{the back room} before first grey on all three mornings and got no name from \person{Jorra Vail}; the burned slate stood at five with a finger between it and the four, the shut-room tellings at two, the row at five askings with no witness (T035/T031, T065/T095, T101 held). No collector came up or went down \place{the clay road} on Bone 7, 8 or 9 (T009/T013/T092). Ch 85.
Through Bone 10–12, Y.H. 813 (Days 309–311) no new place appeared in the district: the month's first Account-day and market fell on the tenth, and the three days used the same \place{the Vail kiln-house}, hearth, \place{the kiln mouth}, \place{the loaf shelf}, \place{the bed}, \place{the boards}, \place{the pot board} and \place{the towel}, \place{the drying racks} with the dressed grain covered under wool at \place{the rack end}, \place{the Clay Yard} with its seven walked-around places, \place{the yard well}, the skinned \place{trough} and \place{the flat stone by the well}, \place{the kindling} and \place{the flagstones}, \place{the Vail wheel-shed}, \lore{the damper} and the finger's-width-open \place{the back room}, \place{the Debt Loft}, \place{the terrace}, \place{the clay road}, \place{the Cross}, \place{the reading block under the moot-post}, \place{the pot board}, \place{the moot-post}'s finger's width of shade, \place{the north bridge} and \place{the out-of-line landing}. There was no market, Cross or bell on Bone 11 or 12. On Bone 10 Berr Clay read Bone's first arithmetic twice over the basket at \place{the reading block}, denied the levy with *your house*, never *I*, and went off up \place{the clay road} toward \place{High Kiln} with the box locked and the true subtraction inside; four arithmetics of the year now stand behind one printed figure, four are four and not one account, and the finding is kept on a place of its own with no payer, not laid across the house's not-doings, the covered grain, the four held changes or the boy's gaps. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay, stood half a pace off through the reading and the bell and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing; Hessa Rale carried the slate up the road toward \place{Greybank} before noon edge-on and unread, and the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called (T015/T041/T047, T058/T079, T080, T090). The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told and not made a seventh (T078); at \place{the pot board} and the market's edge a silent woman's green and a foundling's shape stayed a seventh of each with no eighth (T050/T051, T033/T034 held). The stayed frost had gone off the well's north lip on Bone 3 while the trough kept its skin and the silt-clouded plate stood on its flat stone out of the path of feet, all called weather and entered nowhere with a finger between every pair (T083/T085 held). The returned half-check of Bone 8 stayed on \place{the flagstones} beside the Day 161 damper fraction, the Day 166 shelf beat and the Frost 5 returned beat, four held changes on four places and not a rule (T062/T035/T031 held). Five openings in five months were five and not a rule, the widow's mark untouched at every hour that was not an opening (T058/T079). The burned slate stood at five with a finger between the fifth and the four and went through the reading untouched, no witness, no hand at the flame and no sixth (T035/T031/T065/T095 held); the row stayed at five askings with one shaping and no witness (T101 held), Ilva was offstage with her strip in her sleeve and her ninth hand nine (T080 held), and the dressed grain lay covered and unfigured under a number that did not know what it cost (T077). No collector came up or went down \place{the clay road} on Bone 10, 11 or 12 (T009/T013/T092). Ch 86.

Through Bone 13–15, Y.H. 813 (Days 312–314) no new place appeared in the district: the middle working week used the same \place{the Vail kiln-house}, \place{the hearth}, \place{the pot board} with the twice-mended pot, \place{the loaf shelf}, \place{the bed}, \place{the boards}, \place{the towel}, \place{the drying racks} and the cool stone at \place{the rack end} where the dressed grain lay covered under wool, \place{the Clay Yard} with its seven walked-around places, \place{the yard well}, the skinned \place{trough} and the \place{flat stone by the well}, \place{the kindling} and \place{the flagstones}, \place{the Vail wheel-shed}, \lore{the damper} and the finger's-width-open \place{the back room}, \place{the Debt Loft}, \place{the terrace} and \place{the clay road}. There was no market, Cross or bell on Bone 13, 14 or 15, and Bone 20 (Day 319), the month's second Account-day, stood five, four and one mornings ahead and was not brought early. Sella carried the boy down and up at the half-pace from every open slate and went wide around the clean dark line and its finer grey called the thaw, the space beneath the lowest rack with its broken birth-slates, the gone pan's round ring, the trough, the field's empty place out on \place{the worked strips} and the returning-line place where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out — seven places gone around with a look and a wide step and no number (T009/T013/T092, T098 carried). The stayed frost was gone off the well's north lip while the trough kept its skin and the silt-clouded plate stood out of the path of feet, three coverings called weather and entered nowhere (T083/T085 held). At \place{the yard well} on Bone 14 \person[Ilva]{Ilva} was glimpsed once drawing with the pail against her knee, clay pale in her nail crescents and rack dust in one thumb seam, her hand going toward her sleeve and staying there with the notched strip unread, answering *Ask the pot what it cost. I keep the count, not the account*; on the 13th and 15th she was offstage (T080 advanced, not paid). A Cold Acre hare went toward \place{the north gate road} answered with the weather and a barrow man's cost-question was sent to the pot, leaving no entry (T077). On the morning of Bone 15, under the room's low angle at \place{the loaf shelf}, the boy's hand stayed at the inch toward the river's note without closing the two inches to the crust — a physical fraction on the bed and not a new room, road or landmark (T060/T086, T061/T082 advanced, not paid). Four arithmetics of the year stood locked up \place{the clay road} in \person{Berr Clay}'s box toward \place{High Kiln} with one in twelve printed and unchallenged, the figure read still not the figure paid, no signature on the space and the finding on a place of its own with no payer (T015/T041/T047). No collector came up or went down \place{the clay road} on any of the three days (T009/T013/T092).

Through Bone 16–18, Y.H. 813 (Days 315–317) no new place appeared in the district: the last working week before the month's second reading used the same \place{the Vail kiln-house}, \place{the hearth}, \place{the pot board} with the twice-mended pot and its crack showing, \place{the loaf shelf}, \place{the bed}, \place{the boards}, \place{the towel}, \place{the drying racks} and the cool stone at \place{the rack end} where the dressed grain lay covered under wool, \place{the Clay Yard} with its seven walked-around places, \place{the yard well}, the skinned \place{trough} and \place{the flat stone by the well}, \place{the kindling} and \place{the flagstones}, \place{the Vail wheel-shed}, \lore{the damper} and the finger's-width-open \place{the back room}, \place{the Debt Loft}, \place{the terrace} and \place{the clay road}. There was no market, Cross or bell on Bone 16, 17 or 18, and Bone 20 (Day 319), the month's second Account-day and market, stood two mornings ahead and was not brought early. Sella carried the boy down and up at the half-pace from every open slate and went wide around the clean dark line and its finer grey called the thaw, the space beneath the lowest rack with its broken birth-slates, the gone pan's round ring, the trough, the field's empty place out on \place{the worked strips} and the returning-line place where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out — seven places gone around with a look and a wide step and no number (T009/T013/T092, T098 carried). The stayed frost was gone off the well's north lip while the trough kept its skin and the silt-clouded plate stood on its flat stone out of the path of feet, three coverings called weather and entered nowhere with a finger between every pair (T083/T085 held). At \place{the yard well} on Bone 17 \person[Ilva]{Ilva} was glimpsed once drawing with the pail against her knee, clay pale in her nail crescents and grey rack dust in one thumb seam, her hand going toward her sleeve and staying there with the notched strip unread, answering *Ask the pot what it cost. I keep the count, not the account*; on the 16th and 18th she was offstage (T080 advanced, not paid). The month's hunts and repairs went on as directions judged by the carrying arm and the weather: a man with a cracked jar in a rope sling asked what the mending would want and was sent to the pot, which said nothing; a man shifting a burden from one collarbone to the other said the cost was the weather and went off toward \place{the clay road} with nobody weighing what he carried and nobody opening a slate (T077). The dressed grain was turned beneath the wool at the rack end with a progressively lighter touch and given no figure (T077). At \place{the kindling} Tarn kept six where the house could check and the seventh at the lip beside Garl's unforged collector sentence, the knife set back where it could be checked, the Seed 16 breath unspent and no collector up or down the clay road; at \place{the flagstones} his stroke went over the shallow bright patch without the half-check and the returned half-check of Bone 8 stayed on its own place as the fourth of four held changes and not a rule (T036/T076, T062/T035/T031). At \place{the Vail wheel-shed} Jorra's bent finger stood in the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, the dust and the men unnamed before first grey going unasked about (T035/T031/T062). Nothing of Bone 10 through 15 came into these three days as sign, lesson, mending, cause or payment, and the boy's staying at the inch and his early letting-go of Bone 17 were two objects on two places that nobody under any roof saw (T060/T086, T061/T082). Ch 88.

Through Bone 19–21, Y.H. 813 (Days 318–320) no new place appeared in the district: the month's second reading and the two ordinary tending days used the same \place{the Vail kiln-house}, \place{the hearth}, \place{the pot board} with the twice-mended pot and its crack showing, \place{the loaf shelf}, \place{the bed}, \place{the boards}, \place{the towel}, \place{the drying racks} and the cool stone at \place{the rack end} where the dressed grain lay covered under wool, \place{the Clay Yard} with its seven walked-around places, \place{the yard well} with its stone lip, the skinned \place{trough} and the \place{flat stone by the well}, \place{the kindling} and \place{the flagstones}, \place{the Vail wheel-shed}, \lore{the damper} and the finger's-width-open \place{the back room}, \place{the Debt Loft}, \place{the worked strips}, \place{the terrace} and \place{the clay road}. There was no market, Cross or bell on Bone 19 or 21; Bone 20 (Day 319) was the month's second Account-day and market and brought \place{the Cross}, \place{the reading block under the moot-post} with its finger's width of thin shade, \place{the pot board}, \place{the moot-post}'s finger's width of shade, \place{the out-of-line landing} below \place{the north bridge}, and the clay road up toward \place{High Kiln} and on to \place{Greybank} back on stage — all ground already drawn on \place{atlas/home-settlement}, \place{atlas/the-clay-yard}, \place{atlas/cross-and-fishstairs}, \place{atlas/vail-house}, \place{atlas/high-kiln} and \place{atlas/slate-row}. Bone's second arithmetic went up \place{the clay road} in \person{Berr Clay}'s locked box behind Frost's three and Bone's first and made **five arithmetics stand behind one printed figure**, five readings five and not one account, the figure read still not the figure paid and no signature on the space; the boy kept the finding on a place of its own with no payer and did not lay it across the house's not-doings, the covered grain, the four held changes, the two small objects of the hand or his own gaps (T015/T041/T047 advanced, not paid). Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay, stood half a pace off through the reading and the bell and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing; \person{Hessa Rale} carried the slate up the road toward \place{Greybank} before noon, edge-on and unread, and the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called (T058/T079, T080, T090). The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told; the silent woman's green at the pot board and the foundling's shape at the market's edge stayed sightings with no eighth (T078, T050/T051, T033/T034 held). At \place{the yard well} on Bone 20 \person[Ilva]{Ilva} was glimpsed once drawing with the pail against her knee, her hand going toward her sleeve and staying there with the notched strip unread, answering *Ask the pot what it cost. I keep the count, not the account*; on Bone 19 and 21 she was offstage (T080 held at nine). The hare carried from \place{Cold Acre} and the cracked jar in a rope sling were answered with the weather and the pot and left no entry (T077); the stayed frost gone off the north lip, the skinned trough and the silt-clouded plate on its flat stone stayed weather entered nowhere with a finger between every pair (T083/T085 held). No new road, room or landmark came into the district, and Bone went on without a boundary drawn; Bone 30 (Day 329), the month's third and last Account-day, stood nine mornings ahead and was not brought early. Ch 89.

### High Kiln
**Kind:** district. **Region:** Sill, north terrace.
The master potters' quarter on the steep street above Low Kiln, where the glaze is mixed and the lead smell hangs.
**Relations:** About 200 paces north and uphill of Low Kiln; the Cross is a short way west.

On Tallow 22, Y.H. 813 a kiln-master come up from this terrace on the wrong wind, with Ash on his cuffs and the sweetish lead edge of \lore{lead glaze} riding the air, stopped at the Clay Yard and listened to a pot under Jorra's hands; he said *That one's singing before its time. A pot that sings early generally sings itself out early*, took Jorra's silence for agreement and went off up the yard with his saying, and no pot was lost. On Tallow 21 a pot set down at the racks had already given a high thin note before its time with nobody making a lesson of it, and the boy filed both as dangers of the month, not as a fourth near-full answer. Ch 41.

### The Glaze Room
**Kind:** workshop. **Region:** High Kiln, Sill.
The room where the glaze is mixed and tasted by the glaze-mixers of House Clay; the lead in the mix causes the sweet-sickness, whose first sign is a pale line at the gum. Fenna Clay works here.
**Relations:** Inside High Kiln, on the terrace above Low Kiln; the Vail kiln-house buys its glaze here.

### The Cross
On Reed 18, Y.H. 813 Berr Clay came up short off the clay road with the red cord folded under his coat, wiped his thumb twice, paid two marks short for two pair of eels and said *A number goes into a slate, not a bundle* and *your house*, never *I*; the true subtraction stayed in the locked box up the clay road and the low sentence about one in twelve was spent on the boards with nobody leaning in. No fifth green came to a silent woman's mouth at the pot board in these three days. Ch 32.

**Kind:** market square. **Region:** Sill.
The meeting of the Clay Road and the river road, where the moot shouts, the slate is read and the pigs are weighed on market-day. On Account-day two hundred people lean in to hear the figure while eight pigs are driven over the wet boards; the reading is given twice, once for the moot and once for the households that came late. A bell strikes through the morning and afternoon, longer than a church bell, and nobody crosses himself at it; the boy counts its strokes four, four and a gap. The square has four ways out --- the road down to the south bridge and Slate Row, the steep street up to High Kiln, the north bridge, and the road toward the Bone Orchard --- and a Court table set at its Slate Row fringe where business is done in the open air. People bring figures to a heap of turnips and leave them standing side by side without making them agree. On Stone 30, Y.H. 812 the year's last market was held here: one in twelve was read to the crowd, the Five were spoken of as three days off, and the pause in the neat dark hand was seen a fifth time at the Court's table at the square's Slate Row fringe.
On Ilmen 10, Y.H. 813 Account-day resumed here the morning after a woman was taken by the brook and lay uncollected: eight pigs went over the weighing board, the levy was called for the thaw, one in twelve was read twice and not challenged, and the number for the thaw went from a fist to a mouth to a shoulder and was never given. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself; Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade and carried it home saying nothing about what had been read over it. On Ilmen 20, Y.H. 813, Account-day, Ch 12, Berr Clay came to the block with the red cord folded under his coat and green of glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing and read one in twelve over the basket twice; the true subtraction went unspoken, the number for the thaw went from fist to mouth to shoulder and was given to nobody, and the bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself. The clerk at the Court's table and the clerk set at the Fishstairs both put hand to slate before eye to person, and the boy kept the two orders apart.

On Silt 10, Y.H. 813, Account-day held itself on the dry step above the water while the second step stood under an inch of brown water gone flat and glossy. Berr Clay read one in twelve twice over the water and the basket, the true subtraction went over it unspoken, the number for the flood's cost went from fist to mouth to shoulder and reached nobody, and the bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself; the two hundred leaned in and went back to the pigs. Ch 15.

On Silt 20, Y.H. 813, Account-day, the crowd was thinner than at the flood's reading, the water and the lean week having taken bodies out of the market; Berr Clay read one in twelve over the fewer bodies, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, took himself out of his own sentence and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked. The printed figure stood unchallenged over the thinner crowd and a figure read aloud was not the figure paid. The bell went four, four and a gap and nobody crossed himself; Sella set the basket in the moot-post's thin shade with his cheek to the cold iron and did not look into it. Ch 17.

On Silt 30, Y.H. 813, the tenth day of the tend and the last market of Silt, Berr Clay came to the block with the red wool cord of his office folded under his coat and the green of tasted glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, read one in twelve twice over the basket, passed a finger over the figure and said *The levy is called. It is not called by me*. To a man who brought a number in a fist and asked what the thaw would cost he said *A number goes into a slate, not into a well. If the house wants it, there'll be a slate at the watch*; the number went from the fist to his mouth to the shoulder of a man going up toward High Kiln and arrived nowhere. The true subtraction went over the basket unspoken and the printed figure stood unchallenged; he took himself out of his own sentence and went off toward High Kiln with the box in his house locked and the true deduction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The bell went four, four and a gap and nobody crossed himself, the heard bell kept apart from the told bell of the Long Night; a woman stood across the market with green at one corner of her mouth and was filed in silence without a name. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade with the cold iron at his cheek and carried him home at dusk saying nothing about what had been read over him. Ch 18.

On Seed 10, Y.H. 813, the second Account-day of Seed, the reading block stood wet with market mud and eight pigs went over it while a Sill assessor wrote them down before anybody could ask which eight he meant, his hand going to the slate before his eyes to the rail and then his mouth. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's thin shade and carried it at a half-pace from every open slate. Berr Clay came up with the red wool cord of his office folded under his coat and the green of tasted glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, read *one in twelve, as the notice says* twice over the basket, passed a finger over the figure, called the levy for Seed and said it was not called by him, and went off up the clay road toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The number for the planting went from a fist to a mouth to a shoulder and arrived nowhere, a figure read aloud was not the figure paid, and the bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself. At the Slate Row fringe a man whose name was on no roll, with soft hands and a patient face, handed a figure to a slate and held it where nobody else could see; the boy filed the soft hands apart from Marra, the Drawing collector, the Court-table clerk and the Fishstairs clerk, apart from the child and the loaf, and did not call it a sale. Ch 20.

 On Seed 30, Y.H. 813, Account-day and the last market of Seed, Berr Clay came up to the reading block short with the red wool cord of his office folded under his coat and the green of tasted glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice on his apron before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened against his forearm and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the top of the two hundred, saying *The levy is called. It is not called by me* and, to a man who brought a number up out of a fist and asked what the planting cost now that hands had been in it, *A number goes into a slate, not into a strip; if your house wants it again, there'll be a slate at the watch* --- *your house*, never *I*. The figure read aloud was not the figure paid, the true subtraction sat in the locked box up the clay road, and the figure that went into the slate did not know the hand that had not come back out of the strip (T098 carried). The bell went four, four and a gap and nobody crossed himself, the heard bell kept beside the told bell, and the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at the out-of-line Fishstairs landing with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's shade with his cheek to the cold iron and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing; one place in the returning line was still gone around with a look and no figure, and Reed came on in the dark beyond the door. Ch 26.

On Reed 7, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and kept a half-pace from every open slate, giving the instruction to the wool and the river wind and never to the basket. The bell went four, four and a gap and nobody crossed himself, the heard bell kept beside the told bell. Across the square a woman stood looking at pots with the sweetish green of lead glaze at one corner of her mouth, unnamed and silent, turning a cup a quarter and setting it back where it stood, a pale line across the back of one hand where something had once been worn tight and long; the boy called the colour green and nothing else and held the third such green on a place of its own beside Berr's and the Silt 30 and Seed 28 greens, with no name and no cause. Below the bridge at the Fishstairs the soft-cuffed clerk was still set for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, the market sold over him and a girl with a creel took the out-of-line landing sideways without a fall. Ch 29.

On Reed 20, Y.H. 813 the second Account-day of Reed came to the reading block under the moot-post with the reed month in its last tend: Berr Clay came up short with the red wool cord folded under his coat, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, read *one in twelve, as the notice says*, called the levy and denied it with *your house*, never *I*; the true subtraction stayed in the locked box up the clay road and a figure read aloud was not the figure paid. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told; a silent woman across the square at the pot board turned a cup a quarter and set it back, the fifth green at a mouth and the sixth shape of the colour, a colour and not a name. Below the bridge the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at the out-of-line landing, hand to slate before eyes to stalls. Ch 33.

On Tallow 10, Y.H. 813 (Day 159), the first Account-day and market-day of the firing month, the crowd leaned over the reading block under the moot-post while Berr Clay came up short off the clay road with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one mouth-corner. He wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure, read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the goers-by, called the levy and denied it with *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told; Hessa Rale of Slate Row took Berr's narrow slate up the clay road toward Greybank with her hand to the slate before her eye to the crowd and the writing turned to her sleeve, a runner and not a reader, joined to none. Below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called, and a girl with a creel took the landing sideways without a fall. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and carried it home at the going of the light saying nothing. Ch 38.

On Tallow 20, Y.H. 813 (Day 169), the second Account-day and market-day of the firing month, Berr Clay came up to the reading block under the moot-post with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the crowd. He called the levy and denied it with *your house*, never *I*, and sent a man with a number in his fist to a slate at the watch; the printed figure stood unchallenged, the true subtraction went back up the clay road in the locked box and a figure read aloud was not the figure paid. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told. Hessa Rale went up the clay road with Berr's narrow slate held edge-on, hand to slate before eye to crowd, a runner who carried and did not read, and to a woman who asked what she carried said *I don't read it. I carry it. There's a difference, and the road doesn't care which* and *Cross to Greybank. Reading goes up before noon. I've got a road to walk*; no figure came off the slate. Below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called, the market selling over him as water goes over a stone that lies where a stone lies. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing of what had been read over him. Ch 40.

On Tallow 30, Y.H. 813 (Day 179), the third Account-day and the last market of the firing month, Berr Clay came up short off the clay road to the reading block under the moot-post with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the crowd, said *The levy is called. It is not called by me* and *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness; the printed figure stood unchallenged and a figure read aloud was still not the figure paid. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told. Hessa Rale went up the clay road toward Greybank before noon with Berr's narrow slate held edge-on to her forearm and the writing turned to her sleeve, and to two women who asked what she carried said *I don't read it. I carry it. There's a difference* and *Cross to Greybank. Reading goes up before noon. I've got a road to walk*; no figure came off the slate. Below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called, his hand to slate before eyes to stalls. Across the square at the pot board a silent woman turned a cup a quarter and set it back where it stood, the sweetish green of lead glaze at one corner of her mouth, a colour joined to no ear and never Fenna in narration; at the market's edge the foundling was not seen. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and stood half a pace off, giving no instruction and no reason to the basket, and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing of what had been read over him. Ch 42.

On Ember 10, Y.H. 813 (Day 189), the first Account-day and market-day of the kiln month, the two hundred leaned over the reading block under the moot-post while eight pigs went over the wet boards. Berr Clay came up short off the clay road with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure, read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the goers-by, said *The levy is called. It is not called by me* and *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness; the printed figure stood unchallenged and a figure read aloud was still not the figure paid. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told. Hessa Rale took his narrow slate up the clay road toward Greybank before noon, edge-on and writing inward, and no figure came off it; below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called, the market selling over him as water over a stone. Across the square at the pot board a woman with the sweetish lead-glaze green at one corner of her mouth turned a cup a quarter and set it back where it stood, a colour joined to no ear; at the market's edge a small shape in cast-offs caught the light on a green sliver and went dull, given no name and not counted as a seventh sighting. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and stood half a pace off through the reading and the bell, giving no instruction or reason to the basket, and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing. The reading did not answer the man carried out on Day 188: no figure, collector or slate knew what he cost. Ch 46.

On Ember 20, Y.H. 813 (Day 199), the second Account-day and market-day of the kiln month, two hundred leaned to the reading block under the moot-post while eight pigs went over the wet boards. Berr Clay came up short off the clay road with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *One in twelve, as the notice says* low into the top of the crowd — the figure going twice over the basket, once for the moot and once for the households that came late. He said *The levy is called. It is not called by me* and *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself; Hessa Rale took the slate up the road toward Greybank unread, the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for an uncalled measure, a silent woman's green at the pot board was a colour and the foundling at the market's edge a shape. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and stood half a pace off, giving no instruction or reason to the basket. The reading did not answer the man carried out on Day 188: no figure, collector or slate knew what he cost. Ch 49.

On Ember 30, Y.H. 813 (Day 209), the third and last Account-day and the last market of the kiln month, two hundred leaned to the reading block under the moot-post while eight pigs went over the wet boards. Berr Clay came up short off the clay road with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *One in twelve, as the notice says* low into the top of the crowd, the figure going twice over the basket, once for the moot and once for the households that come late. He said *The levy is called. It is not called by me* and *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The district's arithmetic changed for the third time in the kiln month and cannot be changed back; the printed figure stood unchallenged and a figure read aloud was still not the figure paid. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told; Hessa Rale took the slate up the road to Greybank unread, edge-on and writing to her sleeve; below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called; across the square at the pot board the sixth green stayed a colour and at the market's edge the foundling a shape, with no seventh of either at Ember's last market. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and stood half a pace off, giving no instruction or reason to the basket, and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing of what had been read over him. The reading answered nothing for the man carried out on Day 188: no figure, collector, slate, cause or offstage death. Ch 52.

On Grain 10, Y.H. 813 (Day 219), the first Account-day and market of Grain, Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and stood half a pace off through the reading and the bell, giving the instruction to the wool and the river wind and never to the basket. Berr Clay came up short off the clay road with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the crowd, the figure going twice over the basket, once for the moot and once for the households that came late; he called the levy, denied it with *your house*, never *I*, sent a man's number from fist to mouth to shoulder into a slate at the watch, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The district's arithmetic changed for Grain and cannot be changed back; the printed figure stood unchallenged and a figure read aloud was still not the figure paid, and the reading answered nothing for the man carried out on Day 188. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told. Hessa Rale took the narrow slate up the clay road toward Greybank before noon, edge-on, writing inward and unread, and to a woman at the rail who asked what she carried said *I don't read it. I carry it. There's a difference, and the road doesn't care which*. Below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called. Across the square at the pot board a silent woman's seventh green was a colour and at the market's edge a seventh shape turned a half-beat late and stopped; neither was named, joined or entered. Sella carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing of what had been read over him. Ch 56.

On Grain 20, Y.H. 813 (Day 229), the second Account-day and market of Grain, Berr Clay came up short off the clay road to the reading block under the moot-post with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the top of the crowd, the figure going twice over the basket, once for the moot and once for the households that came late. He said *The levy is called. It is not called by me* and *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The district's arithmetic changed for the second time in Grain and cannot be changed back; the printed figure stood unchallenged and a figure read aloud was still not the figure paid, and the reading answered nothing for the man carried out on Day 188 or the hand that went in on Seed 15 and did not come back. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself; Hessa Rale took Berr's slate up the road toward Greybank edge-on and unread, and the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing below the north bridge for a measure nobody called. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay, stood half a pace off and carried him home at midday saying nothing of what had been read over him. Grain 30 (Day 239) is the third and last Account-day of the month. Ch 59.

On Grain 30, Y.H. 813 (Day 239), the third and last Account-day and market of Grain, Berr Clay came up short off the clay road to the reading block under the moot-post with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the top of the crowd, the figure going twice over the basket, once for the moot and once for the households that came late; he said *The levy is called. It is not called by me* and *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The district's arithmetic changed for the third time in Grain and cannot be changed back; the printed figure stood unchallenged and a figure read aloud was still not the figure paid, and the reading answered nothing for the man carried out on Day 188 or the hand that went in on Seed 15 and did not come back (T015/T041/T047, T077, T098 carried). The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told (T078); Hessa Rale took the narrow slate up the clay road toward Greybank before noon, edge-on and writing to her sleeve, unread (T080); below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called (T090); across the square at the pot board the seventh green stayed a colour and at the market's edge the seventh shape a shape, with no eighth of either (T050/T051, T033/T034 held). Sella set the basket in the moot-post's thin shade where no slate lay, stood half a pace off and gave no instruction or reason to the basket, and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing of what had been read over him. Grain went out without a boundary drawn and neither of the district's two month-names was corrected. Ch 62.

On Smoke 30, Y.H. 813 (Day 269), the third and last Account-day and market of the second firing, two hundred leaned over the reading block under \place{the moot-post} while eight pigs went over the wet boards. Berr Clay came up short off \place{the clay road} with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted \lore{lead glaze} at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the crowd, the figure going twice over the basket, once for the moot and once for the households that came late; he called the levy, denied it with *your house*, never *I*, and went off toward \place{High Kiln} with the box locked and the third true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The district's arithmetic changed for the third time in Smoke and cannot be changed back; the printed figure stood unchallenged, the figure read was still not the figure paid, and the dressed grain covered under wool at home was read over by a figure that did not know what it cost (T015/T041/T047, T077). Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay, stood half a pace off through the reading and the bell and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, the heard bell kept beside the told and its gap not folded into the three counts under the roof or heard as a seventh (T078); \person{Hessa Rale} went up the clay road toward \place{Greybank} before noon with the slate edge-on and writing to her sleeve, and no figure came off it (T080); the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set below \place{the north bridge} at \place{the out-of-line landing} for a measure nobody called (T090). At \place{the pot board} a silent woman's seventh green stayed a colour and at the market's edge a foundling's seventh shape a shape, with no eighth of either, neither named, joined or entered, no loaf, slate or sale (T050/T051, T033/T034 held). Ch 72.

**Relations:** Central Sill, between the two bridges; Slate Row lies a quarter league east; the Fishstairs below the north bridge; Low Kiln across the river to the south.

On Reed 10, Y.H. 813, the first Account-day of Reed, the crowd leaned over the reading block under the moot-post while Berr Clay came up short with the red wool cord folded under his coat and the green of tasted glaze at one mouth-corner, wiped his thumb twice on his apron before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened against his forearm and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the goers-by. He called the levy and said *The levy is called. It is not called by me*, sending a man with a number in his fist to a slate at the watch with *your house*, never *I*; the true subtraction stayed in the locked box up the clay road and the number read aloud was not the number paid. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, the heard bell kept beside the told bell; below the bridge the soft-cuffed clerk was still set for an uncalled measure, and the yard went around the place where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and no number (T098 carried). Sella set the basket in the moot-post's shade where no slate lay and stood a half-pace off. Ch 30.

On Reed 23, Y.H. 813 Berr Clay came up short off the clay road in the afternoon with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened against his forearm, passed a finger over the figure, read *one in twelve, as the notice says*, called the levy and denied it with *your house*, never *I*, saying a number goes into a slate, not a bundle and that there would be a slate at the watch. At the stalls he took two pair of eels and paid two marks short into the stall-keeper's palm without counting them where anyone could hear, let a low voice about the levy fall on the boards with nobody leaning in, and went off up the clay road toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The figure he read did not know the hand that had not come back out of the strip. Below the north bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called, writing inward, hand to slate before eyes to stalls. Ch 34.

On Reed 30, Y.H. 813, the third Account-day and the last market of Reed, the square stood with every withholding together and none of them read. Berr Clay came up short off the clay road with the red wool cord of his office folded square under his coat and the green of tasted lead glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened to his forearm, passed a finger over the figure and read *one in twelve, as the notice says* low into the goers-by over the last bundles; he called the levy and denied it with *your house*, never *I*, said a number goes into a slate, not a bundle, paid two marks short for two pair of eels into a palm that did not count them aloud and went off up the clay road with the box locked and the true subtraction inside, believing he had done a kindness. The figure read aloud was not the figure paid, and the figure that went into the slate did not know the hand that did not come back. The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, the heard bell kept beside the told bell; below the bridge the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, the market sold over him as water goes over a stone that lies where a stone lies. At the market's edge the foundling was glimpsed a sixth time, unnamed and silent, his head a half-beat late toward a set-down pot and stopping in the middle, no loaf, no slate and no sale; across the square at the pot board a silent woman's sixth green was a colour joined to no ear. Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and carried him home at the going of the light saying nothing. Ch 36.

### The pot board
**Kind:** display board, market fixture. **Region:** the Cross, Sill.
The shallow board at the Cross on which potters set out bowls for buyers to look at, dust in the hollows and the light going along the rims. On Reed 15, Y.H. 813 a silent woman stood before it with the sweetish green of lead glaze at one corner of her mouth, clay under her nails and a pale line across the back of one hand, and turned a cup a quarter without lifting it, then set it back exactly where it had stood and went off with the light. The boy filed the green as the fourth at a mouth and the fifth shape of the colour, a colour and not a name, joined to no ear (T050/T051). The board kept the place where the cup had been.
On Reed 20, Y.H. 813 a silent woman stood at this board on the second Account-day with clay under her nails, a pale line across the back of one hand and the sweetish lead-glaze green in the crease beside her lip; she turned a cup a quarter without lifting it and set it back exactly where it had stood, said nothing and went off with the light. The boy filed it as the **fifth** green at a mouth and the sixth shape of the colour beside Berr's glaze and the Silt 30, Seed 28, Reed 7 and Reed 15 greens, a colour and not a name, joined to no ear and never Fenna in the narration (T050/T051 advanced, not paid). Ch 33.

On Reed 30, Y.H. 813, the last market of Reed, a silent woman with clay under her nails, a pale line across the back of one hand and the sweetish lead-glaze green in the crease beside her lip turned a cup a quarter without lifting it and set it back exactly where it stood, said nothing and went off with the light. The boy filed it as the **sixth** green at a mouth and the seventh shape of the colour counting Berr's glaze — a colour and not a name, joined to no ear, never Fenna in the narration — held on a place of its own and not joined to the foundling's sixth half-turn at the market's edge (T050/T051 advanced, not paid). Ch 36.

On Ember 20, Y.H. 813, the second Account-day of the kiln month, a silent woman with clay under her nails in small crescents, a pale line across the back of one hand and the sweetish green of lead glaze in the crease beside her lip turned a cup a quarter, set it back exactly where it stood, said nothing and went off with the light. The boy filed the green as the sixth at a mouth and the seventh shape of the colour counting Berr's glaze, a colour joined to no ear and never Fenna in narration, held on a place of its own and not joined to the foundling's half-turn at the market's edge (T050/T051 advanced, not paid). Ch 49.

On Grain 10, Y.H. 813 (Day 219), the first Account-day and market of Grain, a silent woman stood at this board looking at pots and said nothing all morning: clay under her nails in the small crescents, a pale line across the back of one hand where something had once been worn tight and long, and the sweetish green of lead glaze in the crease beside her lip. She turned a cup a quarter without lifting it and set it back exactly where it had stood, then went off with the light. The boy filed the green as the **seventh** such colour to come to a silent woman's mouth at the pot board, a colour on a place of its own beside the six behind it, with a finger between it and the hearings and no name spoken over it, never Fenna in the narration and joined to no ear, cause or romance. The pale line on the back of her hand went on its own place, not laid across the line read at the well on Day 187 or the line on the man carried out on Day 188; the board kept the shallow print where the cup had stood and gave nobody a figure (T050/T051 paid as a sighting only). Ch 56.

**Relations:** At the Cross, central Sill, beside the reading block and the moot-post's thin shade; the fish-trade shade lies below the north bridge.

### Slate Row
**Kind:** Court quarter. **Region:** Sill.
A narrow street holding the Court's room, the assessor's house, the collector's table and the Waiting Room. On market-days the Court also sets a table at the fringe of the Cross, where a clerk touches the top slate before he looks at the woman waiting and says *it's entered as it stands*; from that end of the square Slate Row is spoken of as a direction, not a place anybody means to go that day. On Ilmen 1, Y.H. 813 the new year's slates were opened here and spoken of from the Clay Yard only as a direction; one in twelve stayed printed and unchallenged and the true deduction was spoken by nobody.
**Relations:** A quarter league east of the Cross; Low Kiln is across the river, south-west.
*The close ground of the quarter drawn on `atlas/slate-row`: the four rooms and tables, the Clay Road and the steep street, the lane to the landing and the Ilm. Direction is from the Court's room.*

### The Waiting Room
**Kind:** room. **Region:** Slate Row, Sill.
The room where the collected are kept overnight before banking, and where the Forty of Low Kiln were held in Y.H. 812.
**Relations:** Inside Slate Row, behind the collector's table.

### The Fishstairs
**Kind:** landing steps. **Region:** Sill, north bank.
The steps below the north bridge where eels are sold at dawn and rumours at noon; Rusk keeps a room above them. The way down counts four steps, four, and a landing where a step should have been; in the Breaking the Ilm comes up over the steps and puts them back a hand's breadth out of line, and the district reads the water as weather and goes on using them.
On Ilmen 9, Y.H. 813 a collector's clerk with a cuff gone soft from washing and a slate was set at the steps for a measure nobody had called; when Sella carried the basket down past the edge the boy saw the hand go to the slate before the eyes went to the stalls. The clerk is not Marra and is not joined to the soft-cuffed clerk who keeps the Court's table at the Cross. On Ilmen 16 and Ilmen 18 he was still set there, cuff grey from washing and slate held to his forearm, hand going to the slate before his eyes to the stalls; word came up with the neighbours on the later morning that nobody had taken him off, and the eels went on being sold over the top of him. Below the bridge on Ilmen 16 a brine-gull went off upriver with a coal from a banked fire that was not yet cold. On Ilmen 20 and Ilmen 24, Y.H. 813 he was still set there in the same order, and the boy filed the hand-to-slate-before-eyes apart from Marra, the Drawing collector and the Court-table clerk; on Ilmen 24 Mira Silt worked the eels with pale slime on her sleeves and showed the second cord twisted twice beneath the grey with the knots uncounted, her board beside Orra's without touching it.

On Silt 2, Y.H. 813 the soft-cuffed clerk sat at the landing with his cuff gone grey from washing, hand to his slate before his eyes to the stalls, and did not speak; word came up on Silt 7 that nobody had taken him off. Mira worked the eels at the outer boards with pale slime on her sleeves and silt in her neck, answered a question about the run with her own answer, moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie, and when a buyer said a figure put her thumb to the grey cord and showed the second twisted twice beneath it, never to Nine Pots or a door, neither knot counted; her board lay beside Orra's knot-string and did not touch it. Berr came up short with the red cord folded under his coat, wiped his thumb twice and said a number goes into a slate and not into a well. Ch 14.
In Silt 9–15, Y.H. 813 the Ilm came up over the lower steps in a thin brown sheet and went up the road beyond them, taking the low houses' doorsteps; it stood on the second step on Silt 10 and came back down through Silt 11–13, giving the four steps back one at a time. The steps came back four, four and a landing where a step should have been, a hand's breadth out of line, and the district called the whole of it weather. On Silt 13 Mira worked the eels at the outer boards as the water fell, Orra stood at the edge with her board and cord and got no answer, and the soft-cuffed clerk kept his place at the landing with his hand to his slate before his eyes to the stalls. Ch 15.

On Silt 17, Y.H. 813 the steps had come back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line, and a whole market went up and down over the gap without making a fall of it; the soft-cuffed clerk sat at the landing with his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to the stalls. Mira Silt worked the eels below the bridge with pale slime on her sleeves and silt in her neck, answered a question about the run with her own answer, and showed the second cord twisted twice beneath the grey with neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's without touching it. Ch 16.

On Silt 20 and Silt 24, Y.H. 813 Mira worked the eels below the north bridge with pale slime dried on her sleeves and silt in the hollow of her neck, answered a question about the run with her own answer and moved eel-bones while deciding, and when a figure was named put her thumb to the grey cord and showed the second twisted twice beneath it with neither knot counted, never upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door and the thin child's knot untouched; her board lay beside Orra's knot-string and did not touch it. The steps had come back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked over carefully without making a fall of it, and the soft-cuffed clerk sat at the landing with his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls. Ch 17.

On Silt 29 and Silt 30, Y.H. 813 Mira worked the eels at the outer boards below the north bridge with pale slime dried on her sleeves and silt in the hollow of her neck, answered a question about the run with her own answer and moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie. When a buyer named a figure her thumb went under her apron to the grey cord and the second cord twisted twice beneath it showed, never carried upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door, neither knot counted; her board lay beside Orra's knot-string and did not touch it. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked over carefully without making a fall of it --- a girl with a creel went over it sideways, caught the rail and went on selling --- and the soft-cuffed clerk still sat at the landing with his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, filed apart from Marra, the Drawing collector and the Court-table clerk with no sixth sighting of the neat dark hand. On Silt 30 a foundling in cast-offs was glimpsed at the market's edge, unnamed in the narration; when a pot was set down his head went toward it a half-beat after the boy's, after the knuckle and after the note, and he rubbed the green sliver at his throat and could not say why. No loaf passed and no slate was seen. Ch 18.

On Seed 4, Y.H. 813 Mira worked the eels at the boards below the north bridge with pale slime dried on her sleeves, silt in the hollow of her neck and her head held where the top of her left ear had been; asked whether the run would be thin she answered with her own question and answer --- *Thin? Who's to say thin? It comes when it comes. Ask the river* --- and moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie. When a buyer named a figure her thumb went under her apron to the grey cord and stayed, and shifting a creel showed the second cord twisted twice beneath it, never carried upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door, neither knot counted and the knot for the thin child upriver untouched; her board lay beside Orra's knot-string and did not touch it. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked carefully over without a fall, and word came up that the weak board in the plank walk at the Weir was gone or trodden flat with nobody gone down to see which. The soft-cuffed clerk still sat at the landing with his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, filed apart from Marra, the Drawing collector and the Court-table clerk with no sixth sighting of the neat dark hand. Ch 19.

On Seed 8, Y.H. 813 Mira worked the eels at the boards below the north bridge with pale slime dried on her sleeves, silt in the hollow of her neck and her head held where the top of her left ear had been; asked whether the run would be thin she answered *Thin? Who's to say thin? It comes when it comes. Ask the river*, moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie, and when a buyer named a figure put her thumb under her apron to the grey cord, showing the second cord twisted twice beneath it with neither knot counted and the knot for the thin child upriver untouched, her board beside Orra's knot-string and not touching it. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked carefully over without a fall, and the soft-cuffed clerk still sat at the landing with his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls. At the market's edge on Seed 9 a small foundling in cast-offs worn like a uniform, silt dried pale in his wrists, a gap in his grin and a green sliver of lead-glaze at his throat, turned his head toward a pot a half-beat after the boy's and opened his mouth on the tail of a kiln-hand's sentence without finishing it; no loaf passed, no slate was opened within reach of the basket and no sale was paid. On Seed 10 the clerk was still set while the Account-day figure went over the Cross. Ch 20.

**Relations:** Immediately below the north bridge at the Cross; the Weir is 1 league upriver; Rusk's room is above the steps.
 On Seed 14, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket at the end of the fish-trade shade where no slate lay; Mira worked the eels with pale slime dried on her sleeves, silt in the hollow of her neck and her head held right where the top of her left ear had been, answered a question about the run with her own answer and moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie. When a buyer named a figure her thumb went under her apron to the grey cord and shifting a creel showed the second cord twisted twice beneath it, never carried upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door, neither knot counted and the knot for the thin child upriver untouched; her board lay beside Orra's knot-string and did not touch it. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked over carefully without a fall, and the soft-cuffed clerk still sat with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, filed apart from Marra, the Drawing collector and the Court-table clerk with no sixth sighting of the neat dark hand. Ch 21.
 On Seed 17, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket at the very end of the fish-trade shade where no slate lay and gave the instruction to the wool and the running water and never to the basket. Mira worked the eels with pale slime dried on her sleeves, silt dried pale in the hollow of her neck and her head held a little to the right where the top of her left ear had been; asked whether the run would be thin she answered her own answer, *Thin? Who's to say thin? It comes when it comes. Ask the river*, moved eel-bones against her thumb while deciding whether to lie and put one away. When a buyer named a figure her thumb went under her apron to the grey cord and stayed, and shifting a creel showed the second cord twisted twice beneath it, never carried upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door, neither knot counted and the knot for the thin child upriver untouched; her board lay beside Orra Weir's knot-string and did not touch it, and Orra watched from where she watched and got no answer. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked over carefully without a fall, and the soft-cuffed clerk still sat with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, filed apart from Marra, the Drawing collector and the Court-table clerk with no sixth sighting of the neat dark hand. Ch 22.

 On Seed 23, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket at the very end of the fish-trade shade where no slate lay, gave the instruction to the wool and the running water and never to the basket, and stood a half-pace from every open slate. Mira worked the eels at the outer boards with pale slime dried in long grey strokes on her sleeves, silt dried pale in the hollow of her neck and her head held right where the top of her left ear had been; asked whether the run would be thin she answered her own answer (*Thin? Who's to say thin? It comes when it comes. Ask the river*), moved eel-bones against her thumb while deciding whether to lie and put one away, and when a buyer in a coat with the river in its hem named a figure her thumb went under her apron to the grey cord and stayed longer than the figure required, showing the second cord twisted twice beneath it, never carried upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door, neither knot counted and the thin child's knot untouched. Her board lay beside Orra Weir's knot-string and did not touch it; Orra was upriver and present only as the board. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked over carefully without a fall, a woman with a creel taking the landing sideways and catching the rail, and nobody calling it an accident. The soft-cuffed clerk still sat at the landing with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, filed apart from Marra, the Drawing collector, the Court-table clerk and the soft-handed man at the Cross, with no sixth sighting of the neat dark hand and no sale. No foundling turned half a beat late at any board. Ch 24.
 On Seed 26, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket at the very end of the fish-trade shade where no slate lay, gave the instruction to the wool and the running water and never to the basket, and kept a half-pace from every open slate and from the soft-cuffed clerk. Mira worked the eels at the outer boards with pale slime dried on her sleeves, silt dried pale in the hollow of her neck and her head held a little to the right where the top of her left ear had been, the small smile kept like a door on the latch; a weir woman with salt dried white at her wrists asked whether the run would be thin now that the planting had gone into its last tend and got Mira's own answer, *Thin? Who's to say thin? It comes when it comes. Ask the river*, while the eel-bones moved against her thumb and one was put away, and Mira said something low about the boat that came up on the flood and nothing about the hands in the strips. When a buyer named a figure her thumb went under her apron to the grey cord and stayed longer than the figure required; shifting a creel showed the second cord twisted twice beneath it, never carried upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door, neither knot counted and the thin child's knot untouched. Her board lay beside Orra Weir's knot-string and did not touch it; Orra was upriver and present only as the board. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked carefully over without a fall, and the soft-cuffed clerk still sat at the landing with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls. At the Cross a man from Slate Row whose name was on no roll handed a figure to a slate held at the edge where nobody else could see it and rested his thumb on the corner afterward without passing it over the number as Berr would; the boy filed the soft hands apart from the other record-first shapes with no sixth sighting and no sale. Ch 25.

 On Seed 29, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket at the very end of the fish-trade shade where no slate lay, gave the instruction to the wool and the running water and never to the basket, and stood a half-pace from every open slate. Mira worked the eels at the outer boards below the north bridge with pale slime dried in long grey strokes on her sleeves, silt dried pale in the hollow of her neck and her head held right where the top of her left ear had been, her small smile kept like a door on the latch; a weir woman with salt white at her wrists asked whether the run would be thin now the hands were out of the strips and got Mira's own answer, *Thin? Who's to say thin? It comes when it comes. Ask the river*, while the eel-bones moved against her thumb and one was put away. When a buyer named a figure her thumb went under her apron to the grey wool cord and stayed longer than the figure required; shifting a creel showed the second cord twisted twice beneath it, never carried upriver to Nine Pots or lifted at a door, neither knot counted and the thin child's knot untouched. Her board lay beside Orra Weir's knot-string and did not touch it. The steps came back four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked carefully over without a fall, and the soft-cuffed clerk sat at the landing with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, hand to slate before eyes to stalls, filed apart from Marra, the Drawing collector and the Court-table clerk with no sixth sighting of the neat dark hand. On Seed 28 across the market a woman with green at one corner of her mouth stood looking at pots, unnamed and silent, and was filed in silence years before the ear that will one day be spoken with it (T050/T051); on Seed 30, Account-day and the last market of Seed, the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at the out-of-line landing with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called. Ch 26.

 On Reed 7 and Reed 9, Y.H. 813 the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at the out-of-line landing below the north bridge with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm for a measure nobody called, his hand going to the slate before his eyes went to the stalls every time, while the market sold its reeds and its eels over the top of him and nobody stepped around him or asked what he was set for. On both mornings a girl with a creel took the landing sideways, one hand out to the rail, and went on selling without looking at her feet, and nobody made a fall of it. The steps kept four, four and a landing a hand's breadth out of line, walked over carefully by a whole market. On Reed 7 a buyer in a coat with the river in its hem named a figure at the eel boards and passed on with it unwritten; the boy set the clerk's order of reaching on a place of its own beside the midwife's reaching hand, the thane's twice-wiped thumb and the woman's hand at the bare shelf, and joined none of them. Ch 29.

On Reed 10 and Reed 11, Y.H. 813 the out-of-line landing below the north bridge kept four steps, four, and a landing a hand's breadth out of line that a whole market walked over carefully without a fall. The soft-cuffed clerk sat with cuff grey from washing and grey again by midday, slate to forearm and hand to slate before eyes to stalls, and entered a buyer's figure before he looked at the buyer; the market sold its reeds and eels over the top of him and nobody took him off. A fish-trade seller wiped his thumb once after touching fish and was kept off Berr's twice-wiped tray. Below the shade on Reed 11 a child's foot stayed dark inside three turns of wool, the cold to the woman who wrapped it and weather to the yard (T093/T094/T096 carried). Ch 30.

On Reed 26, Y.H. 813 the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at the out-of-line landing below the north bridge with his cuff grey from washing and his slate to his forearm, the writing inward, his hand to the slate before his eyes went to the stalls, for a measure nobody called; nobody stepped around him and nobody had taken him off (T090/T080 carried). Below the shade the child's foot stayed dark in three turns of wool, the cold to the woman who wound it and weather to the yard, uncured, uncaused and unnamed while the boy's surgical reading of the extremity going before the core worked perfectly and bought nothing (T093/T094/T096 carried). Ch 35.

### The out-of-line landing
**Kind:** landing, broken step. **Region:** the Fishstairs, Sill, north bank.
The flat place at the foot of the Fishstairs where a fifth step should have been: four steps, four, and a landing a hand's breadth out of line, which a whole market walks over carefully without a fall. In the Breaking the Ilm comes up over it and puts the steps back out of line, and the district reads the water as weather. The soft-cuffed clerk sits here for a measure nobody called. Drawn close on `atlas/cross-and-fishstairs`.
On Reed 20, Y.H. 813 the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at this landing below the north bridge for a measure nobody called, his cuff grey from washing and grey again by midday, his slate held to his forearm with the writing inward, his hand going to the slate before his eyes went to the stalls every time; the market sold its reeds and its eels over the top of him and nobody took him off (T090/T080/T083). Ch 33.

On Ember 20, Y.H. 813, the second Account-day of the kiln month, the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at this landing for a measure nobody called, his cuff grey from washing and grey again by midday, his slate held to his forearm with the writing inward and his hand to the slate before his eyes to the stalls; the market sold over him as water goes over a stone. He is not Marra, not the Court-table clerk and not the foundling, and the figure he entered went inward and unread (T090). Ch 49.

**Relations:** At the foot of the Fishstairs, below the north bridge; the Singing Sill lies downstream.

On Reed 23 and Reed 24, Y.H. 813 the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at the out-of-line landing below the north bridge with cuff grey from washing and slate to forearm, the writing inward, his hand to the slate before his eyes went to the stalls every time; nobody stepped around him, nobody asked what he was set there for and nobody had taken him off since the month turned. Not Marra, not the grey-fingered collector, not the foundling. Ch 34.

On Reed 26, Y.H. 813 the soft-cuffed clerk was still set at this landing for a measure nobody called, cuff grey from washing and slate to forearm, hand to slate before eyes to stalls; the market sold over him and nobody had taken him off (T090/T080 carried). Ch 35.

On Reed 29 and Reed 30, Y.H. 813 the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at this landing for a measure nobody called, cuff grey from washing and grey again by midday, slate to his forearm with the writing inward, his hand to the slate before his eyes went to the stalls; nobody stepped around him, nobody asked what he was set there for and nobody took him off. On the last market of Reed the market sold over him as water goes over a stone that lies where a stone lies (T090/T080 carried). Ch 36.

### The edge of the fish-trade
**Kind:** shaded trading ground. **Region:** the Fishstairs, Sill, north bank.
The boards at the outer edge of the dawn trade, under the north bridge's shadow, where the eel stalls thin out and no Court slate has ever reached. Sella Vail sets the basket here when she wants the boy to see the trade and not be seen into --- the same care she used at the edge of the light at the Drawing. On Stone 15, Y.H. 812 she set him in this shade while Dessa Silt counted nineteen at the head of the steps and Mira Silt put a brown-edged barley crust into the fold. The market bell strikes back toward it from the Cross, four, four and a gap.
**Relations:** At the Fishstairs below the north bridge and within sight of the Cross; the steps run down from it to the landing that is not a step.

On Reed 24, Y.H. 813 a child's foot stayed dark inside three turns of wool below the sill, the cold to the woman who wound it and weather to a neighbour with a pail, so the two facts went past each other and never met; the boy read the extremity going before the core, the almost-smile that was not peace and the afterdrop waiting past a warming nobody would perform, and the perfect reading bought nothing. No silt-leach, cause or cure was named (T093/T094/T096 carried, not paid). Ch 34.

On Reed 26 and Reed 27, Y.H. 813 a child's foot stayed dark inside three turns of wool below the sill, the cold to the woman who wound it and weather to the yard, so the two facts went past each other and never met; the boy read the extremity going before the core, the almost-smile that was not peace and the afterdrop waiting past a warming nobody would perform, and the reading bought nothing. No silt-leach, cause or cure was named (T093/T094/T096 carried, not paid). Ch 35.

On Reed 29, Y.H. 813 a child's foot stayed dark inside three turns of wool below the sill, the cold to the woman who wound it and weather to the neighbour with a pail, so the two facts went past each other and never met; the boy read the extremity going before the core, the almost-smile that was not peace and the afterdrop waiting past a warming nobody would perform, and the perfect reading bought nothing. No silt-leach, cause, cure or recognition was named (T093/T094/T096 carried, not paid). At the last market of Reed on Reed 30 the foundling was glimpsed a sixth time at the market's edge, unnamed and given no dialogue, cast-offs worn like a uniform, silt dried pale in his wrists, a gap where a front tooth had been and a green sliver on a string at his throat; when a pot was set down his head went toward it half a beat after the boy's, after the knuckle and after the note, then stopped in the middle of the turn, and he rubbed the sliver twice and opened his mouth on the tail of a kiln-hand's sentence and let nothing out that any clerk could take down. No loaf passed, no slate was seen near the basket and no sale was paid (T033/T034 advanced, not paid). Ch 36.

### The Bone Orchard
**Kind:** burial ground. **Region:** Grath, outside Sill's north gate.
The ground of pots broken for the dead and of the shallow graves of thin-return children. On market-days a barrow of grey Ash goes out through the north gate toward it, heaped a little above the sides where the load has shifted, and nobody in the square looks at it twice; it is the afternoon's work, the same work that goes out every market-day. the boy puts the grey in the barrow with the ridge under the Vail skirting board and the charred slate in the kiln ash, and nobody explains any of the three. Another barrow went out through the north gate at the last market on Stone 30, Y.H. 812, heaped a little above the sides where the load had shifted; nobody looked twice.
**Relations:** 1 league north of the Cross by the north gate road; Cold Acre is another league beyond it on the ridge.

On Tallow 11–13, Y.H. 813 the yard went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the rack space with its broken birth-slates, the bare dark trough at the well, the pan's gone place and the empty place where a hand went in on Seed 15, with a look and a wide step and no number; men unnamed went low into the finger's-width-open back room before first grey on Tallow 12 and Jorra came through with grey dust ground small as meal, while Tarn's stroke went over the flagstones without the half-check and over the collector's road without a turn, no collector coming. Ch 39.

*The close ground of the burial ground drawn on `atlas/bone-orchard`: the staked orchard, the thin-return graves, the broken-pot ground, the Ash barrow at the south edge, the north gate road and Cold Acre beyond. Direction is from the north gate.*

### The thin-return graves
**Kind:** graves, burial ground. **Region:** the Bone Orchard, outside Sill's north gate.
