The shallow graves of thin-return children inside the Bone Orchard, in uneven rows with gaps and no slate over any of them. A thin return is carried here by the arm that knew the household and set down without a figure. Drawn on `atlas/bone-orchard`.
**Relations:** Inside the Bone Orchard, one league north of the Cross by the north gate road.

### The broken-pot ground
**Kind:** ground for broken pots. **Region:** the Bone Orchard, outside Sill's north gate.
The east ground of the Bone Orchard where pots are broken for the dead and the shards left where they fell. The breaking is done for the house and is not written down. Drawn on `atlas/bone-orchard`.
**Relations:** Inside the Bone Orchard, east of the thin-return graves.

### The Ash barrow
**Kind:** barrow, cart. **Region:** the Bone Orchard, outside Sill's north gate.
The barrow of grey Ash that goes out through the north gate toward the Bone Orchard on every market-day, heaped a little above the sides where the load has shifted, and comes back empty; nobody in the square looks at it twice. Another went out at the last market on Stone 30, Y.H. 812, and was not looked at twice. Its heap is tipped at the orchard's south edge. Drawn on `atlas/bone-orchard`.
**Relations:** From the Cross through the north gate to the Bone Orchard, one league north of Sill.

### The Clay Yard
**Kind:** work yard. **Region:** Low Kiln, Sill.
The slip pits, wheel shed, drying racks and kiln that make up a clay child's whole world. On Kindling, Y.H. 812 the year's fire came up this yard before first light in a clay dish with a coal under ash, passed from hearth to hearth, and no house said where it had first been lit or what it cost. House Silt stands three doors up the yard; Sella Vail's walk from its door to the Vail kiln-house on the third morning is counted by the boy in the bundle as a line of steps, against Dessa's nineteen out and nineteen back. On that morning the sleet of the day before has gone off into a dry cold that lifts the oiled paper at the windows; a woman at a tub looks up as the bundle passes and goes back to her work without appearing to have seen it.
In Silt 9–15, Y.H. 813 the flood stood in the ruts and the shallow pans the slip pits had worn, along the foot of the drying racks, and went around the spent slip and the upturned barrow rather than taking them; when it went it left mud that took a boot to the ankle and gave it back slowly. The sweeping went around the space under the lowest rack three times and entered it none of them; the yard counted what the water took in loaves and glances and a space at the row's end, never in a figure. Ch 15.

In Silt 16–18, Y.H. 813, the flood's mud took a boot and gave it back slowly, and the sweeping went around the clean dark line at the Vail corner, the new finer grey beside it and the space under the lowest rack while the yard counted its losses in loaves, glances and a space at the row's end nobody called a number; at the well a small foundling in cast-offs finished a kiln-hand's sentence, turned his head toward a pot a half-beat after the boy's and took a warm loaf from a man from Slate Row with no slate between them. Ch 16.

Through Silt 19–25, Y.H. 813 the mud set into a brown skin over the ruts that held a boot's print and let it go slowly, and the sweeping went around the clean dark line at the Vail corner, the new finer grey beside it and the space under the lowest rack with a broom and a glance and no figure spoken; the yard counted what the water took in loaves and glances and a space at the row's end nobody called a number. A figure brought from the road in a fist was let go toward the well and arrived nowhere. On Silt 19 the foundling in cast-offs was glimpsed once more at the well with the green sliver at his throat, turning his head toward a pot a half-beat after the boy's and rubbing the sliver; no loaf passed and no slate was seen. Ch 17.

Through Silt 26–30, Y.H. 813 the mud of the lean week set into a brown skin over the ruts that held a boot's print and gave it back slowly, and Sella gave the broom its instruction before she gave it to anybody --- go around what the water left, it cost enough to come in, it will cost more to take out --- going wide around the clean dark line at the Vail corner, the new finer grey beside it and the space under the lowest rack, sweeping none of them. The yard counted its losses in loaves pushed along a shelf, glances at the clay jar and a space at the row's end nobody called a figure. On Seed 1 the first work of the planting month was sweeping, not planting; the strips east of Low Kiln were spoken of as work that would want hands by the morrow and nobody put a figure to the hands or the strips. Ch 18.

Through Seed 2–4, Y.H. 813 the work moved east onto the barley fields and came back to the yard at the well and the racks: brooms went around the brown skin holding the boot-print, the clean dark line, the new finer grey and the space under the lowest rack, and seed went into the strips out of fists nobody counted. Sella gave the broom its instruction before she gave it to anybody (*Round it. The month's for planting and there's nothing planted here. It cost enough to come in; it'll cost more to take out*) and swept none of the three lines; Tarn took the broom only after she had spoken to it and went over the collector's road without turning along it. At the well a woman asked what the planting would cost and was told to ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there, because *the house has said it*; the space at the end of the loaf row was left with no figure. The boy was carried at the edge of the work on all three days, ninety-one through ninety-three days old, keeping his count exactly against the yard's loaves and glances. Ch 19.

Through Seed 5–11, Y.H. 813 the planting became waiting: the seed stood in the strips east of Low Kiln and the yard went around the brown skin holding a boot-print, the clean dark line at the Vail corner, the new finer grey beside it called the thaw and the space under the lowest rack with a broom and a glance and no figure spoken. A woman pushed her broom bristles around the stone the flood had left and looked at the ground a moment longer than the stone required; a fist let seed out and came back with chaff in its creases, and nobody said what the fist held. Sella gave the broom its instruction before anybody and never to the basket, said *the house* where another woman would say *I*, and told a woman at the well to ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there because *the house has said it*. Tarn's broom went over the road a house sends a man down when it wants a collector, over and over, without once turning along it; the small knife lay on its sill with six counted and the seventh nearly gone and still held. A loaf's worth went off the Vail shelf in the night of Seed 6 and a woman carried a loaf out under her arm unentered and unnamed. Ch 20.
 Through Seed 15–17, Y.H. 813 the hands at last went into the strips east of Low Kiln and the yard kept its old arithmetic: brooms around the brown skin holding the boot-print, the stone the flood left, the clean dark line and the new finer grey called thaw, no figure spoken over hands, strips or seed, and a near-spoken number going from a strip to a man's teeth and back again. Sella gave the broom and the bucket their instruction before anybody and never the basket, went around the space under the lowest rack and said *the house*; at the well on Seed 16 Tarn agreed with the early thaw and the late and kept wanting the late because it puts off the paying, his knife on its sill with six counted and the seventh nearly gone, his broom over the collector's road without turning and no collector coming. Ch 22.

Through Reed 1–3, Y.H. 813 the planting month went out and the first cut reeds came down into the ruts where the barley had stood a month before, laid down in armfuls and judged by the arm that carried them; nobody put a figure to a bundle standing in the mud. A reed-cutter told a woman at the racks that a bundle was counted in the arm that carried it, and when she asked about the hands that cut it he went past her with another armful and left the question standing. Sella sent a woman who asked what the reeds would cost to the pot with *the house has said it*, and a man who asked what the work would want to the river. The clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the space beneath the lowest rack and the empty place in the returning line where a hand had not come back were all gone around with a look and no figure, and entered by nobody. Ch 27.

Through Reed 7–9, Y.H. 813 the reeds stood in the ruts at last with green at the broken ends and pale at the frost-bitten tips, judged by the arm that carried them and never by a figure, a day's cutting still a going-downriver and a coming-back at dusk. A cutter told a girl with a creel *Ask the arm that carried it. It'll tell you*, and a man who asked what the beds would want was given the weather and the river; a woman who asked what the reeds would cost was sent to the pot with *the house has said it*. The clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the space beneath the lowest rack and the place in the strip where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out were gone around with a look and a wide step and no number, and entered by nobody. Ch 29.

Through Reed 19–21, Y.H. 813 the last reed bundles came into the yard in armfuls judged by the arm that carried them and went back at dusk the same way; a cutter told a girl with a creel to lift her bundle and let her arm tell her what went into it, *and that's the whole of what it knows*, and a woman at the racks who asked what the hands would be paid was answered with the weather and the river and shut two fingers out of a fist. The sweeping went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the space beneath the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay and the empty place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and a wide step and no number (T009/T013/T092, T098 carried). On Day 140 three or four unnamed men went low into the back room off the wheel-shed before first grey. Ch 33.

**Relations:** Inside Low Kiln, beside the Slip Brook; the Ilm is a short walk south.

On Reed 22 and Reed 24, Y.H. 813 the last bundles came downriver and went back in armfuls judged by the arm that carried them; a cutter told a girl with a creel that her arm knew what went into a bundle and that was the whole of it, and a woman who asked what the hands would be paid was answered with the weather and the river and shut two fingers out of a fist as she went. The yard went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the space beneath the lowest rack with its broken birth-slates and the empty place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and a wide step and no number (T098 carried). On Reed 24 the last bundles stood grey in the ruts with nobody counting them, green at the broken ends and pale at the frost-bitten tips, and the third Account-day of Reed was six days off (T071/T072, T077). Ch 34.

On Reed 25–27, Y.H. 813 the reed month went out with the last bundles going downriver and coming back at dusk in armfuls judged by the arm that carried them, never by a figure; a cutter told a girl with a creel that her arm knew what went into a bundle and that was the whole of it, and a man who asked what the hands would be paid was answered with the weather and the river. The yard went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the rack space with its broken birth-slates and the empty place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and a wide step and no number (T098 carried). Ch 35.

On Tallow 1–3, Y.H. 813 the kilns were brought up in the night for the year's last working season and the Clay Yard stood in the hot dry smell of clay giving up its water, with clay dust ground small as meal under the nails and the sweetish edge of lead glaze on a wrong wind off \place{High Kiln}. The first firing of the month was an ordinary working morning: the yard went around the clean dark line where the \lore{Ash} ridge turned, the new finer hairline called \lore{the thaw}, the space beneath \place[the drying racks]{the lowest rack} with the broken birth-slates and the empty place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a broom, a glance and a wide step and swept none of them. A raking woman spoke sideways of a man in \place{Mud End} taken in his sleep and called it merciful, and nobody made a lesson or a figure of it. The kiln's breath went in fours with a gap behind the wall and was the danger of the month; the carried fire missed its fifth under the floor and the river's note came through the finger's-width door, three counts in three places that did not become one. Ch 37.

On Tallow 21–23, Y.H. 813 (Days 170–172), the last working week of the firing month, the Clay Yard stood in hot dry clay smell with the kilns at their heat and the danger gathered in dust ground small as meal, the sweetish lead edge off \place{High Kiln} on a wrong wind and the kiln's breath in fours with a gap; no room was changed and no pot lost. The yard went around the clean dark line where the \lore{Ash} ridge turned, the new finer grey called \lore{the thaw}, the space beneath \place[the drying racks]{the lowest rack} with its broken birth-slates and the empty place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and a wide step and no number (T009/T013/T092, T098 carried). At \place[the yard well]{the well} \place{the trough} stood bare and dark with its skin of ice gone and the water called weather; the shallow clay pan's gone place kept its round ring on a tray of its own. Costs asked of the firing were sent to the pot, the weather and the river and left no entry (T077). Ch 41.

Through Grain 22–24, Y.H. 813 (Days 231–233) no new place appeared in the yard and no map is owed: the dressed grain lay under wool near the hearth, was turned for damp, spread thin on the flat stone and gathered back by the handful, given no figure and entered on no slate (T077). On Grain 23 the second ownerless asking came over the boards, the weather answering and the price of barley after it, and the voice went off up the terrace with the row standing where it stood; the boy set the second telling beside the first on a seventh place of its own with a finger between them (T101 advanced, unpaid). The yard went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the rack space with its broken birth-slates, the pan's gone ring, the bare trough, the field's empty place and the returning-line place with a look and a wide step and no number; a man come down the clay road brought a number up out of a fist toward the well, looked at it and put it back into his sleeve so the figure arrived nowhere. Ch 60.

Through Grain 25–27, Y.H. 813 (Days 234–236) no new place appeared under the Vail roof or in the Clay Yard and no map is owed: the same rooms kept the same counts and the same ways around them. The dressed grain lay under wool near \place{the hearth}, was turned for damp, spread thin on the flat stone and gathered back by the handful on all three mornings, given no figure and entered on no slate (T077). On Grain 26 the third ownerless asking came over the boards, low into the boards and not into the basket; the weather answered and the price of barley after it, the voice went off up the terrace toward \place{High Kiln} and the row stood where it stood with nobody leaning in to take the answer down. The boy set the third telling beside the first two on a seventh place of its own with a finger between each pair (T101 advanced, unpaid). The yard went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called \lore{the thaw}, the rack space with its broken birth-slates, the pan's gone ring, the bare \place{the trough}, the field's empty place and the returning-line place with a look and a wide step and no number (T009/T013/T092, T098 advanced and not paid). A cost-question at the door, one at the well and one at \place{the loaf shelf} went to the pot and the weather; a man come down \place{the clay road} brought a number up out of a fist toward the well, looked at it and put it back into his sleeve so the figure arrived nowhere. No collector came up or went down the clay road on Grain 25, 26 or 27 (T009/T013/T092). Ch 61.

### The slip pits
**Kind:** pits, work-ground. **Region:** the Clay Yard, Low Kiln, Sill.
Shallow pans worn in the clay of the yard where slip is mixed and spent slip is set to settle; their ruts held the flood in Silt and their colour the children read as weather. House Silt stands east of them, a walk counted in nineteen steps to the drying rack.
**Relations:** Inside the Clay Yard, west and south of the Vail kiln-house; the drying racks stand east of them and the Slip Brook runs behind them.

*The close ground of the Clay Yard drawn on `atlas/the-clay-yard`: the yard, the Vail kiln-house and wheel-shed, the drying racks, the houses of the row and the Slip Brook behind them. Direction is from the Vail kiln-house.*

### The flagstones
**Kind:** yard paving. **Region:** the Clay Yard, the Vail kiln-house, Low Kiln.
The paved ground before the Vail kiln-house where Tarn takes his broom only after it has been instructed. The stones keep the shallow bright patch where his stroke stopped on the first fraction of change in the equality under the roof, a place nobody enters; the stroke goes on over it without the half-check and the patch stays where it stopped, joined to no cause and to no ear. On Reed 14, Y.H. 813 the stroke went on over the flagstones and past the bright patch while Jorra's bent finger stood in the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, and neither man named the other's silence (T036/T076).
On Reed 21, Y.H. 813 Tarn took the broom only after it had been instructed, said *Yes, house* to an early thaw and a late and went on wanting the late because it puts off the paying; his stroke went on over the stones and past the shallow bright patch **without the half-check**, and the stones kept the place where it had stopped while neither man named the other's silence. The boy filed the unchecking stroke beside the earlier half-beat with no cause across them (T036/T076, T061/T082). Ch 33.

**Relations:** Before the Vail kiln-house in the Clay Yard, Low Kiln; the clean dark line and the new finer grey lie in the house's corner nearby.

On Reed 23, Y.H. 813 Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones past the shallow bright patch **without the half-check**, the stones keeping the place where it had stopped, while Jorra's bent finger stood in the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and neither man named the other's silence; the house gave no name to the men who had gone low into the back room before first grey. At the going of the light Tarn told the kindling *Six counted. The next I don't count* and brought the ear-sentence a seventh time to the word that would call the loan, stopping there with no figure and no name and drawing the breath he did not spend (T036/T076, T061/T082). Ch 34.

On Reed 26, Y.H. 813 Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones past the shallow bright patch **without the half-check**, the stones keeping the place where it had stopped, while Jorra's bent finger stood in the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and neither man named the other's silence. At the going of the light Tarn told the kindling *Six counted. The next I don't count* and brought the ear-sentence an eighth time, on Reed 26 after Reed 5, 8, 11, 14, 17, 20 and 23, all the way to the word that would call the loan; he stopped there with no figure and no name, drew the breath he did not spend and went on with the broom (T036/T076, T061/T082). Ch 35.

On Reed 29, Y.H. 813 Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones past the shallow bright patch **without the half-check**, the stones keeping the place where it had stopped, while Jorra's bent finger stood in the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and neither man named the other's silence. That night, with the coals banked and the knife set back where it could be checked, he told the kindling *Six counted. The next I don't count* and brought the ear-sentence a **ninth** time, on Reed 29 after Reed 5, 8, 11, 14, 17, 20, 23 and 26, all the way to the word that would call the loan; he stopped there with no figure and no name, drew the breath he did not spend and went on with the broom. The boy filed nine stoppings on the interval tray as intervals and not payments, with no payer named to any, and the loan stayed uncalled with the seventh year one tend from its end. Ch 36.

Through Tallow 11–13, Y.H. 813 the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for a hundred and sixty, a hundred and sixty-one and a hundred and sixty-two days; the kiln breathed in fours behind the wall and the river's note came through the finger's-width door, three counts in three places that did not become one. On Day 160 Sella's finger went beneath the loaf shelf's front edge where the charcoal lies without looking or a word; on Day 161, for the first time under this roof, Jorra's bent finger waited on the cold damper until she had finished *It wants feeding*, and nobody spoke of the waiting; on Day 162 she carried him home from the Weir around the pan's gone place, the trough and the rack space, set him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, and set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand. No opening came in three days and the widow's mark was not touched. Ch 39.

On Tallow 22, Y.H. 813 (Day 171), while Jorra Vail's bent finger stood in the cold iron of \lore{the damper} and Sella Vail's unfinished sentence was allowed to finish, Tarn's broom checked half a beat behind the standing finger on these stones and went on toward the road without a word or a turn of the head; the stones kept the shallow bright patch where the stroke stopped and neither man named the other's silence. It was the third movement of the equality under the roof, the Seed 22 half-check returned changed after a hundred and fifty days, held and unspoken, filed by the boy beside the Day 161 fraction at the damper and the Day 166 held beat beneath \place{the loaf shelf} as three held changes on three places and not a rule with a payer (T036/T076, T062, T061/T082, T100). No collector came. Ch 41.

### The Vail kiln-house
On Reed 16–18, Y.H. 813 the house keeps its fire banked under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, its payer unnamed for a hundred and thirty-five to a hundred and thirty-seven days; Sella carried the boy in and out at the half-pace, went around the iced trough and the pan set out to dry, set him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, and left the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand. Ch 32.

**Kind:** house and kiln. **Region:** the Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
A kiln-house held by the Vail family for four generations; Sella Vail keeps the fire and Jorra Vail is her son; Corin is taken in here as a foundling on the third day and laid on a bed of fresh straw under the roof. The house is known by its objects before its people: the kiln breathing in fours with a gap where the fifth breath should be, coals banked under ash, a door that does not quite shut, the hollow wheel-seat, a salt-stiff coat on a peg behind the door, and a proverb in the house's mouth (*leave the crack showing*) said to the pot and the fire and never to a person. The Ash ridge along the skirting board is near two thumbs wide, turns the corner without a break, and has not been swept since Garl Vail died uncollected in the Long Cold; it will be swept on the Last Day and burned next morning. In the kiln ash lies the charred edge of a slate, black on one side and eaten into the shape of a strip of bark, the writing gone off the end; the house has swept around it for a year without picking it up. The house keeps a roll of its own, entered in a careful hand and not shown to a man come down from Slate Row with a slate. Through the last tend of Y.H. 812 the boy lay on his bed under this roof with two crusts in the fold, one spent from Stone 15 and one given by Mira Silt on Stone 28, and the kiln took its breath in fours and a gap on every day. On Kindling the year's fire was tipped into this kiln from a clay dish brought up the yard, after Sella touched the widow's mark before the opening and at no other hour; it was still banked under ash on Ilmen 1. The Ash ridge was measured twice with one thumb and left where it lay through the Last Day, then burned the next morning and called dealt with by a neighbour, so that its corner was a clean dark line; the coat, the hollow seat, the uncalled tile debt and the charred slate in the kiln ash kept their places through the turn of the year.
On Ilmen 13 Sella Vail set a clay jar fetched from House Silt on the shelf by the wall, not on the bench where the pot stood. On Ilmen 9 and Ilmen 11 she measured the clean dark line where the Ash ridge had turned its corner for a year and two days against the ridge two doors down, and left both where they lay; on Ilmen 15 the line was still unswept, and the boy kept it as a thing left in place and therefore still kept. The carried fire under blind ash and the back of a broken pot warmed the boards by degrees and missed one breath in every four through all seven days. On Ilmen 16–18, Y.H. 813 the boy was carried down to the Fishstairs and back and set on his bed under this roof, not in the Debt Loft; the fire kept four, four and a gap on all three days, and the coal a brine-gull took upriver on Ilmen 16 was entered in no slate and missed in no account. Sella touched the widow's mark once before the opening on Ilmen 16 and at no bank or on either other day. On Ilmen 17 the smoky barley crust Mira put into the fold on Stone 28 went quietly out of it --- eaten by nobody, lifted in no one's sight, named by nobody and never solved --- while the spent crust of Stone 15 lay stiff where it had always lain. On Ilmen 19–25, Y.H. 813, Ch 12, the oiled paper in the window hung slack in its frame, no longer lifting hard as it once did, and Tarn's eye went to it before the light; a second pot set down in the yard out of a woman's shadow on Ilmen 23 answered nearly full with air under the note and a want in it, filed beside the first as an answer to the Stone 13 interval, advanced and not paid. Through all seven days the carried fire kept under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, warming the boards by degrees and missing one breath in every four; the clean dark line where the Ash ridge turned stayed unswept and the boy was set on his bed under the roof each dusk, not in the Debt Loft. Through Ilmen 26–30 and Silt 1, Y.H. 813, Ch 13, the carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, warming by degrees and missing one breath in every four; the coal the brine-gull took on Day 45 was fifteen days gone, entered in no slate. Sella laid one thumb in the clean dark line where the ridge turned and the same thumb in the ridge two doors down, a finger's width wider, and fetched no broom for either, while the yard prepared a sweeping already done on Ilmen 1. On Silt 1 a new hairline of grey gathered in the corner beside the clean dark line, finer than the old line had ever been; Sella stood over it with a broom, said it was the thaw and would go when it goes, and did not sweep. The boy set the new grey and the left-in-place line in two places that did not touch (T092). The house's arithmetic went around in loaves, glances and a space at the shelf's end that nobody called a number, the exact three days to Silt were kept by the boy alone, and each dusk he was set on his bed under the roof, not in the Debt Loft, with one crust in the fold and the empty place beside it unnamed.

Through Silt 2–8, Y.H. 813 the house kept a new, finer hairline of grey beside the old clean dark line where the Ash ridge had turned its corner, unswept because the house said it was the thaw and it would go when it goes; the boy set the two lines in two places and did not let one become the other. No opening came in the seven days and the widow's mark was not touched once. The carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap, with its payer unnamed and the coal the gull took on Ilmen 16 twenty-two days gone. Ch 14.
In Silt 9–15, Y.H. 813 the flood came up the yard and stood a hand's breadth short of the clean dark line at the corner where the Ash ridge had turned for a year and two days, and went around it rather than taking it; the burned line two doors down stood off the water in the same way. The new finer hairline of grey beside the old line lay unswept through the seven days, and Sella stood over both lines with a broom and swept neither, telling the fire it was the thaw. The carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap with its payer unnamed; the boards above kept four nails and four and a knot the width of a thumb, and the two counts did not meet. Jorra's grey dust came out of the room off the shed on Silt 14 and his clean bone-coloured forearms on Silt 15. No opening came in seven days, the widow's mark was not touched, and at dusk the boy was set on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft with one crust stiff in the fold and the empty place beside it unnamed. The door stood a finger's width open with the river's note through the gap and the fire's four-four-and-a-gap through the floor, kept in two places. Ch 15.

Through Silt 16–18, Y.H. 813 the clay jar stood on the shelf by the wall, the charred slate lay in the kiln ash unexamined and the broken birth-slates lay under the lowest rack gone around by the yard, while the two lines in the corner were swept by neither broom. The carried fire lay at four, four and a gap under blind ash and the back of a broken pot with its payer unnamed; at dusk the boy was set on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, with one crust stiff in the fold and the empty place beside it unnamed, and the door a finger's width open with the river's note through the gap kept apart from the fire's four-four-and-a-gap through the floor. The widow's mark was not touched once in three days, because no opening came. Ch 16.

Through Silt 19–25, Y.H. 813 the carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, warming the boards by degrees and missing one breath in every four with its payer unnamed; the clean dark line where the Ash ridge turned and the new finer grey beside it lay unswept and were called the thaw, and the space under the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around and entered by nobody. At dusk Sella set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread at his mouth frozen and unwiped; the door stood a finger's width open with the river's low note coming through the gap, nearer at the going of the water than at its coming, and the fire's four, four and a gap coming through the floor under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, the two sounds kept in two separate places. One brown-edged crust lay stiff in the fold with the empty place beside it unnamed. No opening came in seven days and the widow's mark was not touched; the private roll stayed unshown. Ch 17.

Through Silt 26–30 and Seed 1, Y.H. 813 the carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, warming the boards by degrees and missing one breath in every four with its payer unnamed for ninety days and the coal the gull took entered in no slate. The clean dark line where the Ash ridge turned and the new finer grey beside it lay unswept and were called the thaw that would go when it goes; the space under the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody. On Silt 27 Jorra came through the finger's-width-open wheel-shed door with fine grey slate dust worked past his bent finger, men unnamed in the back room, and moved the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence; a vessel gave its note past the door and his head did not turn. On Seed 1 Sella told the fire it was the thaw and did not touch the widow's mark, because a month's turning is not an opening and no opening had come in six days. At dusk she set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the fire's four, four and a gap through the floor in two places as for ninety days. One brown-edged crust lay stiff in the fold with the empty place beside it still unnamed and no third. Ch 18.

Through Seed 2–4, Y.H. 813 the carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, warming the boards by degrees and missing one breath in every four, its payer unnamed for ninety-three days; Sella told it the thaw each day and turned the coals with the back of a broken pot. On Seed 3 Jorra came through the finger's-width-open wheel-shed door with fine grey slate dust past his bent finger from men unnamed in the back room and moved the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, cold iron taking the cold out of his fingertip. The clean dark line and the new finer grey beside it lay unswept and were called the thaw; the space under the lowest rack was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody. The boards overhead kept four nails and four and a knot the width of a thumb. No opening came in three days --- a strip, a broom, a well, a market, a door and a bed are not openings --- and the widow's mark was not touched. At dusk of Seed 4 Sella set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread at his mouth frozen and unwiped; the river's low muttered note came through the finger's-width-open door, nearer at the going of the water than at its coming, and the fire's four, four and a gap came through the floor, the two sounds in two separate places as for ninety-three days. One brown-edged crust lay stiff in the fold with the empty place beside it unnamed and no third; his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the water and came down. Ch 19.

Through Seed 5–11, Y.H. 813 the house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar on the wall and a space at the row's end nobody calls a number; the fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap with its payer unnamed for a hundred days, and Sella told it the thaw each day and turned the coals with the back of a broken pot. The clean dark line and the new finer grey in the corner lay unswept beside each other, and the space under the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody. On Seed 6 a second empty place appeared on the loaf shelf beside the old unnamed one: a loaf's worth went off the board between dusk and light with no scrape, crumb, print or word, and a neighbour's hand passed over the bare board and went on with her pot while a loaf carried out under a woman's arm went unentered and unnamed. The shelf's two gaps and the empty place at the boy's ear stayed in two rooms that do not touch and were not named, joined or solved. No opening came in seven days --- a strip, a shelf, a basket, a well, a market, a door and a bed are not openings --- and the widow's mark was not touched; the private roll stayed unshown. At the going of the light on Seed 11 Sella set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the fire's four, four and a gap through the floor, in two separate places as for a hundred days. Ch 20.

 Through Reed 1–3, Y.H. 813 the house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar and unnamed spaces while the boy was one hundred and twenty, twenty-one and twenty-two days old and nobody said so. The carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap, its payer unnamed for a hundred and twenty-two days; Sella told it the thaw on all three days, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, fed them and said *The house will keep it* where another woman would say *I*, standing a moment counting under her breath toward no opening, pushing a strand of coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist, the grey streak pale in the dying light, and not looking toward the bed. On Reed 2 men unnamed before first grey went low-voiced into the back room and Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, the iron taking the cold out of his fingertip while the household straightened around his silence; Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check, the stones keeping the shallow bright patch where it had stopped, and neither man named the other's silence. No opening came in three days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown. At the going of the light on Reed 3 she carried him up the clay road from the Weir at the half-pace, went around the space beneath the lowest rack, set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right at his ear and under his chin without looking at his face and left the thread of drool frozen and unwiped, her hand resting a half-beat longer than the setting needed before it went away; the river's note came through the finger's-width gap and the fire's four, four and a gap through the floor under blind ash, the boards overhead keeping four nails, four, and a knot the width of a thumb, three counts in three places that did not become one. Ch 27.
Through Reed 4–6, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and twenty-three, then one hundred and twenty-four, then one hundred and twenty-five days old and nobody said so. The instruction went to the wool, the river wind, the bucket, the broom, the pot and the fire and never to the basket; Sella said *the house*, went wide around the rack space and the empty place in the strip, moved her lips along a shelf standing with two gaps and sent a woman asking the reeds' cost to the pot with *the house has said it*. On Reed 5 Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of her unfinished sentence and Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check; that night, at the hearth, Tarn brought the sentence about an ear worth more than the debt all the way to the word that would call the loan and stopped there with no figure and no name, and the boy filed it on the interval tray as an interval and not a payment. The loan was uncalled and the seventh year held. On all three days the carried fire kept four, four and a gap under blind ash and the back of a broken pot with its payer unnamed for a hundred and twenty-five days; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door, the boards kept their knot and the three counts did not become one. At the going of the light on Reed 6 she set him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face, left the thread frozen, told the fire it was the thaw and went to her own bed. No opening came and the widow's mark was not touched. Ch 28.

Through Reed 7–9, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and twenty-six, then twenty-seven, then twenty-eight days old and nobody said so. Sella carried him at the half-pace from every open slate and gave the instruction to the wool, the river wind, the bucket, the broom, the pot and the fire and never to the basket; she went around the rack space and the missing hand's place as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it, told the fire the thaw on all three days, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, said *The house will keep it*, counted under her breath toward no opening, pushed coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist, the grey streak pale, set the pot rim out and handle toward the reaching hand and did not look toward the bed. On Reed 8 men unnamed came before first grey into the back room and Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of her unfinished sentence while Tarn's stroke went on without the half-check, neither man naming the other's silence; that night, with the coals banked and the knife set where it could be checked, Tarn brought the sentence about an ear worth more than the debt a **second** time to the word that would call the loan and stopped again with no figure and no name, drawing the breath he should have spent and not spending it. The boy filed a second interval and not a second payment; the loan stayed uncalled and the seventh year was pressed further past its third tend and still held. The carried fire kept four, four and a gap under blind ash and the back of a broken pot with its payer unnamed for a hundred and twenty-eight days, the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the boards overhead kept four nails, four, and a knot the width of a thumb, three counts in three places that did not become one. No opening came in three days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown. Ch 29.

Through Reed 19–21, Y.H. 813 the carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap, its payer unnamed for one hundred and thirty-eight, one hundred and thirty-nine and one hundred and forty days; Sella told it the thaw, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, said *The house will keep it*, counted under her breath toward no opening, pushed coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist, set the pot rim out with its handle two inches beyond the reaching hand and did not look toward the bed. The door stood a finger's width open with the river's note of water over water coming through, and the boards overhead kept four nails, four, and a knot the width of a thumb, three counts in three places that did not become one. On Day 140 three or four unnamed men went low into the back room before first grey and Sella gave them no name and did not say the room was cold; Jorra's dust and his finger in the damper went unasked about, and within hearing of Tarn's sixth stopping she ended her instruction at the pot and asked nothing. No opening came in three days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown. At the going of the light she carried him up the clay road, went around a pan set out to dry and the iced trough and the space beneath the lowest rack, set him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread of drool frozen and unwiped; one brown-edged crust lay stiff two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed and no third. Ch 33.

Through Ember 19–21, Y.H. 813 (Days 198–200) the boy was one hundred and ninety-eight, ninety-nine and two hundred days old and nobody said so. The instruction went to the wool, the fire, the pot, the broom, the running water and the river wind and never to the basket; the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls with the fifth missing, its payer unnamed for a hundred and ninety-eight, ninety-nine and two hundred days, while the river's note through the finger's-width door and the boards' four nails, four and a thumb-width knot kept three counts in three places that do not become one. Sella told the fire the thaw, turned the coals, said *The house will keep it*, counted under her breath toward no opening — small intake, hold, letting go — set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand with the half-beat at the setting, fed him from the house's own dish with his mouth unwiped, moved her lips along the two unnamed gaps on the loaf row and gave nobody a figure, and kept her finger from beneath the shelf so the charcoal stayed unread. The widow's mark hung where it hung at a morning, shelf, market, road, basket, yard, afternoon and dusk, because there had been no opening since before the kiln on Ember 1. On Day 199 he was carried to the Cross at the half-pace and set in the moot-post's shade where no slate lay through Berr's twice-read figure and the bell, and carried home at the going of the light around the pan's gone ring, the bare trough, the rack space, the field's empty place and the carriers' place. Her unfinished sentence at the hearth finished at the pot behind Jorra's remembered finger; Tarn brought the ear-sentence to the word that would call the loan and stopped, nine stoppings with no tenth; the stone lay cold in its loft pot. In the small hours of Day 200 the boy's hand went up an inch toward the river's note, held through a whole counted breath Sella counted at her fire in the room that does not touch his and came down with the letting go — a coordination on a sixth place, not a hearing, seventh, stopping, payment or rule and worth nothing. At the close he was set on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, the fold righted without a look at his face and the thread left frozen; the two rooms did not touch. Ch 49.

**Relations:** In the Clay Yard, Low Kiln; the Slip Brook runs behind it; the Old Kiln is 1 league south; the wheel-shed and the Debt Loft are within it.
 Through Seed 12–14, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and one, then one hundred and two, then one hundred and three days old and nobody said so. The house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar and a space at the row's end nobody called a number, while a neighbour named the shelf's old gap and the fold's gap in one breath without seeing they were one absence. The carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap, its payer unnamed for a hundred and three days; Sella told it the thaw each day and turned the coals with the back of a broken pot. The clean dark line and the new finer grey beside it stayed unswept, the space under the lowest rack went around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it, and the broken birth-slates beneath it were entered by nobody. On Seed 13 Jorra's bent finger went into the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and men unnamed spoke low in the back room before first grey. No opening came in three days and the widow's mark was not touched; the private roll stayed unshown. At dusk of each day Sella set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the fire's four, four and a gap through the floor, in two separate places. One brown-edged crust lay stiff in the fold with its empty place unnamed and no third; the shelf kept two unnamed gaps in the other room. Ch 21.
 Through Seed 15–17, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and four, then one hundred and five, then one hundred and six days old and nobody said so. The house kept its days in loaves pushed along a shelf, glances at the clay jar and a space at the row's end nobody called a number; the carried fire lay at four, four and a gap under blind ash and the back of a broken pot, its payer unnamed for a hundred and six days, and the fire answered four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been when Sella told it the thaw and turned the coals with the back of a broken pot. The clean dark line and the new finer grey lay unswept, the space under the lowest rack went around and the broken birth-slates beneath it were entered by nobody. On Seed 16 men unnamed came before first grey and Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, the iron taking the cold out of his fingertip while the household straightened around his silence; at the well Tarn's knife lay on its sill with six years counted and the seventh nearly gone and still held, his chest tight with a breath unspent and his broom over the collector's road without turning. No opening came in three days and the widow's mark was not touched; the private roll stayed unshown. At dusk of each day Sella set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the fire's gap through the floor in two separate places as for a hundred and six days. One brown-edged crust lay stiff in the fold with its empty place unnamed and no third; the shelf kept two unnamed gaps in the other room. At the going of the light on Seed 17 his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the note of water over water and came down. Ch 22.

 Through Seed 21–23, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and ten, then one hundred and eleven, then one hundred and twelve days old and nobody said so. The house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar on the wall and unnamed spaces; the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap, its payer unnamed for a hundred and twelve days, answering four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been when Sella told it the thaw, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot and fed them. On Seed 21 a neighbour with a pot reached past the palm's breadth of bare board on the shelf, opened for the half-beat of an absence and closed on the loaf beyond, naming the old gap a thing the planting brought in and the new one the price of a wet thing set down wrong without joining them or seeing the empty place at the boy's ear; a third shaping of T097, advanced and not paid, with no third gap. On Seed 22 men unnamed came before first grey into the finger's-width-open back room and Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence while Tarn's broom checked half a beat behind it and went on; the household straightened around the silence, the loan stayed uncalled and the broken birth-slates under the lowest rack were entered by nobody. No opening came in three days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown. At the going of the light on Seed 23 Sella set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen, told the fire it was the thaw and *The house will keep it*, stood a moment with the back of the broken pot in her hand counting under her breath and pushed a strand of coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist, the grey streak pale at her temple; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the fire's gap through the floor, two sounds in two places that did not meet, and his hand went up an inch toward the water and came down. One brown-edged crust lay stiff in the fold with its empty place unnamed and no third; the shelf kept two unnamed gaps in a room that does not touch it. Ch 24.

 Through Seed 24–26, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and thirteen, then fourteen, then fifteen days old and nobody said so. The house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar and unnamed spaces; the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap, its payer unnamed for a hundred and fifteen days, and Sella told it the thaw, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, fed them and said *The house will keep it* where another woman would say *I*. On Seed 24 a neighbour at the shelf laid the old space at the row's end and the empty place at the boy's ear under one cause in a single breath without seeing they were one absence, a fourth shaping of T097 advanced and not paid; a man brought a number up out of a fist toward the yard well and put it back in his sleeve so it arrived nowhere. On Seed 25 men unnamed came before first grey into the finger's-width-open back room and Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence while Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check it had made the day before, the stones keeping the place where it had stopped, the two silences unjoined and the loan uncalled. No opening came in three days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown. At the going of the light on Seed 26 Sella carried him up from the Cross at the half-pace, went around the space beneath the lowest rack, set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen, and did not look toward the bed; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the fire's gap through the floor in two places that did not meet. Ch 25.

 Through Seed 27–30, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and sixteen, then seven, eight and nineteen days old and nobody said so. The planting month went out and Reed came on in the dark beyond the door; the house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar and unnamed spaces, and the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap with its payer unnamed for a hundred and nineteen days. Sella told the fire it was the thaw on all four days, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, fed them and said *The house will keep it* where another woman would say *I*, stood a moment counting under her breath toward no opening and did not look toward the bed, the grey streak pale in the dying light. On Seed 28 men unnamed came before first grey into the finger's-width-open back room and Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence while Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check, the stones keeping the place where it had stopped and the two silences unjoined. On Seed 29 she set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door three doors down at the edge of the light and away from every open slate. No opening came in four days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown. At the going of the light on Seed 30 she set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, went around the space beneath the lowest rack, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread of drool frozen and unwiped, her hand resting a half-beat longer than the setting needed; the river's note came through the finger's-width-open door and the fire's four, four and a gap through the floor under blind ash, the two counts not meeting and the three counts not becoming one. One brown-edged crust lay stiff in the fold with its empty place unnamed and no third, and his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the note of water over water and came down. Ch 26.

Through Reed 10–12, Y.H. 813 the house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar cold under its glaze and an unnamed space, while the boy was one hundred and twenty-nine, then one hundred and thirty, then one hundred and thirty-one days old and nobody said so. The instruction went to the wool, the river wind, the pot, the broom and the fire and never to the basket; the fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap with its payer unnamed for one hundred and twenty-nine, one hundred and thirty and one hundred and thirty-one days; the door stood a finger's width open with the river's note of water over water coming through it, and the boards overhead kept four nails, four, and a fifth that was a knot the width of a thumb. Three counts in three places, and none became one. Sella carried him up the clay road at the half-pace each dusk, went around the rack space where the broken birth-slates lay as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it, set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without a look at his face, left the thread frozen, and did not look toward the bed (T058/T079, T088). Ch 30.

Through Reed 22–24, Y.H. 813 Sella still gave the instruction to the wool, the river wind, the pot, the broom and the fire and never to the basket, said *the house*, kept her half-pace from every open slate, and set the boy on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft. On Day 141 she set the basket low near the bench leg at the angle at which the underside of a board becomes a roof and, with a neighbour at the shelf who reached past the bare board with her hand before her eyes found the boy and named the old gap and the empty place at his ear under one appetite in one breath, sent her to the pot and gave no figure; a further shaping of T097, advanced and not paid (T097, T058/T079). Standing with her back to the basket she reached a finger BENEATH the board where the charcoal tally lies, without looking and without a word, and brought it away; she did it twice again on Day 143 (T100 carried, unread). On Day 142 three or four men with collars high went low into the room off the wheel-shed and she gave them no name and did not say the room was cold, and Jorra's bent finger moved the cold damper ahead of her unfinished sentence. At the going of the light on Day 143 she went around a pan set out to dry and the trough by the well with its skin of ice, set him on his bed, put the fold right without looking at his face, left the thread frozen, told the fire it was the thaw and said *The house will keep it*, set the pot rim out with its handle two inches beyond the reaching hand, and neither looked toward the bed nor touched the widow's mark, because a dusk is not an opening (T058/T079). Ch 34.

Through Reed 25–27, Y.H. 813 the house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar cold under its glaze and unnamed spaces, while the boy was one hundred and forty-four, then forty-five, then forty-six days old and nobody said so. The carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap, its payer unnamed for a hundred and forty-four, forty-five and forty-six days; Sella told it the thaw on all three days, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, said *The house will keep it* and set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand. At the going of the light on Day 145 Tarn brought the ear-sentence an eighth time to the word that would call the loan and stopped there with no figure and no name, and the boy filed the eighth stopping on the interval tray as an interval and not a payment; the loan stayed uncalled and the seventh year stood one tend from its end. No opening came in three days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown; the river's note through the finger's-width door, the fire's missing fifth pull under the floor and the boards' four nails, four and a knot remained three counts in three places that did not become one. Ch 35.

Through Reed 28–30, Y.H. 813 the boy was one hundred and forty-seven, forty-eight and forty-nine days old and nobody said so. The house kept its days in loaves pushed along the shelf, glances at the clay jar cold under its glaze and unnamed spaces; the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four, four and a gap with its payer unnamed for a hundred and forty-seven, forty-eight and forty-nine days, and Sella told it the thaw, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, said *The house will keep it*, counted under her breath toward no opening, pushed coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist, the grey streak pale off the coals, and set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand. On Day 147 her finger went beneath the loaf shelf where the charcoal tally lies, without looking or a word, and came away unread. On Day 148 men unnamed went low into the back room before first grey and she gave them no name and did not say the room was cold; Jorra's bent finger led at the damper ahead of her unfinished sentence while Tarn's stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check, and within hearing of Tarn's ninth stopping she gave the end of her instruction to the pot and asked nothing. At the going of the light on Day 149 she carried him up the clay road around the pan set out to dry and the iced trough and the space beneath the lowest rack, set him on his bed and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right at his ear and under his chin without looking at his face, left the thread of drool frozen and unwiped, her hand resting a half-beat longer than the setting before it went away, told the fire it was the thaw and went to her own bed without explaining the count. No opening came in three days, the widow's mark was not touched and the private roll stayed unshown; the river's note through the finger's-width door, the fire's missing fifth pull under the floor and the boards' four nails, four and a knot remained three counts in three places that did not become one. Ch 36.

On Tallow 1, Y.H. 813, the first firing of the month, the chapter's one meaningful change happened at the kiln mouth of this house: Sella Vail put her hand to \lore{the widow's mark} before opening, held it one breath, let go, tipped the carried coal under the back of a broken pot and did not touch the mark again at a shelf, a well, an afternoon or a dusk. The fire answered four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for a hundred and fifty, then a hundred and fifty-one, then a hundred and fifty-two days. At the going of the light on all three days she told the fire it was \lore{the thaw}, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, said *The house will keep it* where another woman would say *I*, counted under her breath toward no opening, pushed coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist, set the pot rim out with its handle two inches beyond the reaching hand and left the mark untouched because a dusk is not an opening. The shallow clay pan that had stood mouth-up against the wall through all of Reed was gone with no scrape, crumb, print, chip, word or dust disturbance, and nobody said so. The two rooms did not touch. Ch 37.

Through Tallow 21–23, Y.H. 813 the house kept its fire under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for a hundred and seventy through a hundred and seventy-two days (T088); the kiln breathed behind the wall in fours with its gap and the river's note came through the finger's-width door, three counts in three places that did not become one. Sella gave every instruction to the wool, the fire, the pot, the broom and the running water and never to the basket, said *the house*, kept her half-pace, left \lore{the widow's mark} where it hung at a morning, a shelf and a dusk, told the fire it was \lore{the thaw}, turned the coals, said *The house will keep it*, counted under her breath toward no opening, pushed coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist, and set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand. On all three days her eye went once to the clay jar cold under its glaze and her hand shut half over nothing without touching it. On Day 171 her instruction *The fire's low. It wants---* was allowed to finish while Jorra's bent finger waited in the cold damper, and the fraction was remembered in the holding and not in a word (T062). At the going of the light each day she carried him home up the clay road, set him on \place{the bed} under the roof and not in \place{the Debt Loft}, put the fold right without looking at his face, left the thread frozen and unwiped, let her hand rest a half-beat longer at the setting, and went to her own bed; the two rooms did not touch. Ch 41.

Through Ember 10–12, Y.H. 813 (Days 189–191) the boy was one hundred and eighty-nine, then ninety, then ninety-one days old and nobody said so. The instruction went to the wool, the fire, the pot, the broom, the running water and the river wind and never to the basket; the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for a hundred and eighty-nine, ninety and ninety-one days; the river's note came through the finger's-width door and the boards overhead kept four nails, four, and a knot the width of a thumb — three counts in three places that did not become one. Sella told the fire it was the thaw, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, said *The house will keep it* where another woman would say *I*, counted under her breath toward no opening, pushed coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist so the grey streak showed pale, set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand with her hand resting a half-beat longer at the setting, and glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze without touching it. The widow's mark hung where it hung at every morning, shelf, rack, road, basket, yard, afternoon and dusk, because the one opening was before the kiln on Ember 1. On Day 189 she carried him down to the Cross and set the basket in the moot-post's shade where no slate lay; on Days 190 and 191 she sent women's cost-questions to the pot and the weather with *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*, and went wide around the clean dark line, the finer grey called the thaw, the rack space with its broken birth-slates, the pan's gone ring, the bare trough, the field's empty place and the place the carriers crossed, with a look and a wide step and no number. Her finger did not go beneath the loaf shelf on any of the three days, so the charcoal stayed unread. Tarn's knife lay at the edge of its sill with six counted and the seventh at the lip, the Seed 16 breath unspent, nine stoppings with no tenth, the ear-sentence short of the word that would call the loan and the stroke over the flagstones without the half-check; Jorra's dusty forearms, withheld note and remembered damper holding stood as they had. At the going of each light she set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face and left the thread frozen, and went to her own bed; the two rooms did not touch. The crust lay leathery two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed and no third. Ch 46.

Through Ember 13–15, Y.H. 813 (Days 192–194) the boy was one hundred and ninety-two, then ninety-three, then ninety-four days old and nobody said so. The account had gone away up the road in Berr's locked box and nothing under this roof changed hands. The instruction went to the wool, the fire, the pot, the broom and the running water and never to the basket; the carried fire lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for a hundred and ninety-two, ninety-three and ninety-four days; the river's note came through the finger's-width door and the boards overhead kept four nails, four, and a knot the width of a thumb — three counts in three places that did not become one. Sella told the fire it was the thaw, turned the coals with the back of a broken pot, said *The house will keep it* where another woman would say *I*, stood with the back of the pot in her hand counting under her breath toward no opening — the small intake, the hold, the letting go — pushed coal-black hair back with the burn-wrist so the grey streak showed pale, set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand with her hand resting a half-beat longer at the setting, fed him from the house's own dish and did not wipe his mouth, and did not look toward the bed. The widow's mark hung where it hung at every morning, shelf and dusk, because a quiet morning after a reading is not an opening and there has been no opening since before the kiln on Ember 1. Cost-questions went to the pot and the weather on all three days and arrived nowhere; the yard went wide around the clean dark line, the thaw, the rack space with its broken birth-slates, the pan's gone round ring, the bare dark trough, the field's empty place and the place the carriers crossed, with a look and a wide step and no number. Her finger did not go beneath the loaf shelf on any of the three days, so the charcoal stayed unread; the stone lay cold and unreconciled in its pot in the Debt Loft. Men unnamed went low into the back room before first grey on the last two mornings and Jorra gave them no name, his bent finger standing in the cold damper as a remembered repetition and not a new change. Tarn's ear-sentence came to the threshold of the word that would call the loan and stopped there on all three days, six counted and the seventh held, nine stoppings with no tenth and the loan uncalled. At the going of each light she carried him home up the clay road, went wide around the yard's absences, set him on his bed under the roof and not in the Debt Loft, put the fold right without looking at his face, left the thread frozen and unwiped, banked the coals and did not touch the mark; the two rooms did not touch. In the small hours of Day 194 the boy's hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the river's note and held for the length of one of the breaths she counted at her fire in the room that did not touch his — small intake, hold, letting go — before coming down against the fold; it was filed on a place of its own and not promoted into a hearing, a stopping, a payment or a rule, and he still could not close the two inches to the pot handle. Ch 47.

Through Grain 1–3, Y.H. 813 (Days 210–212) Ember gave way to Grain without a boundary drawn and neither of the district's two month-names was corrected; the firing thinned into the ordinary tending of the year. The carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for two hundred and ten, eleven and twelve days, while the river's note came through the finger's-width door and the boards kept four nails, four and a thumb-width knot, three counts in three places that do not become one. Sella gave every instruction to the wool, fire, pot, broom, running water and river wind and never to the basket, said *the house*, kept her half-pace and her two inches, moved her lips along a row with two unnamed gaps and gave nobody a figure, kept her finger on top of the shelf's front edge so the charcoal stayed unread, told the fire it was the thaw and left the widow's mark untouched — no opening in thirty, thirty-one and thirty-two days. Garl was kept in objects — salt coat, hollow seat, two unswept lines, proverb gone to the pot, the debt against the tiles circled without a name — and the yard went around the pan's gone ring, the bare trough, the rack space with its broken birth-slates, the field's empty place and the place the carriers crossed with a look and a wide step and no number. The boy's hand was asked six times across the three days and answered as the body answers, the sixth in the small hours of Day 212 held through a whole counted breath at Sella's fire in the room that does not touch his. There was no market and no Account-day; Grain 10 (Day 219) is the first. Ch 53.

Through Grain 4–6, Y.H. 813 (Days 213–215) no new place appeared under this roof or in the Clay Yard: the first working week of Grain used the same rooms and the same ways around them. The carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for two hundred and thirteen, fourteen and fifteen days, while the river's note came through the finger's-width door and the boards kept four nails, four and a thumb-width knot: three counts in three places that do not become one. Sella came to the fire before the basket, gave every instruction to the wool, fire, pot, broom, running water and river wind and never to the basket, kept her half-pace and her two inches, set the pot handle two inches beyond the reaching hand with a half-beat at the setting and no look toward \place{the bed}, moved her lips along a row with two unnamed gaps, kept her finger on top of the shelf's front edge so the charcoal stayed unread, told the fire it was \lore{the thaw} and left \lore{the widow's mark} untouched — no opening in thirty-three, thirty-four and thirty-five days. At \place{the Vail wheel-shed} Jorra came through with meal-fine grey slate dust from men unnamed who went low into \place{the back room} before first grey; his bent finger stood in \lore{the damper} until Sella's sentence reached the pot. On \place{the flagstones} Tarn's stroke went over the shallow bright patch and the collector's road without the half-check, no collector coming. At \place{the yard well} the bare \place{the trough} stood dark with its water called weather, a rack woman's and a clay-road man's questions went to the pot and the weather, and a number shut in a fist arrived nowhere. The burned-slate word came over the boards a third time from \place{Slate Row} and a kiln-hand come up on a wrong wind from \place{High Kiln} let the saying about a pot not heard from stand; neither arrived anywhere. There was no market and no Account-day; Grain 10 (Day 219) is the first. Ch 54.

Through Grain 7–9, Y.H. 813 (Days 216–218) no new place appeared under this roof or in the Clay Yard: the second working week of Grain used the same rooms and the same ways around them, and no map is owed. The carried fire still lay under blind ash and the back of a broken pot at four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, its payer unnamed for two hundred and sixteen, seventeen and eighteen days, while the river's note came through the finger's-width door and the boards kept four nails, four and a thumb-width knot: three counts in three places that do not become one, for agreeing them would want a payer and none stands to any. At \place{the loaf shelf} Sella kept her back to the basket, her finger on top of the front edge with the charcoal tally unread, and set the pot rim out with its handle two inches beyond the reaching hand and a half-beat at the setting; the widow's mark was untouched in thirty-six, thirty-seven and thirty-eight days. At \place{the Vail wheel-shed} Jorra came through with meal-fine grey slate dust from men unnamed who went low into \place{the back room} before first grey, and his bent finger stood in \lore{the damper} until Sella's sentence reached the pot; the burned-slate word came over the boards a fourth time from \place{Slate Row} and arrived nowhere. On \place{the flagstones} Tarn's stroke went over the shallow bright patch without the half-check and the collector's road without a turn, no collector coming and the warm stone cold in its loft pot. At \place{the yard well} the bare \place{the trough} stood dark with its water called weather, a rack woman's questions went to the pot and the weather, and a number shut in a fist arrived nowhere. A kiln-hand come up on a wrong wind from \place{High Kiln} let the saying that a pot was gone quiet on every row come the thaw stand as a saying, and no pot was lost. There was no market and no Account-day; Grain 10 (Day 219) is the first. Ch 55.

Through Grain 13–15, Y.H. 813 (Days 222–224) no new place appeared under this roof or in the Clay Yard: the middle tend used the same rooms and the same ways around them and no map is owed. The year's first cut grain came off \place{the worked strips} east of \place{Low Kiln} and was taken by Sella at the edge of \place{the Clay Yard}, carried under the roof and set near \place{the hearth} under a fold of wool, counted in glances and handfuls and by the carrying arm's weight, entered on no slate and given no figure. With the returning hands the yard went around the empty place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and a wide step and no number — no name, cause, body, figure or payment (T098 advanced, not paid). Cost-questions at \place{the yard well} and the door went to the pot and the weather; a wrong-wind voice off \place{High Kiln} let one of the three shapings of the pot saying stand over the boards and went on to the price of barley. There was no market and no Account-day; Grain 20 (Day 229) is the second. Ch 57.

Through Grain 22–24, Y.H. 813 (Days 231–233) no new place appeared under this roof and no map is owed: the same rooms kept the same counts and the same ways around them. The carried fire gave four low pulls with the fifth missing under the floor, its payer unnamed for two hundred and thirty-one, thirty-two and thirty-three days, while the river's note through the finger's-width door and the boards' four nails, four and a thumb-width knot kept three counts in three places that do not become one (T088, T061/T082). At \place{the loaf shelf} Sella kept her finger on top of the front edge so the charcoal stayed unread, moved her lips along a row with two gaps and gave nobody a figure, and set the pot rim out with its handle two inches beyond the reaching hand with a half-beat and no look toward \place{the bed}; the widow's mark was untouched in fifty-one, fifty-two and fifty-three days (T058/T079, T100). At the kindling Tarn told the wood *Six counted. The next I don't count* with the knife at the very edge of its sill, and at the wheel-shed Jorra's bent finger stood in the cold damper ahead of her unfinished sentence; the two rooms did not touch. The boy's hand answered six times, the last a two-rhythm coordination carried through one of Sella's counted breaths in the other room and down on the river's next low note, remembered and filed on its sixth place with no payer named. Ch 60.

### The bed
**Kind:** bed, household place. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The board-bed under the Vail roof on which Corin is laid on the third day and set again each dusk when Sella carries him home up the clay road, never in the Debt Loft. It holds a fold of grey wool with one stiff brown-edged crust two inches from his ear, its empty place unnamed and no third; the thread of drool at his mouth was frozen in the small hours and left unwiped because nobody under any roof has been given an instruction about the thread. The boards overhead keep four nails, four, and a knot the width of a thumb.
**Relations:** In the boy's room of the Vail kiln-house; the loaf shelf stands in the other room, which does not touch it. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The hearth
**Kind:** hearth, fire-place. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The hearth of the second room, where the carried fire lies banked under blind ash and the back of a broken pot and keeps four low pulls and the silence where the fifth should have been, with no payer named. Sella turns the coals with the back of a broken pot, tells the fire it is the thaw and says *The house will keep it* where another woman would say *I*; Tarn brought the ear-sentence all the way to the word that would call the loan here and stopped with no figure and no name.
**Relations:** In the fire and shelf room of the Vail kiln-house, across the dividing wall from the bed. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The kiln mouth
**Kind:** kiln mouth, opening. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
