The mouth of the Vail kiln, behind the wall from the hearth, where Sella put her hand to the widow's mark before the first firing of Ember on Day 180, held it one breath, let go, lifted the clay lid and tipped the carried coal under the back of a broken pot; the fire took with its fifth still missing. She has not touched the mark at any hour since. On Day 188 two men carried the glaze-room hand out of this kiln and across the yard with the cost entered nowhere.
**Relations:** Beside the wheel-shed, east of the two rooms of the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The kindling
**Kind:** sill, household place. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The sill where Tarn keeps the small knife, six counted in the worn thumb-mark where the house can check and the seventh at the lip where it cannot; he tells the wood *Six counted. The next I don't count* and brings the sentence about an ear worth more than the debt all the way to the word that would call the loan, stopping there with no figure and no name. Garl Vail's unforged collector sentence lies beside it.
**Relations:** Inside the Vail kiln-house, Low Kiln. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The clay jar
**Kind:** jar, shelf object. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The clay jar fetched from House Silt and set on the shelf by the wall on Ilmen 13, not on the bench where the pot stood. It stands cold under its glaze; Sella's eye goes to it and her hand shuts half over nothing without touching it. Its place is held apart from the loaf shelf's two gaps and the crust in the fold.
**Relations:** On the wall shelf of the fire and shelf room of the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The trough
**Kind:** trough, well fixture. **Region:** the Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The trough at the yard well, bare and dark, its thin skin of ice gone, the water within called weather by those who do not taste it. Sella goes around it with a look and a wide step and no number, and the yard treats its emptiness as weather.
**Relations:** Beside the yard well in the Clay Yard, south of the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The charred slate
**Kind:** slate fragment, ash object. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The charred edge of a slate lying in the kiln ash, black on one side and eaten into the shape of a strip of bark, the writing gone off the end; the house has swept around it for a year without picking it up, and it stays where it lay through the turn of the year.
**Relations:** In the kiln ash of the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The hollow wheel-seat
**Kind:** seat, household object. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The hollow seat beside the wheel that the house knows by its objects before its people, named with the salt-stiff coat, the door that does not quite shut and the proverb about leaving the crack showing; the boy knows the shape of it without being told whose it was.
**Relations:** Inside the Vail kiln-house, beside the wheel-shed. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The salt-stiff coat
**Kind:** coat, household object. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
Garl Vail's coat on a peg behind the door of the Vail kiln-house, salt-stiff and keeping his broader shape. His name is not spoken under this roof; the coat, the hollow seat, the uncalled tile debt and the charred slate in the ash kept their places through the turn of the year.
**Relations:** Behind the door of the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The clean dark line
**Kind:** mark, ash trace. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The line at the corner where the Ash ridge turned after it was burned on the morning following the Last Day. A new finer hairline of grey gathered beside it in Silt and was called the thaw; neither has been swept. The flood of Silt 9–15 stood a hand's breadth short of the old line and went around it rather than taking it, and the boy measured the line twice with one thumb and left both where they lay.
**Relations:** In the corner of the fire and shelf room of the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/vail-house`.

### The loaf shelf
On Reed 16, Y.H. 813 the shelf kept its two unnamed gaps while Sella reached a finger beneath the board where the charcoal tally lies, without looking or a word, and brought it away; the marks stayed unread, uncounted and separate from the two gaps above. Ch 32.

**Kind:** shelf, household account. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The board in the Vail kiln-house on which the house's days are kept badly: loaves pushed along it, a glance at the clay jar on the wall, and a space at the row's end that nobody calls a number. A neighbour reaching for a loaf passes her hand over an empty place without a second look, and the absence is ordinary work rather than an event. A saying of the loaf-shelves of Low Kiln holds that two gaps stay two gaps until a finger lays them together, and that an account then wants a name; no finger in Low Kiln has laid these together. Through Seed 5–11, Y.H. 813 the shelf kept **two** unnamed empty places, the old space at the row's end and a new one that came in the night of Seed 6, while the fold on the boy's bed kept one stiff crust and its own unnamed empty place, in a room that does not touch the shelf's. The boy set the two absences on two places and wrote no cause across the space between them. Ch 20.
Through Reed 19–21, Y.H. 813 the shelf kept its **two** unnamed gaps --- the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6 with no scrape, crumb, print or word --- and no third, in a room the fold does not touch. On Days 138 and 140 Sella stood with her back to the basket at the angle where the underside of the board becomes a roof, moved her lips along the row, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and reached a finger beneath the board where the charcoal tally lies, without looking and without a word, and brought it away; the marks stayed unread, uncounted and separate from the two gaps above and the one crust in the fold (T100 carried, not paid). To the woman at the racks who asked what the hands would be paid she gave the weather and the river, and to the neighbour with the wet pot who asked what the row would want she sent the question to the pot with *The house has said it*. The widow's mark was untouched at a shelf and an afternoon, because neither was an opening. Ch 33.

Through Tallow 24–30, Y.H. 813 (Days 173–179), the shelf kept its **two** unnamed gaps, the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6, and the charcoal marks waited under its front edge unread, uncounted and uncopied. Sella stood with her back to the basket and moved her lips along the row but her finger did **not** go beneath the board on any of the seven days, so the Day 166 held beat stayed where it was left, not repeated and not made a pair with the Day 161 fraction at the damper or the Day 171 flagstone half-check (T100 carried, not paid). The one stiff brown-edged crust in the other room lay leathery two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed and no third; the pan's gone ring kept its tray of its own and the field's empty place stayed where the yard went around it, with no third gap, no fourth likeness made a cause and no finger of his drawing the line (T060/T086/T097). Ch 42.

**Relations:** Inside the Vail kiln-house, Low Kiln; the clay jar stands on the wall shelf nearby, and the bed under the roof is in a room that does not touch it.
 On Seed 12, Y.H. 813 a neighbour reached past the bare board, opened for the half-beat it takes to find what was not there and closed on the loaf beyond, and said in one breath that the old space at the row's end came in with the planting and that the empty place at the boy's ear was a child's appetite that would want filling come Reed; she took her loaf and went with her pot and never saw the two were one absence. The boy filed the two tellings on two places and wrote no cause across the space, and T097 stands planted for the far-off hand that will lay the shelf and the fold together and call them an account. The shelf kept two unnamed gaps through Seed 12–14 and no third came. Ch 21.
 On Seed 15 a neighbour again reached past the bare board and said that the old place at the end came in with the planting and that a row comes up short and a body gets used to it, speaking to the board or her pot and not to anybody, and went off with her pot without ever seeing the empty place at the boy's ear. Sella moved her lips along the row standing with two gaps, glanced at the clay jar and gave nobody a figure; the witness T097 stands advanced toward the far-off finger, still not paid, with no joining and no third gap. The shelf's two absences stayed in a room that does not touch the fold's one stiff crust and its unnamed empty place. Ch 22.

 On Seed 21, Y.H. 813 a neighbour with a pot reached past the palm's breadth of bare board that came in on Seed 6, opened for the half-beat it takes to find what was not there, closed on the hard heel of a loaf beyond and went off saying in one breath that the old space at the row's end came in with the planting and that this one was the price of a wet thing set down wrong. She made a cause of two bare boards and never saw they were one absence or saw the empty place at the boy's ear; her hand went to the board before her eyes found him. Sella moved her lips along the row standing with two gaps, glanced at the clay jar cold under its glaze and gave nobody a figure, and did not touch the widow's mark, because a shelf is not an opening. A **third** shaping of the same held absence stood beside the two earlier tellings and the watcher's late turn on Seed 19, advanced and **not paid**, with no joining, no name and no third gap. Ch 24.
 On Seed 24, Y.H. 813 a neighbour again reached past the bare board and in one breath called the old space at the row's end a thing the planting brought in and the empty place at the boy's ear a child's appetite, laying two rooms under one cause without seeing she had made two bare boards one absence. A woman with wet sleeves was sent to the pot with *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*, and carried the question off unanswered while a man put a figure back in his sleeve. Sella moved her lips along the row standing with two gaps, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and gave nobody a figure, and did not touch the widow's mark because a shelf is not an opening. A **fourth** shaping of T097 stood beside the two tellings, the watcher's late turn and the Seed 21 cause, advanced and **not paid**, with no joining, no name, no fourth likeness and no third gap. The shelf kept its two gaps in a room that did not touch the fold's one crust and its empty place. Ch 25.

 On Seed 27, Y.H. 813 a neighbour reached for the place no longer there and called the old gap at the row's end a thing the planting brought in and the new palm's breadth the price of a wet thing set down wrong, still without seeing they were one absence; on Seed 29 a second neighbour reached past the bare board, her hand going to the empty place before her eyes found the boy, and in one breath called the old space a planting and the empty place at the child's ear a child's appetite, without seeing she had joined two rooms. A **fifth** and a **sixth** shaping of T097 stood beside the two tellings, the watcher's late turn and the earlier causes, advanced and **not paid**, with no joining, no name and no third gap. Sella moved her lips along a row standing with two gaps and gave nobody a figure, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and did not touch the widow's mark, because a shelf is not an opening; she sent a woman who asked what the planting cost to the pot with *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*. Ch 26.

 Through Reed 1–3, Y.H. 813 the shelf still stood with **two** unnamed gaps --- the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in the night of Seed 6 --- and no third; a woman asking what the reeds would cost was sent to the pot with *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*, and Sella moved her lips along the row, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and gave nobody a figure. The fold on the boy's bed kept one stiff brown-edged crust two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed, in a room the shelf does not touch. No neighbour named the two gaps in these three days and the witness of T097 was not paid. Ch 27.
 Through Reed 4–6, Y.H. 813 the shelf still stood with two unnamed gaps --- the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6 --- and no third; Sella moved her lips along the row without a figure, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and did not touch the widow's mark, because a shelf is not an opening. The one stiff brown-edged crust lay two inches from his ear in a room the shelf does not touch, with its empty place unnamed, and no likeness was laid across the two rooms. Ch 28.

 Through Reed 7–9, Y.H. 813 the shelf still stood with two unnamed gaps, the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6, and no third; Sella moved her lips along the row without a figure, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and gave nobody a figure. The one stiff brown-edged crust lay two inches from his ear in a room the shelf does not touch, its empty place unnamed; no finger in the district laid the two shelf gaps together or drew a line between shelf and fold, and a saying of the loaf-shelves of Low Kiln held that two gaps stay two gaps until a finger lays them together and an account then wants a name. Ch 29.

 Through Reed 10–12, Y.H. 813 the shelf still kept its **two** unnamed gaps — the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6 with no scrape, crumb, print or word — and no third, and the fold in the other room kept one stiff brown-edged crust two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed; the two rooms did not touch. From the basket's low angle at the shelf on Reed 10 the boy saw, for the first time, short charcoal marks in clusters on the UNDERSIDE of the board, some pressed hard, some faded nearly into the grain, a keeping put where nobody thought to look above a row kept badly on top. He did not read, count, copy or show them, did not lay them beside the two gaps above, the fold's crust, Alda's tin, Mira's cords, Orra's knots, Dessa's pewter tin or the reed-cutter's cord, and set them on a place of their own held for the far-off day a hand not his lays the district's records together (T100 planted). On Reed 12 Sella stood at the shelf with her back to the basket, moved her lips along the row with its two gaps, and once reached a finger BENEATH the board where the marks lay, without looking and without a word, and brought it away; he saw the hand go under and come back and saw nothing of what the marks said, and made no cause of the habit (T100 carried). A neighbour with a wet pot who asked what the row would want before the tend was out was sent to the pot with *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*. The widow's mark was untouched at a shelf, an afternoon and a dusk, because none was an opening. Ch 30.

On Reed 22 and twice on Reed 24, Y.H. 813 the shelf kept its two unnamed gaps — the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6 with no scrape, crumb, print or word — and no third came; the fold in the other room kept one stiff brown-edged crust two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed, and the rooms did not touch. Sella stood with her back to the basket, moved her lips along the row, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and reached a finger beneath the front edge where the charcoal tally lies, without looking and without a word, and brought it away and did not go back to the shelf. A neighbour's one-breath joining of the old shelf-gap and the boy's empty ear on Day 141 was a further shaping of T097 held on a place of its own, advanced and not paid, with no finger laid and no fourth likeness made a cause. The widow's mark was untouched at a shelf and an afternoon and a dusk, because none was an opening. Ch 34.

Through Reed 25–27, Y.H. 813 the shelf still kept its **two** unnamed gaps — the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6 with no scrape, crumb, print or word — and no third, in a room the fold does not touch; the fold kept one stiff brown-edged crust two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed. On Day 144 Sella set the basket low near the bench leg at the angle at which the underside of a board becomes a roof and, standing with her back to it and moving her lips along the row, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and reached a finger BENEATH the front edge where the charcoal tally lies, without looking and without a word, and brought it away and did not go back to the shelf; a hand that goes under without looking either knows the board by heart or has been told not to look, and the reason is left between the two. The marks stayed unread and were not laid beside the two gaps above or the one crust in the other room (T100 carried). Ch 35.

Through Reed 28–30, Y.H. 813 the shelf still kept its **two** unnamed gaps — the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6 with no scrape, crumb, print or word — and no third, in a room the fold does not touch; the fold kept one stiff brown-edged crust two inches from his ear with its empty place unnamed. On Day 147 Sella set the basket low near the bench leg at the angle at which the underside of a board becomes a roof and, standing with her back to it and moving her lips along the row, glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and reached a finger BENEATH the front edge where the charcoal tally lies, without looking and without a word, and brought it away and did not go back to the shelf; such a hand either knows the board by heart or has been told not to look, and the reason stands between the two. The marks stayed unread and were not laid beside the two gaps above or the one crust in the other room (T100 carried). The widow's mark was untouched at a shelf and an afternoon, because neither was an opening. Ch 36.

On Tallow 2, Y.H. 813 the shelf kept its two unnamed gaps and its unread charcoal; the shallow clay pan that had stood against the wall through Reed was gone with no scrape, crumb, print, chip or dust disturbance, leaving a round pale ring the exact width of a thing taken away, and the boy held that empty place on a tray of its own, apart from the shelf's two gaps, the fold's one crust in the other room, \place{the worked strips}' empty place, the sixth green and the sixth shape. No third gap came and no fourth likeness was made a cause. Standing with her back to the basket and moving her lips along the row, Sella reached a finger BENEATH the front edge where the charcoal tally lies without looking or a word, held it a breath out and back, brought it away and did not go back to the shelf; a hand that goes under without looking either knows the board by heart or has been told not to look, and the reason stands between the two (T100 carried, unread). To a neighbour with a wet pot who asked what a firing cost set down there she said *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*, and the woman carried the question away level with nothing slopped (T077). Ch 37.

Through Tallow 21–23, Y.H. 813 the shelf kept its **two** unnamed gaps, the old space at the row's end and the palm's breadth that came in on the night of Seed 6, and the charcoal marks waited under its front edge unread. Sella went to the shelf with the basket at the low angle at which its underside becomes a roof and moved her lips along the row, but her finger did **not** go beneath the board on any of the three days, so the Day 166 held beat stayed where it had been left, not repeated and not made a pair with the Day 161 fraction at the damper (T100 carried, not paid). The one stiff brown-edged crust in the other room lay two inches from his ear, gone leathery in the dry heat, with its empty place unnamed and no third; the pan's gone ring and the field's empty place kept their own trays and no third gap came. Ch 41.

### The Vail wheel-shed
**Kind:** workshop. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The shed where Garl Vail's wheel stands with its seat worn hollow on the left side from twenty years of leaning; Sella will not let anyone fill it, though she permits her son to sit there --- a distinction the house has never explained. Jorra Vail works there with slip dried to the colour of old bone up both forearms and the left little finger bent from a splinter at eleven, and the clay-bound hand Tarn sleeps above it. A child of the house first learns the full note of a pot here. On the third day a pot set down on the bench gives its small flat note; Tarn's broom stops for two strokes and nobody says what the sound was.
On Reed 21, Y.H. 813 three or four unnamed men came up with their collars high before first grey and went low-voiced into the finger's-width-open back room, neither followed nor named; Sella carried the boy past at the half-pace, gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold. Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bent little finger and under the nails, spoke to the wheel before anyone (*It doesn't care whose hands. It cares if they're steady*), told a pot *You'll do*, knew its full note before it left the wheel and withheld it from a High Kiln kiln-master who said it was singing before its time, and put his bent finger into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence. The broken birth-slates under the lowest rack were entered by nobody and the two tellings of the shut counting room stood beside the damper with no third. Ch 33.

**Relations:** Inside the Vail kiln-house; the Debt Loft is above it; the drying racks stand outside to the east.

On Reed 11, Y.H. 813, before first grey, three or four unnamed men with their collars high went low-voiced into the finger's-width-open room off the shed; Jorra gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold. He came through with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bent little finger, not bone-coloured slip, kept dusty forearms and clean ones on one tray, spoke to the wheel before anyone (*It doesn't care whose hands. It cares if they're steady*), told a pot *You'll do*, knew its full note before it left the wheel and withheld it from a High Kiln kiln-master who would have called it early. He put his bent finger into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, moved the tongue a hair's breadth and left it standing, and the household straightened around his silence; the boy read the iron again as the bite that holds and the bite that can take the hand off a man, with no Grathic word for both mercies, filed as an object joined to no kiln (T035/T031/T062). Tarn took the broom only after its instruction and his stroke went on over the flagstones without the half-check while the stones kept the shallow bright patch where it had stopped; neither man named the other's silence (T036/T076). The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, entered by nobody. Ch 30.

On Tallow 22, Y.H. 813 three or four unnamed men came up the clay road with their collars high before first grey and went low into the finger's-width-open \place{the back room}; Sella carried past at the half-pace, turned no head, gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold, and a second day of men going low brought no third telling of the counting room shut in the war (T035/T031, T065/T095). Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bent finger and under the nails, not bone-coloured slip, kept dusty forearms and clean ones on one tray and asked neither which slate was broken nor whether it was a birth-slate; the broken birth-slates stayed under \place[the drying racks]{the lowest rack}, entered by nobody. He spoke to the wheel before anyone (*It doesn't care whose hands. It cares if they're steady*), told a pot *You'll do*, knew its full note and withheld it from a High Kiln kiln-master who said a pot singing before its time generally sings itself out early; the knowing went to the near-full tray and not among the hearings, and no pot was lost (T061/T082). His bent finger then went to the cold iron of \lore{the damper} and held until Sella's sentence finished, the fraction remembered in the holding and not in a word (T062). Ch 41.

### The drying racks
**Kind:** racks, work-place. **Region:** the Clay Yard, Low Kiln, Sill.
The open racks outside the Vail wheel-shed to the east, where wet pots stand to dry and the yard does its ordinary work around a basket set down at the edge of the floor. Fine grey slate dust from the room off the shed comes back on a thrower's forearms past the bend of the little finger and is read against bone-coloured slip; under the racks is the space where whole slate should have stood and which the yard goes around without entering. On Ilmen 21, Y.H. 813 Jorra came in from the back room with the grey dust again, and Sella went around the space under the lowest rack as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it. On Ilmen 23 a woman set a pot down here out of her shadow and it answered nearly full with air under the note and a want in it, the second earthly answer to the interval carried since Stone 13, advanced and not paid. On Stone 25, Y.H. 812 a pot set down at the racks cracked all the way through with the small flat knock and was called a pot gone wrong and swept around; on Stone 29 the Last Day brooms came out among the racks and a woman at the tubs said the grey would be left where it lay until then.
On Silt 2, Y.H. 813 the brook came up behind the racks with clay heavier than in Ilmen and went over itself with a low note; on Silt 4 the broken birth-slates still lay under the lowest rack, gone around and entered by nobody; on Silt 6 a rack-woman set a pot down out of her own shadow and, under nobody's knuckle, it answered nearly full with air under the note and a want in it, the third such answer, advanced and not paid; on Silt 7 and 8 the slip dried pale in rows while the water went over itself behind them. Ch 14.
On Silt 14, Y.H. 813 the sweeping that had gone around twice already went around a third time and the ridge stood through all three; the space under the lowest rack where whole slate should have stood was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it, and entered by nobody. A woman at the tubs swept up to the rack's shadow and swept back rather than cross it, and another carried a tray of wet pots over it at arm's length. Through the flood the water stood along the foot of the poles and went around. Ch 15.
Through Silt 19–25, Y.H. 813 the space under the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around by the broom and by every woman working the racks, and entered by nobody; the new finer grey in the Vail corner lay finer than the old clean dark line and was not swept, because the house called it the thaw. Ch 17.

Through Seed 2–4, Y.H. 813 the racks stood beside the first planting work: Sella and every woman working there went around the space under the lowest rack as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered it into nothing, and a woman at the racks told a man with a barrow who said the strips wanted hands that the weather would have the last word. The seed went into the fields east of the town and the racks kept their pile of broken birth-slates under the lowest board. Ch 19.

Through Seed 27–30, Y.H. 813 the drying racks stood with the planting's last hands going out of the strips east of Low Kiln and the seed up; the space under the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody, while the clean dark line and the new finer grey in the Vail corner lay unswept and were called the thaw. On Seed 27 Sella carried the boy at the very edge of the worked strips, held the basket level and gave the instruction to the wool and the river wind and never to the basket; one hand that had gone in on Seed 15 did not come out with the last hands and the yard went around its place in the returning line with a look and no number, and the boy filed the absence on a place of its own, not joined to the rack space or the shelf's two gaps (T098). Ch 26.

 Through Reed 1–3, Y.H. 813 the drying racks stood with the first reeds of the year laid in the ruts beyond them, counted in glances and armfuls and never in a figure. The space beneath the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody; the clean dark line and the new finer grey in the Vail corner lay unswept and were called the thaw. On Reed 1 a woman at the rack end lifted two fingers out of a fist and shut them without showing what they had held, and asked the reed-cutter about the hands that cut the bundle. Ch 27.
 Through Reed 4–6, Y.H. 813 the racks stood with the reeds laid in the ruts beyond them in long grey ranks, green at the broken ends and pale at the frost-bitten tips, judged by the arm that carried them and never by a figure. On Reed 4 a cutter told the yard *A bundle's counted in the arm that carries it* and went past a woman's question *And the hands that cut it?* with another armful. The space beneath the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody, and the clean dark line and the new finer grey beside it lay unswept and were called the thaw. Ch 28.

 Through Reed 7–9, Y.H. 813 the racks stood with the reeds laid in the ruts beyond them, green at the broken ends and pale at the frost-bitten tips, armfuls judged by the arm that carried them and never by a figure. The space beneath the lowest rack where the broken birth-slates lay was gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody, and the clean dark line and the new finer grey beside it lay unswept and were called the thaw. Ch 29.

Through Bone 22–24, Y.H. 813 (Days 321–323) the dressed grain lay under wool on the cool stone at the rack end away from the hearth, dry and faintly rasping, giving up its water and going on giving it up, and Sella turned it with a lighter touch and gave no figure. On Bone 22 she lifted the wool, took a small handful and let it fall back through her fingers onto the heap instead of spreading it, saying nothing; on Bone 23 and 24 her fingers went in and came out without closing and the wool came down over the place where no handful had fallen. The boy kept the handful as one object on a place of its own — not a fifth held change, sign, lesson, mending, cause, sixth arithmetic or rule, with no payer and not made again — and the empty fingers of the next two mornings went beside it as a keeping and not as a second (T077 advanced, T060/T086 and T061/T082 advanced, not paid). The space beneath the lowest rack with its broken birth-slates and the other walked-around places were gone around with a look and a wide step and no number; the month's hunts and repairs were directions judged by the carrying arm and the weather, a Cold Acre hare answered with the weather and a cracked jar in a rope sling sent to the pot, which, being a pot, said nothing. Ch 90.

Through Bone 28–30, Y.H. 813 (Days 327–329) no new place appeared in the district: the month's last three days used the same \place{the Vail kiln-house}, \place{the hearth}, \place{the pot board} with the twice-mended pot and its crack showing, \place{the loaf shelf}, \place{the bed}, \place{the boards}, \place{the towel}, \place{the drying racks} and the cool stone at \place{the rack end} where the dressed grain lay covered under wool, \place{the Clay Yard} with its seven walked-around places, \place{the yard well} with its stone lip, \place{the trough} and \place{the flat stone by the well}, \place{the kindling} and \place{the flagstones}, \place{the Vail wheel-shed}, \lore{the damper} and the finger's-width-open \place{the back room}, \place{the Debt Loft}, \place{the worked strips}, \place{the terrace} and \place{the clay road}. There was no market, Cross or bell on Bone 28 or 29; Bone 30 (Day 329), the month's third and last Account-day and market, brought \place{the Cross}, \place{the reading block under the moot-post} with its finger's width of shade, \place{the pot board}, \place{the out-of-line landing} below \place{the north bridge} and the clay road up toward \place{High Kiln} and on to \place{Greybank} back on stage — all ground already drawn on \place{atlas/home-settlement}, \place{atlas/the-clay-yard}, \place{atlas/cross-and-fishstairs}, \place{atlas/vail-house}, \place{atlas/high-kiln} and \place{atlas/slate-row}. Bone's third arithmetic went up the clay road in \person{Berr Clay}'s locked box behind the three of Frost and the first two of Bone and made **six arithmetics stand behind one printed figure**, six readings six and not one account, the figure read still not the figure paid and no signature on the space; the boy kept the finding on a place of its own with no payer and did not lay it across the house's not-doings, the covered grain, the four held changes, the handful, the thumb's resting or his own gaps (T015/T041/T047 advanced, not paid). Sella set the basket in the moot-post's finger's width of shade where no slate lay and stood half a pace off through the reading and the bell, carrying him home at the going of the light saying nothing; Hessa Rale carried the slate up the road toward \place{Greybank} before noon, edge-on and unread, and the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing for a measure nobody called (T080, T090). The bell went four, four and a gap with nobody crossing himself, heard beside told (T078). At \place{the yard well} on Bone 29 \person[Ilva]{Ilva} was seen once drawing with the pail against her knee, clay pale in her nail crescents and grey rack dust in one thumb seam, her hand going toward her sleeve and staying with the notched strip unread, answering *Ask the pot what it cost. I keep the count, not the account*; on Bone 28 and 30 she was offstage (T080 held at nine). At \place{the kindling} Tarn kept six where the house could check and the seventh at the lip beside Garl's unforged collector sentence and did not make the thumb's resting of Bone 26 again on any of the three days; a Cold Acre hare was answered with the weather, a cracked jar in a rope sling was sent to the pot which said nothing, and a woman with a basket of kindling asked what the seventh year would want while two fence-menders argued the thaw early and late (T036/T076/T006, T077 held). At the shed's edge and \lore{the damper} Jorra parted dusty forearms from clean, withheld the full note from a High Kiln master and put his bent finger into the cold iron ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence; \person{Garl Vail} stayed in objects with his name unspoken three hundred and twenty-seven, twenty-eight and twenty-nine days and his tile debt uncalled (T035/T031/T062, T009/T013/T092). Garl's coat, hollow wheel-seat, clean dark line and finer grey called the thaw, twice-mended pot and proverbs stayed where they had always been, and the three coverings called weather were entered nowhere (T083/T085). Bone went out without a boundary drawn and neither of the district's two habits of naming its months was corrected; Ash 1 (Day 330) one morning ahead and Ash 2 (Day 331), the sixth opening, two mornings ahead, were not brought into a day that had not earned them. No collector came up or went down \place{the clay road} on Bone 28, 29 or 30 (T009/T013/T092). Ch 92.

 Through Ash 1–3, Y.H. 813 (Days 330–332) no new place appeared in the district: the first three days of the dark month used the same \place{the Vail kiln-house}, \place{the hearth}, \place{the kiln mouth} out of the draught on \place{the flagstones}, \place{the pot board} with the twice-mended pot and its crack showing, \place{the loaf shelf}, \place{the bed}, \place{the boards}, \place{the towel}, \place{the drying racks} and the cool stone at \place{the rack end} where the dressed grain lay covered under wool, \place{the Clay Yard} with its seven walked-around places, \place{the yard well} with its stone lip, \place{the trough} with its skin of ice and \place{the flat stone by the well} with the silt-clouded plate out of the path of feet, \place{the kindling} and the knife at its sill, \place{the flagstones} with the shallow bright patch, \place{the Vail wheel-shed}, \lore{the damper} and the finger's-width-open \place{the back room}, \place{the Debt Loft}, \place{the worked strips}, \place{the terrace}, \place{Cold Acre} and \place{the clay road} as directions, and \place{High Kiln} only as a wrong wind carrying the sweet edge of \lore{lead glaze} over the boards. There was no market, Cross or bell on Ash 1, 2 or 3; Ash 10 (Day 339), the dark month's first Account-day and market, stood seven mornings ahead and was not brought into a day that had not earned it. The chapter's one change was made in ground already charted: on Ash 2 \person{Sella Vail} carried the basket to \place{the kiln mouth}, set it down level out of the draught where the heat came sideways, and before any instruction went to the basket and before any word put her hand to \lore{the widow's mark} at her throat for one breath, lifted the clay lid with the back of a hand and tipped the carried coal under the back of a broken pot; the fire took, still answering four low pulls with the silence where the fifth should have stood and its payer unnamed three hundred and thirty-one days. **Six openings in six months — Tallow 1, Ember 1, Smoke 1, Frost 2, Bone 2 and Ash 2 — are six and not a rule**; no seventh was made due, nobody praised the opening, named what it mended, entered a slate or made a lesson of it, the five behind were not looked at again as signs or payments, and the mark was not touched again at shelf, well, rack, road, basket, yard, morning, afternoon, dusk or going of the light on any of the three days, because the hand goes to the mark before the kiln and at no other hour (T058/T079, T088 advanced, not paid). Seven walked-around places were gone around with a look and a wide step and no number, the three coverings called weather stayed entered nowhere with a finger between every pair, the dressed grain was turned under wool with the lighter touch and no figure and no handful, and the month's hunts and repairs stayed directions judged by the carrying arm and the weather: a \place{Cold Acre} hare answered with the weather and gone toward \place{the clay road}, a cracked jar in a rope sling sent to the pot, and the pot said nothing (T077, T083/T085 held). At \place{the yard well} on Ash 2 \person[Ilva]{Ilva} drew with the pail against her knee, the rope stiff in the Ash cold, clay pale in her nail crescents and rack dust in one thumb seam, and answered *Ask the pot what it cost. I keep the count, not the account* with her strip unread; the boy filed the order of reaching as the ninth record-first hand with a finger between it and \person{Hessa Rale}'s eighth and filed no tenth (T080 advanced, not paid). \person{Tarn} kept six where the house could check and the seventh at the lip beside \person{Garl Vail}'s unforged collector sentence, the thumb's resting on the uncut seventh checked and not made again and *Yes, house* to an early and a late thaw with the late kept because it puts off the paying; \person{Jorra Vail} parted dusty forearms from clean, spoke to the wheel and a pot before any person, withheld the full note known three turns ahead from any High Kiln master who would make a song of it, put his bent finger into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and kept *he's not Vail* sealed with the promise to the slate-burners neither given nor withdrawn (T036/T076/T006, T035/T031/T062). Garl stayed in objects with his name unspoken three hundred and thirty, thirty-one and thirty-two days and his tile debt uncalled; six arithmetics of the year stood behind one printed figure in \person{Berr Clay}'s locked box up the road with one in twelve printed and unchallenged and the figure read still not the figure paid (T015/T041/T047 held). Ash went out without a boundary drawn and neither of the district's two habits of naming its months was corrected; no collector came up or went down \place{the clay road} on Ash 1, 2 or 3 (T009/T013/T092). Ch 93.

**Relations:** Inside the Clay Yard, east of the Vail wheel-shed; the Slip Brook runs behind them; House Silt stands three doors down the yard.

### The Debt Loft
**Kind:** sleeping loft. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
The narrow loft above the wheel-shed where the house's clay-bound hands sleep; Tarn has slept here for six of his seven years and keeps his few things in an unmarked clay pot. On the third day the foundling is given a bed under the Vail roof while Tarn is given this loft --- a poor household sorting its own members and calling the sorting kindness. Tarn came down the ladder with a broom when the boy was laid on the straw, and said *He's a foundling. I'm a debt. Don't put us in the same sentence, Sella.*
**Relations:** Above the Vail wheel-shed, inside the Vail kiln-house.

### The back room
**Kind:** work room, kept closed. **Region:** the Vail kiln-house, Clay Yard, Low Kiln.
A room off the wheel-shed which the house does not enter in any roll it shows. Men come into it whose names are not spoken and speak in low voices; Jorra Vail comes out of it with slate dust worked into the creases of his forearms, grey and fine and not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel, and says *the back room's cold* to nobody in particular. The boy watches the dust and wants to know which slate was broken and whether it was a birth-slate, and cannot ask. Sella shifts the damper instead of answering. The broken slates under the drying rack remain unshown.
On Silt 4, Y.H. 813 the room kept its finger's width of open all morning and Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust worked into both forearms past the bend of his little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel; two men had come in since first light and spoken low and were not named. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack where the yard went around them and entered them into nothing. Ch 14.
On Silt 22, Y.H. 813 the room kept its finger's width of open and Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust worked into both forearms past the bend of his little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel; men had come in since first light and spoken low and he gave the house no name for them. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, gone around and entered by nobody, and the boy kept dusty forearms and clean ones on one tray without asking which slate was broken or whether it was a birth-slate. Ch 17.

On Seed 3, Y.H. 813 the room again kept its finger's width of open and Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust worked into both forearms past the bend of his little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel; men had come in since first light and spoken low in it and he gave the house no name for them. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody. He moved the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence and read it as the iron bite that holds warmth in and can take the hand off a man, with no Grathic word for both mercies; the two tellings of the shut counting room stayed on their tray joined to no kiln. Ch 19.
On Seed 7, Y.H. 813 the room again stood a finger's width open and men came in before first grey and spoke low in it; Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust worked into both forearms past the bend of his little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel, and gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody. His bent finger went into the damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, and he read the iron as the bite that holds warmth in and can take the hand off a man, with no Grathic word for both mercies. Ch 20.
 On Seed 16, Y.H. 813 the room again stood a finger's width open and men came in before first grey and spoke low in it; Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust worked into both forearms past the bend of his little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel, and gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, gone around and entered by nobody, and the boy kept dusty forearms and clean ones on one tray without asking which slate was broken or whether it was a birth-slate. Jorra's bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of Sella's unfinished sentence, the iron taking the cold out of his fingertip while the household straightened around his silence, and he filed the reading of the double bite as an object, exact and worth nothing, joined to no kiln. Ch 22.

 On Seed 22, Y.H. 813 men came up the Clay Yard before first grey with their collars high and spoke low in the finger's-width-open back room; Jorra gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold, and came through with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bend of his little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel. He spoke to the wheel before anyone (*It doesn't care whose hands. It cares if they're steady*), told a pot *You'll do*, knew its full note before it left the wheel and would not say so before a kiln-master from High Kiln. When Sella had not finished telling him the fire was low his bent finger went into the cold damper ahead of her sentence, the iron taking the cold out of his fingertip and giving a little back in reverse of the core; the household straightened around his silence and she gave the end of her instruction to the pot. **For the first time Tarn's broom checked half a beat behind the standing finger and went on toward the road without a word or a turn of the head** — the first fraction of change in the equality under the roof, still unspoken, the two silences not joined and the loan that can hear not called. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, gone around and entered by nobody. Ch 24.

 On Seed 28, Y.H. 813 men came up the Clay Yard before first grey with their collars high and went low-voiced into the finger's-width-open room off the wheel-shed; Jorra gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold, and came through with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bend of his little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel. He kept dusty forearms and clean ones on one tray without asking which slate had been broken or whether it was a birth-slate. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, gone around as wide as a pail of hot water set where a child might knock it and entered by nobody. Ch 26.

 On Reed 8, Y.H. 813 men unnamed came up the Clay Yard before first grey with collars high and went low-voiced into the finger's-width-open room off the wheel-shed; Jorra gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold, and came through with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bend of the little finger, not the bone-coloured slip of the wheel, keeping dusty forearms and clean ones on one tray. He spoke to the wheel before anyone (*It doesn't care whose hands. It cares if they're steady*), told a pot *You'll do*, knew its full note before it left the wheel and withheld it from a kiln-master come up from High Kiln who would have called it early. The broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack, entered by nobody. Ch 29.

On Reed 11, Y.H. 813 the room off the wheel-shed stood a finger's width open before first grey while three or four men unnamed went low-voiced into it with their collars high; Jorra came out of the shed with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bent little finger, gave the house no name for the men and did not say the room was cold. The two tellings of the shut counting room stayed beside the cold iron with no third, joined to no kiln (T065/T095); the broken birth-slates under the lowest rack and the place where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come back were gone around and entered by nobody. Ch 30.

On Reed 26, Y.H. 813 three or four men with collars high went low into the finger's-width-open back room before first grey; Sella gave the house no name for them and did not say the room was cold, and Jorra came through with fine grey slate dust ground small as meal into both forearms past the bent finger, not bone-coloured slip, keeping dusty forearms and clean ones on one tray and asking neither which slate had been broken nor whether it was a birth-slate. He spoke to the wheel before anyone (*It doesn't care whose hands. It cares if they're steady*), told a pot *You'll do*, knew its full note before it left the wheel and withheld it from a High Kiln kiln-master who said it was singing before its time; the broken birth-slates stayed under the lowest rack entered by nobody and the two tellings of the shut counting room stood beside the cold damper with no third (T035/T031/T062, T065/T095). Ch 35.

**Relations:** Inside the Vail kiln-house, off the wheel-shed; the Debt Loft is above the shed.

### The close ground of Sill
*The small features of the birth-town drawn on `atlas/home-settlement`: the land a child of Low Kiln knows by foot. Direction and distance are from the Cross unless the entry says otherwise.*

### The north bridge
**Kind:** bridge. **Region:** Sill, at the Cross.
The upper of Sill's two bridges, by the market square; the Fishstairs lie just below it on the north bank and the Singing Sill a little way downstream.
**Relations:** At the Cross, over the Ilm above the four-foot fall; the south bridge lies downriver, beyond Slate Row.

### The south bridge
**Kind:** bridge. **Region:** Sill.
The lower bridge, carrying the Clay Road and the river road out east of the town toward Greybank.
**Relations:** Downriver of the Cross and Slate Row; the meal mill stands just below it on the south bank.

### House Clay
**Kind:** house, holding. **Region:** High Kiln, Sill.
The kiln-master's house of Berr Clay, Thane of Clay, above the terrace. The red wool cord is folded away in a drawer here, and a locked box kept in the house has never been destroyed.
**Relations:** In High Kiln, above Low Kiln and west of the Glaze Room.

### House Silt
**Kind:** house, holding. **Region:** Low Kiln, Sill.
The two-room clay-daubed house of Dessa and Mira Silt, three doors from the Vail kiln-house; its front room is the birth room of Chapter 1, where Corin is born on Stone 7, Y.H. 812. The floor is trodden ash packed until it has the spring of old bread, and a grey ridge of Ash runs along the skirting board from the bench by the door, under the door and into the next room, left there until the Last Day. The room holds a board-bed with a fold of grey wool, a stool by the wall, a hearth with a slip pot, and the birth-slate propped on the bench within the midwife's right hand, its writing turned under. The door does not shut; a low song from a woman working below comes up through the floor. The slip pits are a walk away, counted in nineteen steps to the drying rack. The stone at the newborn's belly was warmed at the Vail hearth across the yard and carried here in a rag; on the third morning it is left behind in the fold of grey wool on the abandoned bed when Sella carries the boy away, and the cup across the room gives its flat note to the empty bed. Alda sets a cup down twice in this room on the second day and neither time does anyone say what the sound was.
On Ilmen 9, Y.H. 813 Dessa Silt was taken by the clay-heavy rise of the brook and the room was empty thereafter; on Ilmen 13 Sella Vail carried the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door and fetched a clay jar from the shelf below another shelf, beside a chipped cup, setting it on the shelf by the wall at home. Above the board-bed a round pewter tin still holds a scrap of cloth from the birth, with a pale fold showing at the seam of the lid and a thumb's bright rub on it; the boy saw it from the height at which shelves are highest and entered it nowhere, and nobody opened it or said the woman's name or the number in her thumb. On Ilmen 27, Y.H. 813, Ch 13, Sella carried the basket three doors down and set it just inside the door, standing a finger's width open, at the edge of the light and away from every open slate; she opened no slate and turned no errand into a walk to Slate Row. From the low angle of the basket the boy saw the tin again, neither opened; Alda Reed came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt, laid the birth-slates writing-down on the bench before turning to the fold, pressed the fold too long, crossed two fingers at the flower's red edge unaware and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa, touched the tin inside her bodice without opening it and wrote no sixth note. Two private records in two materials stood in one room on separate trays and were read by nobody (T089, T023).

On Silt 5, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light and away from every open slate. Above the abandoned board-bed the pewter tin kept its pale fold and thumb-bright rub, unopened, and the fold of grey wool was empty of the small pale river stone that had once lain there, unmentioned by house or boy. Alda laid the slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, covered the unaware sign with the date she gave Dessa, said *warm and breathing* exact and not true, and wrote the sixth note in the second hand which she turned face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name; the hidden record grew from five pages to six, unread. Ch 14.
On Silt 13, Y.H. 813 as the water fell Sella carried the basket three doors down and set it just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light, away from every place a slate might lie; the round pewter tin above the abandoned board-bed kept its pale fold and thumb-bright rub, unopened, and the fold of grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale stone had once lain. Alda's sixth narrow page stayed turned face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy could not name, and neither record was carried to a door or read by anybody. Ch 15.

On Silt 17, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light and away from every place a slate might lie. The round pewter tin above the abandoned board-bed kept its pale fold at the seam and thumb-bright rub on the lid, unopened, and the fold of grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain; nobody said it was gone. Alda Reed laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before turning to the fold, crossed two fingers at the flower's red edge unaware and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa, now thirty-nine mornings dead, said *warm and breathing* which was exact and not true, and turned her sixth page toward a floorboard; neither record was read or carried to a door. Ch 16.

On Silt 21, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light and away from every place a slate might lie, opened no slate and turned no errand into a walk to Slate Row. The round pewter tin above the abandoned board-bed kept its pale fold at the seam and thumb-bright rub on the lid, unopened; the fold of grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain, unmentioned. Alda Reed laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed it too long at his shoulder, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower unknowing and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa, now forty mornings dead and uncollected; she said *warm and breathing*, exact and not true, touched the little tin in her bodice without opening it and turned her sixth narrow slate face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name. Two private records in two materials stood unread in one room and were carried to no door. Ch 17.

On Silt 28, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light and away from every place a slate might lie, stood a half-pace off and opened no slate. Above the abandoned board-bed the round pewter tin kept its pale fold at the seam and thumb-bright rub on the lid, unopened; below it the fold of grey wool held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain, and nobody said either was gone. A chipped cup stood on the shelf by the wall and a grey ridge of Ash ran along the skirting from the bench under the door into the next room. In the back room a neighbour scrubbed at a board with lye water and a low song flattened out of its words by work. Alda Reed came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and lye sour under the soap, laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed it too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew it and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa --- more mornings dead now than any living person keeps count of --- saying *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, and going on with dates after the question had stopped. Her hand went once to the little tin in her bodice without opening it; her sixth narrow slate stayed face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name, six pages unread, no seventh written and T023 not paid again. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door. Ch 18.

On Seed 4, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light, away from every place a slate might lie, opened no slate and turned no errand into a walk to Slate Row, standing a half-pace off. The round pewter tin above the abandoned board-bed kept its pale fold at the seam and thumb-bright rub on the lid, unopened, and the fold of grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain; nobody said either was gone. Alda Reed came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and lye sour under the soap, nails scrubbed to the quick with clay still under them, laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed it a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the birth-flower's red edge before she knew it and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa, a death no living person can take it off. Her little tin in her bodice was touched once and unopened and her sixth narrow slate stayed turned face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door; a neighbour scrubbed in the back room and did not look up. Ch 19.

On Seed 8, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light, away from every open slate, opened none and stood a half-pace off. Above the abandoned board-bed the round pewter tin kept its pale fold at the seam and thumb-bright rub on the lid, unopened, and the fold of grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain; nobody said either was gone. Alda Reed came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and lye sour under the soap, nails scrubbed to the quick with clay under them, laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew it and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa, a woman more mornings dead than any living person keeps count of. She said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, went on with dates after the question had stopped, touched the little tin in her bodice without opening it and turned her sixth narrow slate face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name, six pages unread, no seventh written and T023 not paid again. A neighbour scrubbed in the back room and did not look up. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door. Ch 20.

**Relations:** In Low Kiln, east of the slip pits of the Clay Yard; the Vail kiln-house is three doors west, down the yard.
 On Seed 14, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at midday, away from every open slate, opened none and turned no errand into a walk to Slate Row. Alda laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew it and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa; she said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, and touched the little tin inside her bodice without opening it. Her sixth narrow slate stayed face-down toward a floorboard, six pages unread and no seventh written (T023 not paid again); the round pewter tin above the empty bed kept its pale fold and thumb-bright lid shut and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone lay. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room and were carried to no door. Ch 21.
 On Seed 17, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at midday, away from every open slate, opened none and turned no errand into a walk to Slate Row. Alda laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew it and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa; she said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, and went on with dates after the question had stopped. Her little tin in her bodice was touched once and unopened; her sixth narrow slate stayed face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name, six pages unread, no seventh written and T023 not paid again. Above the abandoned board-bed the pewter tin kept its pale fold and thumb-bright lid shut and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone lay. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room and were carried to no door; no living person could take the date off the sign. Ch 22.

 On Seed 23, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at midday, at the edge of the light and away from every place a slate might lie, opened none and did not turn the errand into a walk to Slate Row; a neighbour scrubbed at a board with lye water in the back room and did not look up at the writing, the tin or the fold pressed too long, while a low song came up through the floor flattened out of sense by work. Alda laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew it and covered the sign with *Stone seven. The account went up on the eighth, before the paper went out*, the date she gave Dessa, now standing over a woman more mornings dead than any living person keeps count of and uncollected. She said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, and went on with dates after the question had stopped; her hand went once to the little tin in her bodice and came away without opening it, her sixth narrow slate stayed face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name, six pages unread, no seventh written and **T023 not paid again**. Above the abandoned board-bed the round pewter tin kept its pale fold at the seam and thumb-bright rub unopened, and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain; nobody said either was gone. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door. Ch 24.
 On Seed 25, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at midday, at the edge of the light and away from every place a slate might lie, opened none and did not turn the errand into a walk to Slate Row; a neighbour scrubbed at a board with lye water in the back room and did not look up at the writing, the tin or the fold pressed too long, while a low song came up through the floor flattened out of sense by work. Alda laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew she had done it and covered the sign with *Stone seven. Above the abandoned board-bed the round pewter tin kept its pale fold and thumb-bright rub unopened and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain; nobody said either was gone. Ch 25.

 On Seed 29, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at midday, at the edge of the light and away from every place a slate might lie, opened no slate and turned no errand into a walk to Slate Row; a neighbour scrubbed at a board with lye water in the back room and did not look up at the writing, the tin or the fold pressed too long, and a low song came up through the floor flattened out of sense by work. Alda came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and lye sour under the soap, laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew it and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa, a woman more mornings dead than any living person keeps count of and uncollected. She said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, and went on with dates after the question had stopped; her hand went once into her bodice to the little tin and came away without opening it, her sixth narrow slate stayed face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name, six pages in a second hand all unread, no seventh written and T023 not paid again. Above the abandoned board-bed the round pewter tin kept its pale fold at the seam and thumb-bright rub on the lid unopened, and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain; nobody said either was gone. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door. Ch 26.

 On Reed 9, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light and away from every open slate, stood a half-pace off and opened no slate and turned no errand into a walk to Slate Row. Above the abandoned board-bed the round pewter tin kept its pale fold at the seam and its thumb-bright rub, unopened, and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had once lain; nobody said either was gone. Alda came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and lye sour under the soap, laid her birth-slates before the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew it and covered the sign with the exact date she gave Dessa, saying *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, over a woman more mornings dead than any living person keeps count of. Her hand went once to the little tin inside her bodice and came away without opening it; her sixth narrow slate stayed face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name, six pages unread, no seventh written and T023 not paid again. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door. Ch 29.

On Reed 12, Y.H. 813, midday, Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light, away from every open slate, opened none and stood a half-pace off. Alda Reed came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and lye sour under the soap, laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool a breath too long, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower without knowing it and covered the sign with the date she gave Dessa — *Stone seven. The account went up on the eighth, before the paper went out* — over a woman more mornings dead than any living person keeps count of. She said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, went on with the cooper's seventh in Mud End and a potter's third child in Stonemouth, touched the little tin in her bodice without opening it and left six pages turned face-down toward a floorboard with no seventh. The round pewter tin above the abandoned board-bed kept its pale fold and thumb-bright rub shut, and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had lain; nobody said either was gone. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door (T007/T008/T023/T089, T023 not paid again). Ch 30.

On Day 146, Reed 27, Y.H. 813 Sella set the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door at the edge of the light, away from every open slate, and stood a half-pace off while Alda Reed came short with soap-and-milk gone cold under river silt and lye sour under the soap. Alda laid her birth-slates writing-down on the bench before she turned to the fold, pressed the wool flat at his shoulder a breath longer than a fold requires, crossed two fingers at the red edge of the birth-flower before she knew she had done it and covered the sign with *Stone seven. The account went up on the eighth, before the paper went out*, the date she gave Dessa, now standing over a woman more mornings dead than any living person keeps count of and uncollected. She said *Warm and breathing, that's all I've set down*, exact and not true, and went on with dates after the question had stopped, because dates are a handle on a thing too large to carry; her hand went once into her bodice to the little tin and came away without opening it. Her sixth narrow slate stayed face-down toward a floorboard in a house the boy cannot name, six pages in a second hand all unread, no seventh written and T023 not paid again. Above the abandoned board-bed the round pewter tin kept its pale fold at the seam and its thumb-bright rub unopened, and the grey wool below held only the hollow where the small pale river stone had lain; nobody said either was gone. Two private records in two materials stood apart in one room, read by nobody and carried to no door (T007/T008/T023/T089). Ch 35.

### The birth room
**Kind:** room, birth chamber. **Region:** the front room of House Silt, Low Kiln, Sill.
The front room of House Silt, where Corin is born on Stone 7, Y.H. 812, and where he is set again in later months with the basket just inside the finger's-width-open door. It holds the bench by the door with a pot on it and the birth-slate propped writing-down, the hearth with a slip pot, a stool by the wall, the shelf with a chipped cup, and the board-bed of grey wool above which the pewter tin hangs. The grey ridge of Ash runs along its skirting from the bench under the door into the next room. It shares its bench, pot, draught and gap with the Vail kiln-house, the Drawing house and the Court's room, which the boy keeps in four separate places rather than call one cause.
**Relations:** Inside House Silt, three doors down the Clay Yard from the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/house-silt`.

### The second room of House Silt
**Kind:** work room, back room. **Region:** House Silt, Low Kiln, Sill.
The inner room of House Silt, entered from the birth room by a door near the middle of the dividing wall. A neighbour scrubs at a board with lye water here while a low song comes up through the floor, flattened out of its words by work; the boy has never been entered into it. The grey Ash ridge comes under the dividing door and goes on into this room. It is a different room from the back room off the Vail wheel-shed.
**Relations:** Inside House Silt, behind the birth room. Drawn close on `atlas/house-silt`.

### The pewter tin
**Kind:** tin, holding. **Region:** above the abandoned board-bed, House Silt, Low Kiln.
The round pewter tin above the abandoned board-bed in House Silt, holding a scrap of cloth from the birth, with a pale fold showing at the seam of the lid and a thumb's bright rub on it. The boy saw it from the height at which shelves are highest and entered it nowhere; nobody opened it or said the woman's name or the number in her thumb. It stands on a place of its own, not laid across the two gaps of the Vail loaf shelf, the empty place in the fold or the hand that did not come out of the strip.
**Relations:** Above the board-bed in the birth room of House Silt, three doors down the Clay Yard from the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/house-silt`.

### The Drawing house
**Kind:** dwelling, death-house. **Region:** Low Kiln, Sill; two doors down the Clay Yard from the Vail kiln-house.
An unnamed neighbour's clay-daubed house, the same room as a birth room and the Vail kiln-house with its bench by the door, its pot on the bench, its draught under the door and the grey of the Ash in a corner. Sella Vail carried the boy here before the kiln's first breath on Stone 12, Y.H. 812, and set the basket at the edge of the light where he could see the collection and not be part of it, never where a slate lay open. A dying neighbour on a board-bed against the far wall gave under the collector's cold iron tube, left with a pale round place on his arm where the iron had been; a rekindled child on a folded quilt at the other side of the hearth grew worse before the day was out. The Ash ridge along this room's skirting board is thicker by a finger's width than the ridge at Vail and turns the corner by the door without a break. The boy counted the room's nails four, four and a knot the width of a thumb, the same gap as under the Vail roof. On Stone 29, Y.H. 812 Sella walked two doors down and laid her thumb in this ridge after measuring the Vail one; this ridge was a finger's width thicker, and neither was swept.
**Relations:** Two doors down the Clay Yard from the Vail kiln-house; House Silt is three doors down in the other direction.

*The close ground of the Drawing house drawn on `atlas/the-drawing-house`: the bench by the door with its pot, the draught going along the floor, the nails four, four and a knot, the thicker ridge turning the corner, the pallet and the quilt. Direction is from the door in the south wall.*

### The drawing pallet
**Kind:** board-bed, pallet. **Region:** the Drawing house, Low Kiln, Sill.
The board-bed against the far wall of the Drawing house on which the dying neighbour lay on Stone 12, Y.H. 812, when the collector set the cold iron tube to the way; he was left with a pale round place on his arm where the iron had been. The chart draws the pallet, the fold of its covering and the pale ring; neither the house nor the man bears a name on it.
**Relations:** Inside the Drawing house, two doors down the Clay Yard from the Vail kiln-house. Drawn close on `atlas/the-drawing-house`.

### The folded quilt
**Kind:** quilt, child's bed. **Region:** the Drawing house, Low Kiln, Sill.
The folded quilt at the other side of the hearth in the Drawing house where a rekindled child was laid on Stone 12, Y.H. 812; the child grew worse before the day was out. The chart draws the pot set between the child and the door and a warmed stone at the belly, as the hearth-maids set them, and enters no figure and no cause.
**Relations:** Inside the Drawing house, beside the hearth. Drawn close on `atlas/the-drawing-house`.

### The thicker ridge
**Kind:** Ash ridge, mark. **Region:** the Drawing house, Low Kiln, Sill.
The grey Ash ridge along the skirting board of the Drawing house, thicker by a finger's width than the ridge under the Vail roof and turning the corner by the door without a break. On Stone 29, Y.H. 812 Sella Vail came two doors down the yard and laid her thumb in it after measuring the Vail ridge; neither was swept. It stands on a place of its own, not the clean dark line at Vail or the finer grey hairline called the thaw beside it.
**Relations:** Inside the Drawing house, turning the corner by the door. Drawn close on `atlas/the-drawing-house`.

### House Reed
**Kind:** house, holding. **Region:** Low Kiln, Sill.
The reed-cutters' house of Alda Reed, the hearth-maid, with a tin of second notes hidden under a floorboard. The chart draws the house and the lifted board with its six pages turned face-down, but not the little tin she keeps on a cord inside her bodice.
**Relations:** In Low Kiln, east of the Clay Yard, near the birth-flower shrine; the loose board is inside it.

*The close ground of the hearth-maid's house, with the niche a few paces east drawn at the same scale, is on `atlas/house-reed`: the room with its bench, hearth and warmed stone at the belly, the loose board and its six pages, and the shrine with the mark cut above. Direction is from the finger's-width-open door in the south wall.*

### The loose board
**Kind:** floorboard, hiding-place. **Region:** House Reed, Low Kiln, Sill.
A board in the room of House Reed that lifts at one end. Beneath it lie the second notes in a hand the Court has never seen: six narrow slates turned face-down, the first written by a hearth-maid long before Alda Reed was born, the sixth written on Day 64, Y.H. 813, and none of the six read by anybody. The chart draws the lifted board and the dark gap and leaves the pages unread; the little tin on its cord is not drawn at all.
**Relations:** Inside House Reed, in Low Kiln, east of the Clay Yard. Drawn close on `atlas/house-reed`.

### Rusk's room
**Kind:** room, dwelling. **Region:** the Fishstairs, Sill.
A room above the Fishstairs in which an old man keeps a drawer with a small lock. He never sits down indoors.
**Relations:** Above the Fishstairs, below the north bridge.

### The Court's room
**Kind:** hall, court and place of the Return. **Region:** Slate Row, Sill.
The room where the slate is read aloud on market-day and the district's measure is spoken for; in the Keeping, bank and church are one room.
