On Stone 19, Y.H. 812 a neighbour come back from it described it to the yard: a bench by the door from which the slate is read, a pot set on the bench, a draught under the pot going along the floor to the corner and felt at the ankles before the cold is felt, and a place in the figures where a line should stand and does not, the same every time; the figure is read twice, once for the moot and once for those who come late. The boy set this room beside the birth room in House Silt, the Vail kiln-house and the death-house two doors down, which share the bench, the pot, the draught and the gap, and kept the four likenesses in four separate places rather than write a cause where he had only noticed a likeness. On Weighing, Y.H. 812 this room reached the boy only as a voice through a doorway --- a bench by the door, a pot on it, a draught going along the floor to the corner, and a place in the figures where a line should stand and does not --- and he set it beside the birth room, the Vail kiln-house and the death-house as a fourth likeness, writing no cause.
**Relations:** In Slate Row, a quarter league east of the Cross; a table carrying its business in the open air stands at the Slate Row fringe of the Cross.

### The Court's table at the Cross
**Kind:** table, open-air place of business. **Region:** the Cross, Sill.
A table at the Slate Row fringe of the market where the Court's business is done in the open air before it is carried into Slate Row. The clerk who keeps it has a cuff gone soft from washing and ink in the creases of two fingers; he lays his hand on the top slate before he looks at the woman waiting, then says *it's entered as it stands. There's nothing in it the household need correct.* The upper line of that slate is in a strange neat hand, with a small stop between two clusters that the boy has seen twice before and cannot read. The table is distinct from the collector's table in Slate Row, where the measure is actually drawn. On Stone 30, Y.H. 812 the soft-cuffed clerk laid his hand on the top slate here a third time, and the small stop in the neat dark upper line came a fifth time and lay unjoined beside the other four.
**Relations:** At the Slate Row end of the Cross; Slate Row lies a quarter league east.

### The reading block
**Kind:** block, place of reading. **Region:** the Cross, Sill.
The block under the moot-post at which the slate is read aloud on market-day and the pigs are weighed on the rail. On Account-day two hundred lean over it and the figure is read twice, once for the moot and once for the households that came late; a figure read over the block is not the figure paid. Drawn close on `atlas/cross-and-fishstairs`.
**Relations:** At the Cross, beside the moot-post; the Court's table stands at the square's Slate Row fringe.

### The moot-post
**Kind:** post, notice-board. **Region:** the Cross, Sill.
The post by the block where the slate is read, with a nail in it from which the printed notice hangs --- a square of paper gone soft at one corner, with a row of marks the wind turns over and back. Sella sets the basket down beside it in the late morning, in the post's finger's width of shade, the only shade at that end of the square, and the boy lies with his cheek to its cold iron. She did so again on Stone 30, Y.H. 812, at the last market of the year, with both crusts in the fold at his side.
**Relations:** At the Cross, beside the reading block.

### The assessor's house
**Kind:** house. **Region:** Slate Row, Sill.
The narrow house of Sill's assessor, built in Y.H. 776.
**Relations:** In Slate Row, beside the Court's room.

### The collector's table
**Kind:** table, place of business. **Region:** Slate Row, Sill.
The table at which the collector draws the measure and writes a name; the Waiting Room stands behind it.
**Relations:** In Slate Row, before the Waiting Room.

### The smithy
**Kind:** works, forge. **Region:** Low Kiln, Sill.
A small forge east of the Clay Yard where kiln irons, barrow irons and the collectors' cold-iron tubes are mended. It keeps no kiln-mark and is not a great house. Harn the smith has worked here eleven years and sets his count in chisel notches on the bench where no officer has asked to read it; the letter he wrote in the last tend of Silt, Y.H. 813 to his sister at the Greybank quay is kept in a tin of iron tallies there.
**Relations:** In Low Kiln, between the Clay Yard and House Silt.

*The close ground of the forge drawn on `atlas/the-smithy`: the forge hearth and bellows, the anvil, the bench with its notches, the kiln behind the bench breathing in fours with the fifth missing, the pegs of mended tubes, the Keldish iron with its blue bloom, the ash pit and the trough; the Clay Road, the yard well, House Silt, House Reed and the birth-flower shrine drawn faint at the edges. Direction is from the anvil. Surveyed and inked in Y.H. 813, the month of Frost.*

### The smith's hearth
**Kind:** forge hearth. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The hearth of the small forge where kiln irons and the collectors' tubes are heated. The smith taps iron the way the women at the racks tap a pot, because a rule that holds for clay holds for anything worked and may fail; a cold-iron tube answers neither full nor flat.
**Relations:** Inside the smithy, east of the Clay Yard.

### The smith's bellows
**Kind:** bellows, forge tool. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The bellows at the smith's hearth. He was old enough to hold them in the year of the Rising, Y.H. 668, and not old enough to be told what the tubes he watched were for. He learned what they were for by watching what came back.
**Relations:** Beside the smith's hearth, inside the smithy.

### The smith's anvil
**Kind:** anvil, forge tool. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The anvil on its block where kiln irons, barrow irons and damper irons are re-set. The smith taps iron the way the women at the racks tap a pot, because a rule that holds for clay holds for anything worked and may fail.
**Relations:** On the floor of the smithy, east of the smith's hearth.

### The smith's tubes
**Kind:** iron tubes, instruments. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The collectors' cold-iron tubes brought to the smithy to be mended. They come in with the grip polished grey to the thumb and the grey worked into the creases of the handle; the smith draws them clean and sets the seam cold and enters nothing. He does not set a tube to a wrist, which is the officer's work. The tubes have no note of their own: they take and do not give.
**Relations:** On pegs inside the smithy; used by officers of the Keeping at the collector's table in Slate Row and at collections throughout Grath.

### The smith's bench
**Kind:** bench, keeping place. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The bench where the smith sets his count in chisel notches and has done for eleven years without an officer asking what the notches were, which is how he knows they are not a figure. His sister at Greybank struck an eleven through and put nine underneath it, because two notches had run together in one tend; the place where they ran is left as it stands. No slate in the shop would serve for a sentence.
**Relations:** Inside the smithy, under the kiln behind it.

### The kiln behind the bench
**Kind:** small kiln. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The kiln at the smith's back that takes its breath in fours and misses the fifth. He has worked beside it eleven years and never heard the fifth, and has never given the missing breath a name; the yard calls it the kiln and goes on.
**Relations:** Inside the smithy, behind the smith's bench.

### The smith's ash pit
**Kind:** ash pit. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The dark pit outside the forge where the smith's iron-gall and charcoal sweepings go. Grey works into it from every cuff that leans over the bench, and is left where it lies like the grey in a Low Kiln corner.
**Relations:** Beside the smithy door, south of the forge hearth.

### The smith's trough
**Kind:** water trough. **Region:** the smithy, Low Kiln.
The trough outside the smithy door in which hot iron is cooled. It is not the trough at the yard well, which is skinned over in Frost and stands beside a flat stone out of the path of feet.
**Relations:** Beside the smithy door, east of the ash pit.

### The meal mill
**Kind:** mill, works. **Region:** Sill, on the Ilm below the south bridge.
A small water-mill grinding the barley baked into the brown-edged loaf of Low Kiln.
**Relations:** On the south bank just below the south bridge, downriver of the Cross.

### The birth-flower shrine
**Kind:** shrine, niche. **Region:** Low Kiln, Sill.
A small hearth-maids' niche where birth-slates are left overnight and the Return is taught to the district's children. A crossed mark is cut above it, which the chart draws but does not explain. A priest of the Temple of the Return preached here on the Long Night of Y.H. 812 over the slates the women had laid to lie overnight; the women answered him with the place where the figure is not.
**Relations:** In Low Kiln, beside House Reed, a few paces east of the reed-cutter's door, east of the Clay Yard.

*The niche drawn close, with the flat stone of birth-slates, the slate-box at its foot, the candle end and the crossed mark cut above, is on `atlas/house-reed`, together with House Reed a few paces west.*

### The crossed mark
**Kind:** cut mark, carving. **Region:** the birth-flower shrine, Low Kiln, Sill.
Two strokes cut into the stone above the birth-flower shrine, older than the niche and older than the name. A priest asked what it signified gave the date Y.H. 782, the year the maids began to be paid, and gave the same date when asked again in another way. A line in a hand nobody owns reads: *The mark was here before the niche. The niche was here before the name.* The chart cuts it and does not explain it. A crossed mark of the same kind is cut into the blessing stone at the Weir.
**Relations:** Above the niche of the birth-flower shrine, east of House Reed. Drawn close on `atlas/house-reed` and at the Weir on `atlas/the-weir`.

### The shrine's slate-box
**Kind:** box, keeping. **Region:** the birth-flower shrine, Low Kiln, Sill.
The box at the foot of the niche in which the answered birth-slates are kept, among them the letter from the old hearth-maid at Cold Acre about the warming of stones and the second chill, folded thrice with a twist of grey wool through the tie-hole. The chart draws the box with two slates showing and does not open it.
**Relations:** At the foot of the birth-flower shrine, beside House Reed in Low Kiln. Drawn close on `atlas/house-reed`.

### The yard well
**Kind:** well. **Region:** Low Kiln, Sill.
The shared well of the low houses, with a stone lip and a little roof, a short walk from the drying racks. It is not the never-freezing well of Cold Acre. On Ilmen 2, Y.H. 813 a woman letting down the bucket said the water tasted different, and a man come up from the Ilm said the ice was honeyed under the mud; the yard called the change a warm spell and then the weather, and nobody said the word for the thaw. The boy kept the shape of the unnamed thing because keeping a shape was the only work available to him.
On Ilmen 18, Y.H. 813 frost-bloom stood up in white fronds on the stone lip where the Warm had lately gone out of the stone; a woman letting the bucket down said the ice would go soft before its time and that the water had been tasting of the Warm all week, and a woman at the tubs said it would hold because the nights were still cold. The argument ended in weather, and by noon the sun had taken the writing off the lip and left the stone bare. A number passed from fist to mouth to shoulder at the well that morning and was given to nobody.
On Ilmen 19 and again on Ilmen 25, Y.H. 813 frost-bloom wrote itself back onto the lip in white fronds where the Warm had lately gone out of the stone; the woman who had said the ice would hold said nothing and the man who had called it honeyed kept his counsel, so that the argument ended with nobody left to carry it. On both mornings the rope went over the writing without touching it and the yard called it weather; a man come back from looking set a number in his fist on the lip, looked at it, and put the fist in his sleeve, and the number for the thaw went from fist to mouth to shoulder and arrived nowhere. On Ilmen 26, Y.H. 813, Ch 13, a man come downriver from Greybank with road-mud on his boots told the bucket-round that in the last war a counting room was shut with the damper closed and the men inside died of the breath, eighteen leagues east; the women at the tubs spoke of the skin on the water to the bucket and not to the boy, and the yard took the stranger's story as a curiosity with a date. Frost-bloom came and went on the stone through the small hours and was gone by noon; the boy filed the saying on a tray of its own beside the told bell, the heard bell and the kiln's gap and wrote no cause across it (T065, T085).

On Silt 8, Y.H. 813 the stone lip was wet and mud had got into the rope to the last strand, coming up under the fingernails of every woman who drew and going back down with the bucket; the women spoke of the skin on the water to the bucket and not to the boy, one saying it would hold a poured spoon and another that it would not, and a man come back from looking said to the boards that the ice was honeyed all through and yellow in the middle. Ch 14.
On Silt 12–15, Y.H. 813 the rope had mud ground into it to the last strand and the stone lip was wet where it had been dry two days ago; women spoke of the skin on the water to the bucket and not to the boy, one saying it would hold a poured spoon and another that it would not, and both went on drawing. A man come back from looking at the Singing Sill said the note had come early again and the water ran fast under the skin, and was answered *Weather*. Ch 15.

On Silt 25, Y.H. 813 a second man come downriver from Greybank, road-mud on his boots and a bag, waited his turn at the well and told the bucket-round that in the last war a counting room was shut with the damper closed and the men inside died of the breath, eighteen leagues east, a curiosity with a date. A woman spoke of the war, a man said a room shut up was a room nobody had counted and somebody said the frost would take the pits early; the argument went off into the thaw and none joined the sealed room to any kiln in Low Kiln. Sella told the fire it was the thaw. The boy filed the second telling on the same tray as the first (Ilmen 26) without letting the two touch, keeping the likeness and the difference --- breath through the nose against breath in the throat, both saying *not the war's hand* --- and wrote no cause across either. Ch 17.

On Seed 2, Y.H. 813 a neighbour with wet sleeves asked what the planting would cost now that seed was in the ground; Sella moved her lips along the loaf shelf in the kiln-house doorway, glanced at the clay jar on the wall, left the space at the end of the loaf row with no figure and told her to ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there and that *the house has said it*. The bucket was given its instruction before the women were, and the boy was carried at the edge of the well with the cold iron of the handle two inches beyond his hand. On Seed 3 the early-and-late thaw argument went to the boards and the well and to nobody in particular, and Tarn agreed with both. Ch 19.

On Seed 5, Y.H. 813 a woman with wet sleeves asked what the planting would cost now that seed was in the ground; Sella moved her lips along the loaf shelf in the kiln-house doorway, glanced at the clay jar on the wall, left the space at the end of the loaf row with no figure and told her to ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there, because *the house has said it*. The bucket was given its instruction before the women were. On Seed 9 below the sill the woman who wrapped a child's dark foot in wool called it the cold and a neighbour at the tubs called the water weather, so the two facts went past each other and never met. Ch 20.

**Relations:** In Low Kiln, east of the Clay Yard.
 On Seed 13, Y.H. 813 Berr Clay came up the clay road from the Cross with the red wool cord of his office folded under his coat and the green of tasted glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice on his apron before touching nothing, read one in twelve, called the levy and took himself out of his own sentence with *There'll be a slate at the watch*, going off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true deduction inside. A neighbour at the bucket-round only mentioned a room shut in the war with the men inside dead of the breath — a mention, not a telling — and no third telling came, while the yard argued the thaw early against late and Tarn agreed with both but wanted the late because it puts off the paying. Ch 21.

 On Seed 29, Y.H. 813 a neighbour come up from the strips asked what the planting cost now that the hands were out of it; Sella glanced once at the clay jar cold under its glaze and sent her to the pot with *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*, and the neighbour carried the answer off unanswered. Ch 26.

 On Seed 22, Y.H. 813 a woman with wet sleeves stood aside to let another draw and asked what the planting had cost now that hands had been in the strips, first of the ground, then of the bucket, then of Sella; Sella moved her lips along the loaf shelf in the next room, glanced at the clay jar cold under its glaze and sent her to the pot with *Ask the pot what a wet thing costs set down there. The house has said it*, giving the bucket and the broom their instruction before anybody and none to the basket. Berr Clay came up short off the north road with the red wool cord folded under his coat and the green of tasted glaze at one corner of his mouth, wiped his thumb twice on his apron before touching nothing, held a narrow slate unopened against his forearm, read *one in twelve, as the notice says* and said a number goes into a slate and not into a strip, that there would be a slate at the watch — *your house*, never *I*. A man come up from the strips brought a number in his fist toward the well, looked at it, thought better of giving it, put the fist in his sleeve and went off, so the figure arrived nowhere; the boy kept the fist's shape on a place of its own. The early-thaw and late-thaw arguers put their case to the boards and the well; Tarn said *Yes, house* to each, agreed so entirely that no argument could find a foot and kept wanting the late because it puts off the paying, though the paying had stood in the ruts since the water went. Ch 24.

On Tallow 1–3, Y.H. 813 the trough by this well had lost the thin skin of ice that had lain on it like a skim of fat set aside in a dish; the water stood bare and dark under the little roof and a woman letting down a bucket called it weather, so the bucket went down into weather and came up with weather in it. Sella Vail carried the boy past at the half-pace from every open slate and went around the trough's empty place without a word. Ch 37.

On Frost 13–15, Y.H. 813 the stayed frost-bloom still lay white on the north lip where the yard had left it, past the going of the light and into the second working week; the rope stiffened in the hand with a small dry rasp, the water in the trough kept its skin and the plate of ice lifted off the trough still stood on the flat stone by the well out of the path of feet, clouded where the silt had risen into it. The yard called all of it weather, went around it with a look and a wide step and no number, and gave nobody a figure, a sign, a cause, a mending or a payer (T083/T085 held, not paid). On the afternoon of Frost 14 \person[Ilva]{Ilva} drew here with the pail against her knee, clay pale in her nail crescents and rack dust in one thumb seam, and answered the question put with her strip unread; another woman waiting her turn with a bucket, speaking to the boards and the well-roof and not into the basket and not to any person, said that the runner who carries \person[Berr Clay]'s slate once carried a figure that named a person she knew — no owner of the voice standing anywhere to be asked, no room, no hand at a slate, no name, no figure, no confession and no cause. The boy kept the saying on a place of its own beside \person[Hessa Rale]'s eighth hand with a finger between, not a witness, not a joining and not paid (T080 advanced, not paid). No second shaping came on Frost 15. Ch 77.

  On Frost 25–27, Y.H. 813 the stayed white on the north lip, the skin on the water in the trough and the plate of ice clouded with silt on its flat stone out of the path of feet were still called weather and entered nowhere; the rope gave its dry note and the yard went around the seven places with a look and no number (T083/T085 held, T009/T013/T092 carried). On the afternoon of Frost 26 \person[Ilva]{Ilva} drew here with the pail against her knee while an open slate lay on the stone lip with the writing turned toward its owner and nobody reading it aloud; her hand went toward her sleeve and stayed there, the notched strip unread and not joined to the slate, and she answered *Ask the pot what it cost. I keep the count, not the account*. The boy filed the order of her reaching as the ninth record-first hand with no tenth and kept the slate's nearness as an absence; on Frost 25 and 27 she was offstage and no figure came to her well (T080 advanced, not paid). Ch 81.

  On Bone 23, Y.H. 813 (Day 322) \person[Ilva]{Ilva} drew here with the pail against her knee in the stiff dry cold, clay dried pale in the crescents of her nails and the finer grey rack dust worked into the seam of one thumb she did not scrub out. A woman with a pot asked what the firings of Bone had taken; Ilva's hand went toward her sleeve and stayed there with the notched strip of birch unread and no figure coming off the wood, her eyes going to the asker and her mouth opening after both with a half-beat, and she answered the question put and not the one behind it with *Ask the pot what it cost. I keep the count, not the account*. The boy filed the order of reaching as the ninth record-first hand with no tenth and did not join her strip to Sella's handful at the rack end or to the charcoal under the loaf shelf (T080 advanced, not paid). The stayed frost gone off the north lip, the skin on the trough and the silt-clouded plate on its flat stone out of the path of feet stayed weather entered nowhere; on Bone 22 and 24 she was offstage and no figure came to her well. Ch 90.

### The barley fields
**Kind:** fields. **Region:** Sill.
Strips of barley on the warmer ground east of Low Kiln and on the terrace above; the second firing follows the harvest in Smoke. On Seed 1, Y.H. 813, the first day of the planting month, a man in the Clay Yard said the strips east of town would want hands by the morrow and a woman said the weather would have the last word, and nobody put a figure to the hands or the strips; the boy set them on a place of their own, a direction and not a number. The first work of Seed was sweeping, not planting, and the strips had not yet been seen at the close of Chapter 18. On Seed 2–4, Y.H. 813 the boy was carried out onto these strips and the first planting work went into them: brooms went around what the sweep had left, seed came out of a fist in handfuls that nobody counted aloud, and a woman pushed the bristles around a stone and looked at the ground a moment longer than the stone required. A man with a barrow said the strips would want hands before the week was out and a woman at the drying racks said the weather would have the last word, and neither put a number to either thing; the direction carried for a month had become worked ground and still had no figure. Berr Clay came down from the north road to look at them, wiped his thumb twice and said a number goes into a slate and not into a strip. See also `plan/maps-wanted.md`.
Through Seed 5–11, Y.H. 813 the fields stood in the ground as worked ground that asked to be counted and nobody counted it: brooms went around the stone the flood had left in a strip, a fist let seed out into the brown skin and came back with chaff in its creases, and the boy was carried at the edge and read the emptied fist as a chart closed with a neat line and a signature --- exact and worth nothing. A man with a barrow was in the yard again on Seed 5 and neither hands, strips nor seed had a figure spoken over them. The planting had become waiting and still had no number; the boy wanted his own number said by somebody who would know what it counted and got none, reaching one hundred days on Seed 11 with nobody marking it. Ch 20.

 Through Seed 21–23, Y.H. 813 these strips stood in their last tend with the seed up and the hands still going in, counted in glances and handfuls and never in a figure; a woman shut her fist rather than say how many hands the work would want and a man with a barrow shaped the number of backs along the row and thought better of giving it, so the figure went from the strip to his teeth and back and arrived nowhere. The low mutter of the early and late thaw argument went over the strips and neither side won, because the house had said the seed would go in when it went in. The boy was carried at the edge of the work, one hundred and ten through one hundred and twelve days old, and the hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the direction of the seed and came down. Ch 24.
 Through Seed 24–26, Y.H. 813 these strips stood in their last tend with the seed up and nobody giving a figure for the hands that had set it; the hands came out at dusk and went home and came back, and the yard counted them in glances and handfuls and the space where a man's arm should have been. A woman with seed in her fist shut the fist rather than say what it held, and a man with a barrow shaped a number with his mouth and thought better of it, so the last tend held with no figure at its foot. The boy was carried at the edge of the work on one hundred and thirteen through one hundred and fifteen days, and his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the direction of the seed and came down. Ch 25.

 On Seed 27, Y.H. 813 the last hands came out of these worked strips east of Low Kiln with the seed up and worked around, counted in glances and handfuls and never in a figure: a woman lifted two fingers with chaff in the creases and shut them, a man with a barrow shaped the number of stooped backs with his mouth and thought better of it. One hand that had gone in on Seed 15 did not come out with the rest, and the yard went around the empty place in the returning line with a look and no number --- no name, no cause, no accident, no body, no figure and no payment --- and the boy filed it on a place of its own, exact, complete and worth nothing, held for the later day a body is found or a figure is given for what the month cost (T098, planted and not paid). Sella carried him at the very edge of the work with the basket held level, gave the instruction to the wool and the river wind and never to the basket, and kept a half-pace from every open slate; his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the strips and came down. The strips stood with one place gone around through Seed 28, 29 and 30, and on Account-day the figure Berr read aloud over that same ground did not know the hand that had not come back. Ch 26.

**Relations:** East of Low Kiln toward the river road, with a further strip north of High Kiln.
 Through Seed 12–14, Y.H. 813 the seed stood in the worked strips east of Low Kiln and the work waited on hands counted in glances and handfuls and never in a figure; the new finer grey lay unswept beside the clean dark line in the Vail corner and was called the thaw. Ch 21.
 On Seed 15, Y.H. 813 the hands the strips had been said to want went into them at last: stooped backs along the turned brown ground, seed out of fists in handfuls nobody counted, brooms around the stone the flood left, the boot-print and the break into the new finer grey, and no figure spoken anywhere. A woman at the head of the first strip let seed out of her fist and would not say what it held, chaff working into the creases as she closed the empty fist on nothing and went to the next hole; a man with the barrow shaped the number of hands, looked at the strip and thought better of it, so that the figure went from the strip to his teeth and back again and was given to nobody. Berr Clay came down off the north road at midday with the cord folded under his coat, wiped his thumb twice before touching nothing, read *one in twelve, as the notice says*, said a number goes into a slate and not into a strip and that the levy was called and not by him, and went off toward High Kiln with the box locked and the true subtraction inside. The boy, one hundred and four days old, was carried at the edge with the direction he had carried for a month set beside the number the yard would not give, the two things still on two places; his hand outside the wool went up an inch toward the worked strip and came down. Ch 22.
 Through Reed 4–6, Y.H. 813 the planting strip east of Low Kiln stood worked and done, and the reed work went into the beds beyond it as the seed had gone into these strips: armfuls in the ruts, a going-downriver and a coming-back at dusk, no figure anywhere in the line. One place in the returning row was still gone around with a look and a wide step --- the hand that went in on Seed 15 had not come out with the rest on Seed 27 --- with no name, cause, body, figure or payment, and the boy laid no likeness across it, the shelf's two gaps or the fold's one crust and its empty place. Ch 28.

 Through Reed 10–12, Y.H. 813 the worked strips east of Low Kiln stood done behind the reed work, and the empty place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come back was still gone around with a look and a wide step and no number; on Reed 10 a man come up from the strips stood at the edge of the Account-day reading and the yard went around it without a figure, a name, a body or a payer (T098 carried, not paid). Ch 30.

*The close ground of the fields drawn on `atlas/the-worked-strips`: the five long rutted plots east of Low Kiln with their furrows and a flood stone, the way down to the strip landing, the clay-heavy Slip Brook, the reedbeds standing grey in the ruts, the river road above and the Ilm along the head of the chart; Low Kiln and the drying racks drawn faint at the west edge. Direction is from the head of the first strip. The empty place in the returning line is set down in the cartographer's margin and marked nowhere on the plots, for a mark would be a figure and no figure stands.*

### The strip landing
**Kind:** landing, timber stage. **Region:** the worked strips, Low Kiln, Sill.
A small timber landing on the south bank of the Ilm at the head of the way down from the worked strips, shown with a moored punt. No slate stands here and no figure is taken; it is ground a cartographer puts in for neighbourhood, not a market. Drawn on `atlas/the-worked-strips`.
**Relations:** At the north edge of the worked strips, east of Low Kiln; the river road runs above it, the Weir lies one league upriver and Mud End three leagues downriver.

### The turnip grounds
**Kind:** fields. **Region:** Sill, west of Low Kiln.
Small plots of turnips and chard worked by the households of the slip pits.
**Relations:** West of the Clay Yard, toward the river.

### The steep street
**Kind:** street, path. **Region:** Sill.
The steep way from the High Kiln terrace down to the north bank and the north bridge; the potters' children run it barefoot in Ember.
**Relations:** From High Kiln down to the Cross and the north bridge.

### The north gate
**Kind:** gate. **Region:** Sill.
The gate on the north road out of Sill; the Bone Orchard lies a league beyond it and Cold Acre another league past that.
**Relations:** North of the Cross, at the head of the road to the Bone Orchard.

### The reedbeds
**Kind:** marsh, reeds. **Region:** Sill, south and east.
The wet ground at the edge of the settlement where reeds are cut and wolves come in the hunting month of Bone.
**Relations:** South of Low Kiln, toward the Old Kiln and the marsh road to Reedholt.

 Through Reed 1–3, Y.H. 813 the reed work was a going-downriver judged by the arm that carried it, and the first bundles came into Low Kiln in the ruts where the barley had stood. On Reed 3 the boy was carried to the Weir at the edge of the work where no slate lay, going around the clean dark line, the new finer grey and the space beneath the lowest rack. A reed-cutter came along the plank walk with a bundle on his shoulder and a short length of grey wool or reed-fibre cord looped inside his coat, knots set at arm's length and one tied low with teeth and thumb; he turned it once for the boy, showed it to nobody, counted it where nobody could hear, and told a weir woman who could not read a cord *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*. The tally was carried and not counted, a third private record in the district, not joined to Mira's second twist, Orra's knot-string, Alda's six pages or the pewter tin above the abandoned bed (T099 planted). Mira worked the eels at the lower boards with slime dried in grey strokes on her sleeves and her head held right where the top of her left ear had been, answered *Ask the river*, rested her thumb on the cord longer than the figure required and did not look into the basket; her board lay beside Orra's and did not touch it. Orra stood at the lower boards with her board on its side, pulled one knot down with her thumb, gave a date where a figure was asked and got no answer. A man who walked the traps at dusk went along the boards with his collar up and was not named or spoken to: only the man who walks the Weir at dusk, still Rusk to the reader and not to the boy. Ch 27.
 Through Reed 4–6, Y.H. 813 the reed beds went on behind the yard in long grey ranks and the traps stood in the gaps of the weir with their mouths upriver, salt dried white where the flood had touched the posts. A girl with a creel went along the plank walk ahead of the others, her eyes on the boards; the boy watched them as a man watches a floor he has not yet trusted and had no hand to put on one. Ch 28.

Through Reed 10–12, Y.H. 813 the reed work went on in armfuls judged by the arm that carried them and by nothing set on a slate; a day's cutting was a going-downriver and a coming-back at dusk, and the cost asked in the form of a direction was answered with the pot, the weather or the river (T071/T072, T077). The reeds stood grey in the ruts beyond the drying racks while the yard went around the space under the lowest rack, the broken birth-slates and the place where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and a wide step and no number (T009/T013/T092, T098 carried). Ch 30.

On Reed 22 and Reed 24, Y.H. 813 the reeds stood grey in the ruts in the last week of the month, cut in a going-downriver and a coming-back at dusk and judged by the arm that carried them; the yard went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the rack space with its broken birth-slates and the place in the returning line where a hand went in on Seed 15 and did not come out, with a look and a wide step and no number (T009/T013/T092, T098 carried, not paid). Ch 34.

On Reed 25–27, Y.H. 813 the last reed bundles stood grey in the ruts at the going of the light and went downriver and back in armfuls judged by the arm that carried them; the yard went around the clean dark line, the new finer grey called the thaw, the rack space with its broken birth-slates and the empty place in the returning line with a look and a wide step and no number, entering none (T009/T013/T092, T098 carried). Ch 35.

On Tallow 13, Y.H. 813 the reed work was finished and judged by the arm that carried it, a cost asked as a direction answered with the river and the weather and leaving no entry; Orra pulled one knot down at the lower boards and gave the ninth of Ilmen, the boat of six oars and the man at the stern where a figure was asked, her thumb's return left undecided; Mira answered a thin run with *Ask the river* and showed the second twist twice beneath a shifting creel, her board beside Orra's and not touching; the reed-cutter tied one knot low with his teeth on the blessing stone and hid his cord inside his coat; the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set for an uncalled measure and a girl with a creel took the out-of-line landing sideways without a fall; below the shade a child's foot stayed dark in three turns of wool. Ch 39.

### The Weir
On Reed 18, Y.H. 813 the reed-cutter straightened his grey cord on the flat blessing stone, tied one low with his teeth and put it back inside his coat unread; Orra Weir pulled one knot down and gave the ninth of Ilmen with the boat of six oars and the man at the stern who never touched them, keeping the word forty unspoken; Mira Silt worked the eels below with her board beside Orra's and not touching it. Three private records stood within a dozen paces of the basket and none could be entered. Ch 32.

**Kind:** fishing weir and plank walk. **Region:** Grath, on the Ilm.
Eel traps and a plank walk one league upriver, where the Fish Blessing is held and Rusk walks at dusk. Orra Weir keeps the traps and dries the eels here.
**Relations:** 1 league upriver (west) of Sill; Stonemouth is a further four leagues upriver; the Eel House stands on the plank walk.

 On Reed 3, Y.H. 813 the eel traps stood in the gaps of the weir with their mouths upriver, and salt had dried white on the posts where the flood had touched them and gone away. The reed-cutter set his bundle down on their side on the flat stone where the hearth-maids had stood to bless the traps in Ilmen and turned his tally cord there; the boy watched from the edge of the shade where no slate lay. Ch 27.
 On Reed 6, Y.H. 813 the reed-cutter came along the planks again with a bundle on his shoulder, set it on the flat blessing stone, straightened the grey cord on his thumb, turned it once so the knots stood in the small light, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb and put it back inside his coat. To the weir woman who asked how many had gone up yesterday he said *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*; no figure came out of it and no knot was counted. Orra stood at the lower boards with her board on its side, pulled one knot down with her thumb and gave the ninth of Ilmen and a collector's boat with six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them where a levy figure was asked; the shape of her last knot was legible without its name and the word forty stayed unspoken. Mira worked the eels at the outer boards with slime dried in long grey strokes, answered a thin run with her own answer, touched her grey cord when a buyer named a figure and showed the second twist beneath it with neither knot counted, and did not look into the basket; her board lay beside Orra's and did not touch it. Ch 28.

 On Reed 9, Y.H. 813 the reed-cutter came along the planks with a bundle, set it on the flat blessing stone, straightened the grey cord on his thumb, turned it once so the knots stood in the small light, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat; to the weir woman who asked how many had gone up yesterday and said *I can't read a cord* he said *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*, and no figure came up. Orra stood at the lower boards with her board on its side, pulled one knot down with her thumb and gave the ninth of Ilmen, a collector's boat with six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them, where a levy figure was asked; *It's a date. The river takes what the Court doesn't. You can shout at either one. I've tried*. Mira worked the eels at the outer boards with pale slime dried in grey strokes, answered a thin run with *Thin? Who's to say thin? It comes when it comes. Ask the river*, moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie, showed the second cord twisted twice beneath the grey when a buyer in a coat with the river in its hem named a figure, and never looked into the basket; her board lay beside Orra's knot-string and did not touch it. Three private records stood on the boards in wool and teeth and thumb, and the Court's neat slate stood ready a league downriver for a measure nobody called. Ch 29.

On Reed 12, Y.H. 813 the reeds stood grey in the beds beyond the yard while the reed-cutter came along the boards with a bundle, set it on the flat blessing stone, straightened the grey cord on his thumb, turned it once so the knots stood, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat. To the woman who asked what the cord held he said *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*; no figure was read, no knot counted and no joining made to the hand that did not come back or to any other keeping (T099 carried). Orra Weir stood at the lower boards with her board on its side and a cord at her waist, pulled one knot down with her thumb and gave the ninth of Ilmen, a collector's boat with six oars and a man at the stern who never touched them where a levy figure was asked — *It's a date. The river takes what the Court doesn't. You can shout at either one. I've tried* — the forty-first double turn legible and unnamed and the word forty unspoken. Mira Silt worked the eels with slime dried grey on her sleeves and silt pale in the hollow of her neck, answered a thin run with her own answer, moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie, put her thumb under her apron to the grey cord when a buyer named a figure and showed the second twist twisted twice beneath it with neither knot counted and the thin child's knot untouched; her board lay beside Orra's and did not touch it (T024/T059/T025). Three private records in wool and teeth and thumb stood on the boards, all visible and none entered. Ch 30.

On Reed 21, Y.H. 813 the reed-cutter came along the boards with a bundle, set it on the flat blessing stone, straightened the grey cord oiled by a working thumb, turned it once so the knots stood, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat; to the woman who said *I can't read a cord* he answered *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*. Orra Weir stood at the lower boards with her board on its side, pulled one knot down with her thumb and gave the ninth of Ilmen, the collector's boat round the bend on the flood with six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them, keeping the forty-first double turn legible with the word forty unspoken. Mira Silt worked the eels below with pale slime dried in grey strokes on her sleeves and silt pale in the hollow of her neck, answered a thin run with her own answer, moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie, and when a buyer named a figure put her thumb under her apron to the grey cord and showed the second twist twice beneath it with neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's and not touching it. Three private records stood within a dozen paces of the basket and none could be entered. Ch 33.

On Reed 24, Y.H. 813 a reed-cutter came along the plank walk with a bundle, set it on the flat blessing stone, straightened the grey cord oiled by a working thumb, turned it once so the knots stood, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat with the flat of his hand; to the woman who said *I can't read a cord* he answered *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*, without anger or smile, and went up the boards with his knots unread (T099 carried, not paid). Orra Weir stood at the lower boards with her board on its side and her cord at her waist, pulled one knot down with her thumb and gave a date where a figure was asked — the ninth of Ilmen, the collector's boat round the bend on the flood with six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them — keeping the forty-first double turn legible with its name unspoken (T024). Mira Silt worked the eels with pale slime dried in long grey strokes on her sleeves, answered a thin run with *Ask the river*, and when a buyer named a figure put her thumb under her apron to the grey cord with a second twist twice beneath it, neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's and not touching (T059/T025). Three private records stood within a dozen paces of the basket and none could be entered. Ch 34.

On Reed 26 and Reed 27, Y.H. 813 the reed-cutter came along the planks with a bundle, set it on the flat blessing stone, straightened the grey cord oiled by a working thumb, turned it once so the knots stood, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat with the flat of his hand; to the woman who could not read it he said *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*, without anger or smile, its knots unread and not joined to any other keeping (T099 carried). Orra Weir stood at the lower boards with her board on its side and a cord at her waist, pulled one knot down with her thumb and set it among the others with a small flat knock, gave a date where a figure was asked — the ninth of Ilmen, the collector's boat round the bend on the flood with six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them — and kept the forty-first double turn legible with its name unspoken (T024). Mira Silt worked the eels below with pale slime dried in long grey strokes, answered a thin run with her own answer, moved eel-bones while deciding whether to lie and put one away, and when a buyer named a figure put her thumb under her apron to the grey cord with the second twist twice beneath it, neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's and not touching (T059/T025). Three private records stood within a dozen paces of the basket and none could be entered. Ch 35.

On Reed 29, Y.H. 813 the reed-cutter came along the boards with the last bundles and the short grey cord gone grey with the oil of a working thumb; he set a bundle on the flat blessing stone, straightened the cord, turned it once so the knots stood, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat with the flat of his hand, answering the woman who could not read it with *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*, without anger or smile and with no figure, knot, name or joining (T099 carried). Orra Weir stood at the lower boards with her board on its side, hands like old rope, pale eel scars uncovered, salt white at her wrists, the grey streak first through the black hair and the Fen's nasal half-song in her voice where she was tired; where a figure was asked she gave the ninth of Ilmen, the collector's boat round the bend on the flood with six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them, and, told that was not the levy, *It's a date. The river takes what the Court doesn't. You can shout at either one. I've tried.* The forty-first double turn stayed legible with the word forty unspoken and no knot counted (T024), and her thumb went to the cord again at the end, which the boy could not read as lie or habit and made no cause of. Mira Silt worked the eels with pale slime dried in long grey strokes on her sleeves and silt pale in the hollow of her neck, her head held a little right where the top of her left ear had been, answered a thin run with *Ask the river*, and when a buyer named a figure showed the second cord twisted twice beneath the grey with neither knot counted and the thin child's knot untouched, her board beside Orra's and not touching (T059/T025). Three private records stood within a dozen paces of the basket and none could be entered. Ch 36.

On Tallow 2, Y.H. 813 the last of the reed work came downriver as a direction rather than a bundle, counted in the arm that carried it, and Sella set the basket at the edge of the work where no slate lay with instruction to the wool and the running water and never to the basket. The reed-cutter straightened his grey cord on \place[the blessing stone]{the flat blessing stone}, tied one low with his teeth, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat, answering *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court* with no knot counted (T099 carried). At the lower boards Orra Weir pulled one knot down and, where a figure was asked, gave the ninth of Ilmen with the collector's boat of six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them, keeping the forty-first double turn legible and the word forty unspoken (T024). Below \place{the Singing Sill}{the sill} Mira Silt answered a thin run with *Ask the river* and showed the second twist twice beneath the grey with neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's and not touching it (T059/T025). A child's foot below the shade stayed dark in three turns of wool, the cold to one woman and weather to another (T093/T094/T096 carried), and the soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at \place{the out-of-line landing} below \place{the north bridge} for a measure nobody called (T090/T080 carried). Five private records stood within a dozen paces of the basket and none could be entered; no foundling came into the shade, no loaf passed, no slate was seen and no sale was made. Ch 37.

On Tallow 18, Y.H. 813 (Day 167) the reed work was finished and had become a direction counted in the carrying arm, a cost asked as a direction answered with the river and the weather and leaving no entry. The reed-cutter straightened his grey cord on the flat blessing stone, turned it once so the knots stood, tied one low with his teeth and hid it inside his coat, answering *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*, with no knot counted and no joining to the missing hand, Mira's cords or Orra's knot-string (T099 carried). At the lower boards Orra Weir pulled one knot down with a small flat knock — the knock of a thing laid where it belongs, kept apart from the small flat knock of a vessel cracked all the way through — and where a figure was asked gave the ninth of Ilmen, the collector's boat round the bend on the flood with six oars and one man at the stern who never touched them; to *That isn't the levy* she said *It's a date. The river takes what the Court doesn't. You can shout at either one. I've tried*, and her thumb went to the cord again at the end, which the boy did not read as lie or habit (T024/T059/T025). Mira Silt worked the eels below the shade with slime dried grey on her sleeves and silt pale in her neck, answered a thin run with her own answer and a buyer's figure with *Two marks the pair and two short. You've said it twice and I've counted it twice. There's nothing in the sentence that wants an answer*, her thumb under her apron to the grey cord and the second twist twice beneath the shifting creel with neither knot counted, her board beside Orra's and not touching it (T059/T025). A child's foot below the fish-trade shade stayed dark in three turns of wool, the cold to the woman who wound it and weather to the neighbour with a pail, the surgeon's reading of the extremity going before the core and the afterdrop waiting past a warming nobody would perform working perfectly and buying nothing at two paces, with no silt-leach, cause or cure named (T093/T094/T096 carried). The soft-cuffed clerk stayed set at the out-of-line landing below the north bridge for a measure nobody called (T090/T080 carried). Four private records stood within a dozen paces of the basket and none could be entered. Ch 40.

### The Eel House
**Kind:** hut and drying rack. **Region:** the Weir, Grath.
Orra Weir's trap-hut on the plank walk, where eels are salted and the cords of knot-accounts are kept. The Fish Blessing is prepared here on Ilmen 15 (Day 44, Y.H. 813); the older Reed 15 gloss was a planting marker and has been reconciled to the day-count in `bible/calendar.md`.
**Relations:** On the Weir, 1 league upriver of Sill.

### The plank walk
**Kind:** walkway. **Region:** the Weir, Grath.
The raised boards that follow the north bank of the Ilm out to the traps and over the water on posts; the only dry way to the eels when the river rises. Rusk walks it at dusk. The Weir chart draws the boards and their posts but not which of them will bear a man's weight in Silt.
One board in the walk will not bear a man's weight in Silt; the boy filed it as a gap on Ilmen 14 without knowing what it would do. On Ilmen 15 the boards were swept before light and the salt that was swept went over the edge into the Ilm; the hearth-maids stood on the flat stone and blessed the traps, and the boy was set on the boards where a pot would be set, out of the draught. On Ilmen 18, Y.H. 813 a weir woman with salt white at her wrists brought word up the river road that one board in the walk would not bear a man when the river came up, without saying which board; the boy set it beside the missing Fishstairs step and the hairline stopping short of the edge in three places, kept it heard-of and not trodden, and did not make an accident of it. On Ilmen 25, Y.H. 813 it was said plainly, by a weir woman with salt white at her wrists, that the weak board was **still there** and would not carry a man and that nobody had trodden it; the boy set the plain saying beside the missing Fishstairs step and the hairline stopping short of the edge and let none of them touch.

On Silt 7, Y.H. 813 a man come up from the Weir with salt white at his wrists and mud dried the colour of old rope on his boots said to the yard boards that the weak board was still there, would not carry a man when the river came up and had been trodden by nobody; the boy set it beside the missing Fishstairs step and the stopping hairlines and the new grey, three gaps in three places, and made no accident of it. Ch 14.
On Silt 9, Y.H. 813 a man come up from the Weir with salt dried white at his wrists and mud the colour of old rope on his boots brought word that the weak board was either trodden or gone under and that nobody had been down to see which; the yard let it lie. On Silt 15 it was reported gone, or trodden flat by the flood and left under the silt, by nobody seen and by nobody made into an accident. The boy set the gone board beside the missing Fishstairs step and the stopping hairlines, three gaps in three places entered in no account. Ch 15.

On Silt 24, Y.H. 813 word came up that the weak board in the plank walk was gone or had been trodden flat by the flood and left under the silt, and that nobody had been down to see which; no accident was made of it. The honeyed ice, the hairlines that had stopped short of the edge and the early Singing Sill note were spoken of only past tense as the thaw that was, and the out-of-line landing at the Fishstairs was walked over by a whole market without a fall. Ch 17.

On Seed 4, Y.H. 813 word came up again in the Fishstairs market that the weak board was gone or trodden flat and nobody had gone down to see which; the boy set the gone board beside the out-of-line landing and the stopping hairlines in three places entered in no account, and made no accident of any of them. Ch 19.

On Seed 8, Y.H. 813 word came up again in the Fishstairs market that the weak board was gone or trodden flat and nobody had gone down to see which; the boy set the gone board beside the out-of-line landing and the stopping hairlines in three places entered in no account and made no accident of any of them. Ch 20.

On Reed 21, Y.H. 813 the reed-cutter came along this walk with a bundle and the short grey cord inside his coat, set the bundle on the flat blessing stone and turned the cord so the knots stood; he tied one low with his teeth, bit the end off and went to lift a trap with the weir woman's question going down into the water, the knots unread and no figure given (T099 carried). Ch 33.

**Relations:** Along the north bank at the Weir, 1 league upriver of Sill; the Eel House and the eel racks stand beside it, and a spur turns down onto the weir head.

### The eel traps
**Kind:** fishing traps. **Region:** the Weir, Grath.
Conical baskets set in the gaps of the weir with their mouths upriver, so an eel goes in and cannot find the way out. Orra Weir lifts and salts them; the hearth-maids bless them at the Fish Blessing. A trap-keeper's saying about them is cut in the margin of the Weir chart.
**Relations:** In the Ilm at the Weir, 1 league upriver of Sill; the plank walk serves them.

### The eel racks
**Kind:** drying racks. **Region:** the Weir, Grath.
The poles beside the Eel House where the caught eels are hung to salt. These are not the drying racks of the Vail Clay Yard in Low Kiln, though both are entered in the Atlas with the same careful line.
**Relations:** Beside the Eel House on the plank walk at the Weir, 1 league upriver of Sill.

### The blessing stone
**Kind:** stone, shrine. **Region:** the Weir, Grath.
A flat stone on the north bank where the hearth-maids stand to bless the weir and the traps at the Fish Blessing. A crossed mark is cut into it, which the chart draws but does not explain.
On Ilmen 15, Y.H. 813 the stone was brushed free of grit so the cut mark showed, the hearth-maids stood on it with their sleeves damp and salt in the creases of their hands, and one sprinkled salt along the weir head with a flat hand while another said the old words in a low half-song. The boy could not see the mark from where he lay.

**Relations:** On the north bank at the Weir, beside the plank walk, 1 league upriver of Sill.

On Reed 22 and Reed 24, Y.H. 813 a reed-cutter's short grey cord, gone grey with the oil of a working thumb, went past along the boards straightened and turned once where no slate lay, its knots unread (T099 carried). On Reed 24 he set his bundle on this flat stone, straightened the cord, turned it so the knots stood, tied one low with his teeth and a pull of the thumb, bit the end off and put it back inside his coat, drawing it closed with the flat of his hand; to the woman who could not read it he said *You can ask the cord* and *No more can the Court*. No figure, knot, name or joining came of it. Ch 34.

### The river road
**Kind:** road. **Region:** Grath, along the Ilm.
The bank way by which Sill reaches the Weir and Stonemouth; it meets the Clay Road at the Cross in Sill. Packed clay, rutted in Silt and passable on foot when the low road is under water.
**Relations:** Runs along the north bank past the Weir, 1 league upriver of Sill, and on to Stonemouth, 4 leagues further up.

### Cold Acre
**Kind:** farm hamlet. **Region:** Grath, north ridge.
Six houses, barley, turnips and one well that never freezes. The barrow of grey Ash goes past on the ridge road to the north gate on every market-day and the children stand and let it go by; there is no Last Day on a ridge and nothing gets swept.
**Relations:** 2 leagues north of Sill on the ridge; 1 league beyond the Bone Orchard; the Tallow Works about 2 leagues east.

*The close ground of the hamlet drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`: the six houses and byre within their drift-stone wall, the well with its stone lip, clay dish, well-rope and eleven links to the water with the twelfth hanging dry, the well-shrine, the bare ring where frost will not hold and the untaken turn stands, the barley strips and turnip ground, the north gate road south to Sill and the Bone Orchard, and the Tallow Works east. Direction is from the well.*

### The well that never freezes
**Kind:** well, wonder, shrine. **Region:** Cold Acre, Grath.
The well of the six houses which has given water through every one of the ninety-four days of ice and never frozen. It is not warm, which is a thing the priest come up from Greybank called a little sister of the Nine Wells and a mercy of the Hearth drawn through the ridge; the well has given no sign that it heard him. Eleven links of its chain go down to the water and the twelfth hangs dry in the hand of the drawer and has never wetted. Three assessors have measured its lip with a cord and set a figure in a column that has never held anything; the well is not sold, and keeps itself and those who come to it. No slate has stood at the lip. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** At the centre of Cold Acre, within the bare ring; the well-shrine and the stone lip stand beside it.

### The well-shrine
**Kind:** shrine, marker. **Region:** the well that never freezes, Cold Acre.
The crossed-stone marker set beside the well, where the well-man spoke first to the rope, then to the stone lip, the dish, the ridge and the sky above the ridge, and last of all to the six households. Nils of Cold Acre kept the rope and the shrine for two and thirty winters. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** Beside the well that never freezes at Cold Acre.

### The stone lip
**Kind:** well-curb, stone. **Region:** the well that never freezes, Cold Acre.
The stone curb of the Cold Acre well, worn smooth by two and thirty winters of hands; the clay dish is set upon it and not in the water. The assessor measures the lip with a cord; the children's stone is set upon the curb when a turn is left standing. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** The rim of the well that never freezes, inside the bare ring.

### The clay dish
**Kind:** vessel, dish. **Region:** the well that never freezes, Cold Acre.
The dish set on the stone lip to be drawn into, never set in the water; a dish set in the water is a thing left to be fetched. A woman's dish once stood from the first voice of the sermon to the last and was drunk from standing. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** Upon the stone lip of the Cold Acre well.

### The well-rope
**Kind:** rope, chain. **Region:** the well that never freezes, Cold Acre.
The rope and chain of the Cold Acre well, kept and wound by the rope-woman, with eleven links going to the water and the twelfth hanging dry in the hand; it has lowered eleven links every morning for more than thirty winters and never once lowered twelve. The leaf of the children's well-game is kept under the loose stone of the curb in the nick where the rope is coiled. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** Upon the crossbeam of the well that never freezes, inside the bare ring.

### The twelfth link
**Kind:** link, chain, keeping. **Region:** the well-rope, Cold Acre.
The twelfth link of the Cold Acre well-chain, which hangs in the drawer's hand and never wets. A man of the College said it is kept back for the Hearth; one part in twelve is printed on the notice and is not a lie, and a printed thing travels where a chain does not. The two stand in the air between them and neither goes into the other. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** The dry link of the well-rope at the well that never freezes.

### The bare ring
**Kind:** ground, children's place. **Region:** Cold Acre, Grath.
The bare ring of ground that goes around the Cold Acre well where the frost will not hold and the ground stays dark and a little soft to the boot. Here the children pass the stone behind their backs through eight turnings and leave the untaken turn standing with a shut fist on the curb; a child is brought back only by another taking the stone and breathing on it twice. They do not play in the Five or on a market-day while the Ash barrow passes. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** Around the well that never freezes at Cold Acre.

### The untaken turn
**Kind:** place in a children's game, ground. **Region:** the bare ring, Cold Acre.
The turn that stands when the stone stops passing at the eighth line of the Cold Acre well-game: the child shuts her fist on the stone, sets the fist on the curb and does not open it. She is brought back only when another child takes the stone from her hand, breathes on it twice and puts it back. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** On the stone curb of the well that never freezes, inside the bare ring.

### The place where the thirteenth would stand
**Kind:** empty place, margin of a song. **Region:** the well that never freezes, Cold Acre.
The empty place in the Cold Acre drawing-song, between *six for the houses, six for the dead* and the coming of the bell. Nobody answered what it was for; an assessor's clerk put a round nought against it later and left it where he set it. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** Kept in the drawing-song of the well that never freezes, marked on the chart by a red nought beside the curb.

### The barley strips and turnip ground of Cold Acre
**Kind:** worked ground. **Region:** Cold Acre, Grath.
The furrowed barley strips and the short turnip rows of the six houses, standing grey with frost in their edges in Frost while the ground around the well stays bare. Kess of Cold Acre keeps the barley measure and the seed account for the six houses. Drawn on `atlas/cold-acre`.
**Relations:** East of the well and the six houses of Cold Acre.

### The Tallow Works
**Kind:** works. **Region:** Grath, north-east of Sill.
A rendering house for sheep fat whose smell carries into Sill on a north wind, where tallow is poured into lamps and the fourth cup goes under the bench. The lamp-women know the town by its smoke and not by their names; the Thane's watchman burns the wasps' nests at the shed door and enters no value arising.
**Relations:** 2 leagues north-east of Sill; Cold Acre about two leagues to the west by the ridge road.

*The close ground of the works drawn on `atlas/the-tallow-works`: the rendering house and its hot flue, the thumb-wide warm crack where the wall meets the flue, the vats, the lamp-room, the byre, the nest-fire at the shed door, the sheep-folds, the sluice run, the ridge road west to Cold Acre and Sill, and the sweet smoke going down on a north wind. Direction is from the vats.*

### The rendering house
**Kind:** works building, fat-house. **Region:** the Tallow Works, Grath.
The clay-daubed house where sheep fat is rendered in the vats; the lamp-women call it the fat-house. Its north wall holds the heat after the fire is banked and the flue runs hot from the second hour.
**Relations:** At the centre of the Tallow Works, two leagues north-east of Sill.

### The rendering vats
**Kind:** vats, works. **Region:** the rendering house, the Tallow Works.
The pots into which skim, skull and trotter go and come out light; the fat is poured from them while it is still running, and the lamp-woman keeps her tally on the back of a spoiled fat-tally gone soft at the fold.
**Relations:** Inside the rendering house at the Tallow Works.

### The hot flue
**Kind:** flue, vent. **Region:** the rendering house, the Tallow Works.
The vent that runs hot from the second hour and draws the sweet smoke over the ridge and into Sill on a north wind; a spark out of it has singed a watcher's leaf, and the tallow-wasp nests in the seam where it meets the north wall.
**Relations:** On the north wall of the rendering house at the Tallow Works.

### The warm crack
**Kind:** seam, nesting place. **Region:** the hot flue, the rendering house.
A thumb-wide seam running the length of the flue where wall meets vent, behind which the tallow-wasp hangs its grey paper nest; the fat is only where the warmth has been. A second crack in the cold north wall past the flue has never held a nest and is warm on the stillest morning of Ember, with no fire behind it that the watchman can find; he means to try it with a bare crock next Tallow. Set down in `codex/the-nest-in-the-warm-crack`, not marked on any wall.
**Relations:** Along the north wall of the rendering house at the Tallow Works.

### The lamp-room
**Kind:** room, store. **Region:** the Tallow Works.
The room where the wicks are trimmed to a fingernail and the lamps kept; the lamp-woman's poured leaf of the year-turning song lies under a cracked saucer here, sewn with a burned wick into grey wool. There is no Last Day in a lamp-room.
**Relations:** Beside the rendering house at the Tallow Works.

### The byre at the Tallow Works
**Kind:** byre, meeting room. **Region:** the Tallow Works.
The byre where the men sit at the turning of the year and the lamp-woman carries a light in after the pouring, with a bench along its wall.
**Relations:** East of the rendering house at the Tallow Works.

### The nest-fire
**Kind:** fire, disposal place. **Region:** the yard of the Tallow Works.
The fire at the shed door where the wasps' nests are burnt as the factor at Greybank ordered, four marks a nest refused and no reason given; the ash goes down with the wash-water into the sluice when the floor is sluiced.
**Relations:** At the door of the rendering house yard, the Tallow Works.

### The sluice run
**Kind:** drain, runnel. **Region:** the Tallow Works.
The run that carries the wash-water and the ash of the burnt nests away south-east out of the yard.
**Relations:** From the yard of the Tallow Works, running south-east.

### The sheep-folds
**Kind:** folds, pens. **Region:** the Tallow Works.
The rough-posted pens on the rise north of the house where the ewes are kept before shearing. A sheep has no slate and no return.
**Relations:** North of the rendering house at the Tallow Works.

### The ridge road
**Kind:** road. **Region:** Grath, north-east.
The ridge way by which the barrow of grey Ash goes out from Sill through the north gate and on past Cold Acre to the Tallow Works; east of Cold Acre it is a two-league rutted way with no Court surfacing.
**Relations:** From the north gate of Sill past the Bone Orchard and Cold Acre east to the Tallow Works.

### Mud End
**kind:** peat hamlet. **Region:** Grath, downriver.
Nine houses and peat stacks where the moot meets in a byre and the winter peat decides whether anyone stays warm.
**Relations:** 3 leagues downriver (east) of Sill; Greybank a further 15 leagues east; Reedholt lies in the marsh to the south.

*The close ground of the hamlet drawn on `atlas/mud-end`: the nine houses and peat stacks on the dry shelf above the marsh, the byre where the moot meets and its moot-post, sods set out to dry, the peat cuttings in the southern banks, a landing and punt on the Ilm, the Clay Road east to Greybank and west to Sill, and Reedholt faint in the marsh to the south. Direction is from the byre. This is the hamlet of the man taken quiet in his sleep and called merciful, told sideways in the Clay Yard (T077).*

### Reedholt
**Kind:** marsh hamlet. **Region:** Grath, east marsh.
Reed-cutters' huts on the edge of the marsh where wolves come in the reedbeds. Meg Sedge buys eels on the marsh road and weighs peat at the dry here; Lull Mud, a mud-wife, has caught children on the boardwalks for twenty-one years. A man here with two fingers gone clicks four for himself and a fifth he will not explain.
**Relations:** About 4 leagues east of Sill, south of the river road; Mud End about 2 leagues north-east.

*The close ground of the hamlet drawn on `atlas/reedholt`: the reed-cutters' huts on posts, the mud-wife's hut, the peat-dry and its lifted tally board, the peat stacks, the boardwalk with a plank gone, the peat-hut, the Reed Cut and the cut bundles, and the landing and punt on the Ilm. The river road runs above the marsh toward Sill and Greybank; the marsh road comes down from Mud End, passes the hamlet and goes on toward the Quiet Acre, drawn faint at the edges for neighbourhood only. The black peat and the bog-cow are set down in the surveyor's margin and drawn nowhere, for a figure would make a thing stand that does not stand. Direction is from the peat-dry. Surveyed and inked in Y.H. 813, the month of Frost.*

### The east marsh
**Kind:** marsh, wetland. **Region:** Grath, east of Sill.
The wet ground beyond the reedbeds in which Reedholt stands and the peat is cut; dry places in it are a loan from the water, called in when the water pleases. Fen Cant is not written here, and what must be kept is cut in a stick or tied in a knot.
**Relations:** East of Sill and south of the river road; holds Reedholt, the peat-dry, the Reed Cut and the black peat.

### The peat-dry
**Kind:** drying ground, works. **Region:** Reedholt, east marsh.
The dry at Reedholt where Meg Sedge weighs peat and names her figures into an apron; a notched count-stick is kept under a loose board in oiled sacking, and grey is gathered and bottled here in unmarked jars.
**Relations:** East of Reedholt; the marsh road runs past it; the peat-dries of the bog-hunter lie further east.

### The mud-wife's hut
**Kind:** hut, birth-room. **Region:** Reedholt.
The hut on posts of Lull Mud, a mud-wife who catches children on the boardwalks and lays out the drowned of the drained cuts; the reed-name is said three times into the ground under the boards and a knot of grey wool is tied for the word that cannot be written.
**Relations:** Inside Reedholt, on the hummock beside the boardwalk.

### The peat-hut
**Kind:** hut. **Region:** east marsh, east of Reedholt.
The hut of the bog-hunter who keeps the peat-dries for the reeve and takes the bog-cow for its hide and horn; a peat-fire burns under a pot at the end of a stand.
**Relations:** In the black peat east of the marsh road, east of Reedholt.

### The Reed Cut
**Kind:** cutting ground, reeds. **Region:** east marsh, Reedholt.
The ground where reed is cut; a child cutting a first bundle in the dark goes alone and brings it back dry or does not come back. The arm knows a bundle's weight and no slate its price.
**Relations:** South-west of Reedholt, in the reedbeds by the marsh road.

### The black peat
**Kind:** peat ground, bog. **Region:** east marsh.
The black peat east of the marsh road where the bog-cow stands and the ground gives under the boot and comes back warm under a grey skin. It is set down in the surveyor's margin and marked nowhere on `atlas/reedholt`, for a figure would make a thing stand that does not stand.
**Relations:** East of the marsh road, east of Reedholt; the peat-hut stands in it.

### Stonemouth
**Kind:** quarry village. **Region:** Grath, upper Ilm.
The meeting of the Ferr and the Ilm, with lime kilns and coarse sandstone workings. Its men cut lime and coarse sandstone, buy reed-wolf teeth for clamp-handles at two marks apiece, and haul Keldish iron up the Rim Road; the carter Orven and the quarryman Orrel went west past the survey post and came back with a stone they did not enter. The village keeps a name in a room where the Court keeps it on a slate.
**Relations:** 5 leagues upriver (west) of Sill; the Weir is 4 leagues downriver; Nine Pots a further 3 leagues west by the Ferr.

*The close ground of the village drawn on `atlas/stonemouth`: the sandstone workings and lime yard on the rising bank, the lime kilns and the ale-room beside them, the village houses, the weigh-clerk's bench by the landing, the river road east to Sill and west over the ford to Nine Pots, the Ferr coming down to the Ilm and the Lead Run stained from the old working. Direction is from the lime yard. The white hands of the quarrymen are the subject of a drinking song set down here in Y.H. 811.*

### The lime kilns
**Kind:** works, kilns. **Region:** Stonemouth, Grath.
The lime-burning kilns of the quarry village, their mouths white with dust. The lime dust gives the burners the white cough; the book of remedies set down in the lime yard in Y.H. 811 keeps the cures with the errors left standing, among them the fall from the bank and the lead-rat's bite. The ale-room stands beside one of them.
**Relations:** In Stonemouth, on the rising bank above the Ilm, west of the Ferr; 5 leagues upriver of Sill.

### The sandstone workings
**Kind:** quarry, works. **Region:** Stonemouth, Grath.
The coarse sandstone workings of Stonemouth, cut into the rising bank above the river; with the lime yard they are the village's trade, and blocks go out by water and by the river road. Stone from here and lime hauled by the carters are the goods that pass up the Rim Road toward Keld Keep.
**Relations:** In Stonemouth, west of the lime kilns and the Ferr; 5 leagues upriver of Sill.

### The ale-room
**Kind:** ale-room, room. **Region:** Stonemouth, Grath.
The room beside the lime kiln where the woman who keeps it takes the men's names for their ale and gives a name back on the last day of a tend when the factor's man comes. The drinking song called *The White Hands* was set down here in charcoal and lime-dust on the back of a spoiled weigh-slip in Bone of Y.H. 811. A man in a clean coat come up the road from Greybank makes the room stop at the end of the part, and nobody says why.
**Relations:** Beside the lime kilns in Stonemouth; 5 leagues upriver of Sill.

### The weigh-clerk's bench
**Kind:** bench, counting place. **Region:** Stonemouth, Grath.
The bench at Stonemouth where the weigh-clerk counts reed-wolf teeth in marks and lays his cuff on the leaf before he looks at the man who brought them. Every bench in Stonemouth has an end: a name kept in a room is a different weight from a name kept on a slate, and a woman can carry the one when the men beside her have put the other down.
**Relations:** By the landing at Stonemouth, beside the ferry stage and the Ilm; 5 leagues upriver of Sill.

### The Lead Run
**Kind:** stream. **Region:** Grath, upper Ilm basin.
A blue-grey stained stream running off an old working nobody will admit to, said to make a man thin if drunk through a season.
**Relations:** About 6 leagues west of Sill; enters the Ferr; Stonemouth a few leagues down the Ferr.

### Nine Pots
**Kind:** pottery hamlet. **Region:** Grath, west.
Four kilns and a shared clay pit, in permanent feud with Sill over the road.
**Relations:** 8 leagues west of Sill; Stonemouth about 3 leagues east; the Rim Road passes north of it.

*The close ground of the hamlet drawn on `atlas/nine-pots`: the four kilns, the shared clay pit, the potters' houses and worked strips, the feud road forded over the Ferr toward Sill, Stonemouth three leagues east at the meeting of the Ferr and the Ilm, the Lead Run and the Rim Road passing north. Direction is from the hamlet's moot-post.*

### Greybank
**Kind:** granary town. **Region:** Grath, eastern gate.
The Court's granary and factor's house, where the Clay Road ends and the Hearth Road begins; the first place a Sill man sees the Court at full size. On Ilmen 26, Y.H. 813 a man come downriver from Greybank, road-mud on his boots, told the bucket-round at Low Kiln's yard well that in the last war a counting room was shut with the damper closed and the men inside died of the breath; he said it as a curiosity with a date, eighteen leagues east, and went back down the road he came. The yard took it for history and nobody joined the sealed room to any kiln in Sill (T065). On Silt 25, Y.H. 813 a second man come downriver from Greybank with road-mud on his boots and a bag repeated the saying at the same well as a curiosity with a date; the yard took it as history again and nobody joined it to any kiln, while the boy filed the second telling beside the first without letting the two touch, keeping breath through the nose against breath in the throat and both saying *not the war's hand* (T095).
**Relations:** 18 leagues east downriver of Sill; Mud End 15 leagues west; the Hearth Road runs 140 leagues south-east to the Hearthseat.

### The Counting House
**Kind:** counting house. **Region:** Greybank, Grath.
The Vhaleni factor's counting house in Greybank where glass, grain, quiet ash and whole returns are brokered and where documents on salt-paper are left in the damp; Veno Brine keeps his private drawer of dry documents here.
**Relations:** Inside Greybank, 18 leagues east downriver of Sill, near the Court's factor's house and the end of the Clay Road.
